Introduction
Analytic Functions over non-Archimedean Fields
Classical rigid geometry may be viewed as a theory of analyti c functions
over local ﬁelds or, more generally, over ﬁelds endowed with a complete non-
Archimedean absolute value. For example, choosing a prime p, the ﬁeld Qp of
p-adic numbers is such a ﬁeld. To construct it, we start out fro m the ﬁeld Q
of rational numbers and complete it with respect to its p-adic absolute value
|·|p, which is deﬁned as follows: we set |0|p = 0, and|x|p =p−r forx∈ Q∗ with
x = pra
b , a,b,r ∈ Z, and p ∤ab. Then |·|p satisﬁes the usual properties of an
absolute value, as it satisﬁes the following properties:
|x|p = 0⇐⇒x = 0,
|xy|p =|x|p|y|p,
|x +y|p≤ max{|x|p,|y|p}.
Furthermore,|·|p extends to an absolute value on Qp with the same properties.
The third condition above is called the non-Archimedean triangle inequality, it
is a sharpening of the usual Archimedean triangle inequality|x +y|≤| x| +|y|.
This way, the ﬁeld Qp ofp-adic numbers might be viewed as an analogue of
the ﬁeld R of real numbers. There is also a p-adic analogue Cp of the ﬁeld C of
complex numbers. Its construction is more complicated than in the Archimedean
case. We ﬁrst pass from Qp to its algebraic closure Qalg
p . The theory of extensions
of valuations and absolute values shows that there is a uniqu e extension of|·|p to
this algebraic closure. However, as Qalg
p is of inﬁnite degree over Qp, we cannot
conclude that Qalg
p is complete again. In fact, it is not, and we have to pass
from Qalg
p to its completion. Fortunately, this completion remains al gebraically
closed; it is the ﬁeld Cp we are looking for.
After the p-adic numbers had been discovered by Hensel in 1893, there we re
several attempts to develop a theory of analytic functions o verp-adic ﬁelds. At
the beginning, people were just curious about the question i f there would exist
a reasonable analogue of classical function theory over C. However, later when
algebraic geometry had progressed so that applications to n umber theory were
possible, a good theory of analytic functions, say over Cp, became sort of a
necessity. To explain this, let us look at a typical object of arithmetic algebraic
geometry like a scheme X of ﬁnite type over Q or Z. Extending coeﬃcients, we
=====PAGE 4=====
2 Introduction
can derive from it an R-schemeXR as well as a Qp-schemeXQp for each prime p.
There is a local-global principle, which was already envisi oned by Hensel when
discovering p-adic numbers. It says that, in many cases, problems over Q can
be attacked by solving them over R as well as over each ﬁeld Qp. Some evidence
for this principle is given by the formula
∏
c∈P ∪{∞}
|x|c = 1 for x∈ Q∗,
where P stands for the set of all primes and |·|∞ is the usual Archimedean
absolute value on Q. So, in the case we are looking at, we have to consider the
schemesXR andXQp for each p. Sometimes it is desirable to leave the algebraic
context and to apply analytical methods. For example, exten ding coeﬃcients
from R to C, we can pass from XR to XC and then apply methods of classical
complex analysis to XC. In the same way it is desirable to develop analytic
methods for handling the schemes XCp obtained from XQp by extending Qp to
Cp.
There is a nice motivating example, due to J. Tate, showing th at analytical
methods in the non-Archimedean case can give new insight, wh en dealing with
objects of algebraic geometry. Let K be an algebraically closed ﬁeld with a
complete non-Archimedean absolute value |·|, which is assumed to be non-
trivial in the sense that there are elements a∈K with|a|⁄= 0, 1; for example,
we may take K = Cp. Then, using ζ as a variable, look at the algebra
O(K ∗) ={
∑
ν∈Z
cνζν ; cν∈K, lim
|ν|→∞
|cν|rν = 0 for all r> 0}
of all Laurent series which are globally convergent on K ∗. Viewing O(K ∗) as
the ring of analytic functions on K ∗, we can construct its ﬁeld of fractions
M(K ∗) = Q(O(K ∗)) and think of it as the ﬁeld of meromorphic functions on
K ∗.
Now choose an element q∈ K ∗ with|q| < 1, and write Mq(K ∗) for all
meromorphic functions which are invariant under multiplic ation of q onK ∗; i.e,
Mq(K ∗) ={f∈M (K ∗) ; f (qζ ) = f (ζ)}.
Tate made the observation that Mq(K ∗) is an elliptic function ﬁeld with a
non-integral j-invariant, i. e., with |j| > 1. Furthermore, he saw that the set
of K-valued points of the associated elliptic curve EK coincides canonically
with the quotient K ∗/qZ. Elliptic curves which are obtained in this way have
been called Tate elliptic curves since then. As quotients of type K ∗/qZ are not
meaningful in the setting of algebraic geometry, Tate begun to develop a theory
of so-called rigid analytic spaces in which such quotients m ake sense; cf. [T].
In fact, the existence of an analytical isomorphism of type EK≃ K ∗/qZ is a
charcterizing condition for Tate elliptic curves.
The nature of Tate elliptic curves becomes more plausible if we look at
the classical complex case. Choose ω∈ C− R and consider Γ = Z⊕ Zω as a
=====PAGE 5=====
Analytic Functions over non-Archimedean Fields 3
subgroup of the additive group of C; it is called a lattice in C. The quotient C/Γ
makes sense as a Riemann surface; topologically it is a torus , like a life-belt.
Furthermore, the ﬁeld of meromorphic functions on C/Γ may be identiﬁed with
the ﬁeld of Γ -periodic meromorphic functions on C. It is well-known that the
set of isomorphism classes of Riemann surfaces of type C/Γ is in one-to-one
correspondence with the set of isomorphism classes of ellip tic curves over C. In
fact, if ℘(z) denotes the Weierstraß ℘-function associated to the lattice Γ , we
can consider the map
C−→ P2
C, z ↦−→(℘(z),℘ ′(z), 1),
from C into the projective plane over C. It factors through C/Γ and induces an
isomorphism C/Γ ∼−→EC onto an elliptic curveEC⊂ P2
C. The deﬁning equation
of EC in P2
C is given by the diﬀerential equation of the Weierstraß ℘-function.
Thus, we see that C/Γ is, in fact, an algebraic object. As the isomorphism
C/Z ∼−→ C∗ provided by the exponential function induces an isomorphis m
C/Γ ∼−→ C∗/qZ for q =e2πiω, we can also represent EC as the quotient C∗/qZ,
which is the analogue of what we have in the case of Tate ellipi tc curves.
Returning to the non-Archimedean case, one can prove that, j ust as in the
classical complex case, isomorphism classes of elliptic cu rves correspond one-to-
one to isomorphism classes of Riemann surfaces of genus 1 in t he sense of rigid
analytic spaces. However, among these precisely the ellipt ic curves with non-
integralj-invariant are Tate elliptic; all others are said to have good reduction.
Tate elliptic curves may be viewed as the correct analogues o f complex tori.
However, they can only be represented from the multiplicati ve point of view as
quotientsK ∗/qZ, since the additive point of view, as used in the complex case ,
does not work. The reason is that the exponential function, i f deﬁned at all,
does not converge well enough.
After having explained the usefulness of analytic methods i n the non-
Archimedean case, let us turn now to the methods themselves. As we will see
from the next section on, it is by no means clear how to deﬁne ge neral analytic
functions over non-Archimedean ﬁelds. There have been seve ral approaches, and
the only one which has survived, is the one chosen by Tate in hi s fundamental
paper “Rigid analytic spaces” [T]. The theory has been furth er developed by
Grauert, Remmert, Kiehl, Gerritzen, and others, and is toda y referred to as
classical rigid geometry . It will be the subject of the ﬁrst part of these lectures
and is described in detail in the monography [BGR], Parts B an d C.
As rigid geometry is much closer to algebraic geometry than t o classical
complex analysis, it is not surprising, that there is an appr oach via so-called
formal schemes which are objects from algebraic geometry. T his approach of
rigid geometry via methods of formal geometry was suggested by Grothendieck
and has been carried out by Raynaud, who explained it in the le cture [R]. For
a systematic foundation see the papers [FI], [FII], [FIII], [FIV]. Formal rigid
geometry will be dealt with in the second half of these lectur es. It has the
advantage of allowing more general base objects than classi cal rigid geometry.
Also, to handle it, one may resort to the powerful methods of a lgebraic geometry.
=====PAGE 6=====
4 Introduction
Quite recently, there have been developed some derivates of classical rigid
geometry by Berkovich, and of formal rigid geometry by Huber and Fujiwara,
in order to handle questions of ´ etale cohomolgy of rigid spa ces. However, the
frame of formal rigid geometry is ﬂexible enough to provide b asic access also to
these approaches.

1. Classical Rigid Geometry
1.1 Topology induced from a non-Archimedean absolute
value
We start by recalling the deﬁnition of a non-Archimedean abs olute value.
Deﬁnition 1. Let K be a ﬁeld . A map |·|: K −→ R≥0 is called a non-
Archimedean absolute value if for all a,b∈K the following hold :
(i)|a| = 0⇐⇒a = 0 ,
(ii)|ab| =|a||b| ,
(iii)|a +b|≤ max{|a|,|b|} .
One can immediately verify trivialities such as |1| = 1 and |− a| =|a|. To
an absolute value as above one can always associate a valuation ofK. This is a
map v : K−→ R∪{∞} satisfying the following conditions:
(i) v(a) =∞⇐⇒ a = 0
(ii) v(ab) = v(a) +v(b)
(iii) v(a +b)≥ min(v(a),v (b))
Namely, one can set v(a) = − log|a| for a∈ K. This sets up a 1-to-1 corre-
spondence between non-Archimedean absolute values and val uations, as we can
pass from valuations v back to absolute values by setting |a| =e−v(a) fora∈K.
Frequently, we will make no diﬀerence between absolute valu es and valuations,
just saying that K is a ﬁeld with a valuation. An absolute value |·|is called
trivial if it assumes only the values 0 , 1∈ R. It is called discrete if|K ∗| is dis-
crete in R>0. Likewise a valuation is called trivial if v(K ∗) ={0}, and discrete
if v(K ∗) is discrete in R. Unless stated otherwise, we will always assume that
absolute values and valuations on ﬁelds are non-trivial.
In the following, let K be a ﬁeld with a non-Archimedean absolute value
|·|. As usual, the absolute value gives rise to a distance functi on by setting
d(a,b ) =|a−b| and, thus, to a topology on K. Furthermore, we can consider
sequences or inﬁnite series of elements in K and deﬁne their convergence as
in the Archimedean case. In particular, K is called complete if every Cauchy
sequence converges in K.
It has to be pointed out, however, that the non-Archimedean t riangle in-
equality in Deﬁnition 1 (iii) has far reaching consequences .
=====PAGE 8=====
6 1. Classical Rigid Geometry
Proposition 2. Let a,b∈K satisfy|a|⁄=|b|. Then
|a +b| = max{|a|,|b|}.
Proof. Assume|b|<|a|. Then|a +b|<|a| implies
|a| =|(a +b)−b|≤ max{|a +b|,|b|}<|a|
which is impossible. So we must have |a +b| =|a| = max{|a|,|b|} as claimed.
□
Lemma 3. A series ∑ ∞
ν=0aν of elements aν∈K is a Cauchy sequence if and
only if the coeﬃcients aν form a zero sequence, i. e., if and only if limν→∞|aν| =
0.
In particular , if K is complete , the series is convergent if and only if
limν→∞|aν| = 0.
Proof. Choose ε > 0. We have to show there is an integer N such that
| ∑ j
ν=iaν| < ε for all j ≥ i≥ N. As lim ν→∞|aν| = 0, we know there is an
N∈ N such that|aν|<ε for all ν≥N. But then, for any integers j≥i≥N,
an iterated application of the non-Archimedean triangle in equality yields
|
j∑
ν=i
aν|≤ max
ν=i...j
|aν|<ε
which had to be shown. □
In terms of distances between points in K, the non-Archimedean triangle
inequality implies
d(y,z )≤ max{d(x,y ),d (x,z )} for x,y,z ∈K,
where this inequality is, in fact, an equality if d(x,y ) is diﬀerent from d(x,z );
cf. Proposition 2. In particular, given any three points in K, there exists one of
them such that the distances between it and the two remaining points coincide.
In other words, any triangle in K is isosceles. Furthermore, we can conclude
that each point of a disk in K can serve as its center. Thus, if an intersection of
two disks is non-empty, we can choose a point of their interse ction as common
center, and we see that they are concentric.
For a center a∈K and a radius r∈ R>0 we can consider the disk around
a without periphery
D−(a,r ) ={x∈K ; d(x,a )<r},
which is open as well as closed in K; it is symbolically referred to as the open
disk around a with radius r. Similarly, we can consider the same disk with
periphery, which is
=====PAGE 9=====
1.1 Topology induced from a non-Archimedean absolute value 7
D+(a,r ) ={x∈K ; d(x,a )≤r}.
It is open and closed just as well and symbolically referred t o as the closed disk
around a of radius r. In addition, there is the periphery itself, namely
∂D(a,r ) ={x∈K ; d(x,a ) = r}.
Certainly, it is closed, but it is also open. Namely, due to Pr oposition 2, we
have D−(x,r )⊂ ∂D(a,r ) for any x∈ ∂D(a,r ). It is for this reason that the
periphery ∂D(0, 1) is sometimes called the unit tire in K.
The preceding considerations show another peculiarity of t he topology of
K:
Proposition 4. The topology of K is totally disconnected , i. e., any subset in
K consisting of more than just one point is not connected .
Proof. Consider an arbitrary subset A⊂ K consisting of at least two diﬀerent
pointsx,y . For δ = 1
2d(x,y ), set A1 =D−(x,δ )∩A andA2 =A−A1. Then A1
is relatively open and closed in A, and the same is true for A2. Furthermore, A
is the disjoint union of the non-empty open parts A1 and A2. Consequently, A
cannot be connected with respect to the topology induced fro mK on A. □
We may draw some conclusions from the latter observation. Wr iting⌈⌊0, 1⌉⌋
for the unit intervall in R, there cannot exist non-constant continuous paths
⌈⌊0, 1⌉⌋−→K. Consequently, there is no obvious way to deﬁne line integra ls,
and it is excluded that there exists a straightforward repla cement for classical
complex Cauchy theory, providing the link between holomorp hic and analytic
functions. In fact, the concept of holomorphic functions, d eﬁned through diﬀer-
entiability, and that of analytic functions, deﬁned via con vergent power series
expansions, diﬀers largely. On the other hand, it should be a dmitted that in cer-
tain contexts notions of integrals and also line integrals h ave been developed.
Without making it more precise, we mention that a deﬁnition o f holomor-
phic functions via diﬀerentiability is not very rewarding. The class of such func-
tions is very big and does not have good enough properties. So the only ap-
proach towards a reasonable “function theory” over non-Arc himedean ﬁelds,
which might be left, is via analyticity, i. e., via convergen t power series expan-
sions. However, it is by no means clear how to proceed with ana lyticity. Let
us call a function f : U −→K deﬁned on some open subset U ⊂ K locally
analytic if it admits a convergent power series expansion locally aro und each
point x∈ U. Then we cannot expect any identity theorem for such functio ns,
since non-empty open subsets of K are not connected. For example, let us look
at the case, where U is a disk D−(0, 1) or D+(0, 1). Choosing a radius r with
0<r < 1, we may write
U =
⋃
a∈U
D−(a,r )
and get from it a partition of U into disjoint disks. On each of these disks, let’s
call them Di, i∈ I, we can consider an arbitrary convergent power series fi.
=====PAGE 10=====
8 1. Classical Rigid Geometry
Thenf : U−→K deﬁned by f|Di =fi is locally analytic. In particular, we can
take the fi to be constant, and it follows that, on disks, locally analyt ic func-
tions do not necessarily admit globally convergent power se ries expansions. This
shows that locally analytic functions cannot enjoy reasona ble global properties.
The basic principle of rigid analytic geometry is to require that analytic
functions on disks admit globally convergent power series e xpansions. We will
discuss the details of the precise deﬁnition in subsequent s ections.
1.2 Strictly convergent power series
As always, we consider a ﬁeld K with a complete non-Archimedean absolute
value. Let K be its algebraic closure. We will use that the absolute value of K
admits a unique extension to K and that, although K itself might not be com-
plete, this absolute value nevertheless is complete on each ﬁnite subextension
of K/K . Let
Bn(K) ={(x1,...,x n)∈Kn ;|xi|≤ 1}
be the unit ball in Kn.
Lemma 1. A formal power series
f =

ν∈Nn
cνζν =

n ∈K⌈⌊⌈⌊ζ1,...,ζ n⌉⌋⌉⌋

Proof. If f is convergent at the point (1 ,..., 1)∈ Bn(K), the series ∑
νcν is
convergent, and we must have lim|ν|→∞|cν| = 0 by 1.1/3. Conversely, considering
a point x∈ Bn(K), there is a ﬁnite and, hence, complete subextension K ′ of
K/K such that all components of x belong to K ′. Then, if lim |ν|→∞|cν| = 0, we
have lim |ν|→∞|cν||xν| = 0, and f (x) is convergent in K ′⊂K by 1.1/3, again.
□

restricted, or strictly convergent power series .
That Tn is, in fact, a K-algebra is easily checked. Also it is clear that the
canonical map from Tn to the set of maps Bn(K)−→K is a homomorphism of

In particular, it follows that Tn is an integral domain. The stated properties of

Proposition 3. Tn is complete with respect to the Gauß norm . So it is a Banach
K-algebra.
Proof. Consider a series ∑ ∞

With the help of Proposition 3 we can easily characterize uni ts in Tn.
Corollary 4. A series f∈Tn with|f| = 1 is a unit if and only if its reduction
˜f ∈ k⌈⌊ζ1,...,ζ n⌉⌋is a unit , i. e., if and only if ˜f ∈ k∗. More generally , an
arbitrary series f∈Tn is a unit if and only if |f−f (0)|<|f (0)|, i. e., if and
only if the absolute value of the constant coeﬃcient of f is strictly bigger than
the one of all other coeﬃcients of f .
Proof. It is only necessary to consider elements f∈Tn with Gauß norm 1. If f
is a unit in Tn, it is also a unit in R⟨ζ1,...,ζ n⟩. Then ˜f is a unit in k⌈⌊ζ1,...,ζ n⌉⌋
and, hence, in k∗. Conversely, if ˜f∈ k∗, the constant term f (0) of f satisﬁes
|f (0)| = 1, and we may even assume f (0) = 1. But then f is of type f = 1−g
with|g|< 1, and ∑ ∞
i=0gi is an inverse of f . □
Proposition 5 (Maximum Principle). Let f ∈ Tn. Then|f (x)|≤| f| for all
x∈ Bn(K), and there exists a point x∈ Bn(K) such that |f (x)| =|f|.
Proof. The ﬁrst assertion is trivial. To verify the second one, ass ume|f| = 1 and
consider again the canonical epimorphism π : R⟨ζ1,...,ζ n⟩−→k⌈⌊ζ1,...,ζ n⌉⌋.
Then ˜f = π(f ) is a non-trivial polynomial in n variables, and there exists a
point ˜x∈kn with k the algebraic closure of k, such that ˜f (˜x)⁄= 0. The theory
of valuations and absolute values shows that k may be interpreted as the residue
ﬁeld of K, the algebraic closure of K. Choosing a lifting x∈ Bn(K) of ˜x, we
can consider the commutative diagram
R⟨ζ1,...,ζ n⟩ −−−→k⌈⌊ζ1,...,ζ n⌉⌋↓
↓
R −−−→ k
where the ﬁrst vertical map is evaluation at x and the second one evaluation
at ˜x. As f (x)∈ R is mapped onto ˜f (˜x)∈ k, which is non-zero, we obtain
|f (x)| = 1 =|f|, which had to be shown. □
The Tate algebra Tn has many properties in common with the polynomial
ring in n variables over K, as we will see. The key tool for proving all these
properties is Weierstraß theory, which we will explain now and which is quite
analogous to Weierstraß theory in the classical complex cas e.

The reason for considering distinguished elements in Tn is that there is
Weierstraß division by such elements, which is the analogue of Euklid’s division
on polynomial rings.
Theorem 8 (Weierstraß Division). Let g ∈ Tn be ζn-distinguished of some
order s. Then, for any f∈ Tn, there are a unique series q∈ Tn and a unique
polynomial r∈Tn−1⌈⌊ζn⌉⌋of degree r <s satisfying
f =qg +r.
Furthermore,|f| = max(|q||g|,|r|).
Proof. Without loss of generality we may assume |g| = 1. First, let us consider
an equation f =qg +r of the required type. Then, clearly, |f|≤ max(|q||g|,|r|).
If, however, |f| is strictly smaller than the right-hand side, we may assume
max(|q||g|,|r|) = 1. Then we would have ˜ q˜g + ˜r = 0 with ˜q⁄= 0 or ˜r⁄= 0,
and this would contradict Euklid’s division in k⌈⌊ζ1,...,ζ n−1⌉⌋⌈⌊ζn⌉⌋. Therefore we
must have |f| = max(|q||g|,|r|), and uniqueness of the division formula is a
consequence.
To verify the existence of the division formula, we write g = ∑ ∞
ν=0gνζν
n with

ν >s. Set ε = maxν>s|gν| so that ε< 1. We want to show the following slightly
weaker assertion:
(W) For any f∈Tn, there exist q,f 1∈Tn and a polynomial r∈Tn−1⌈⌊ζn⌉⌋
of degree <s with
f =qg +r +f1,

Corollary 9 (Weierstraß Preparation Theorem). Let g ∈ Tn be an element
which is ζn-distinguished of order s. Then there exist a unique monic polynomial

Monic polynomials ω∈Tn−1⌈⌊ζn⌉⌋with|ω| = 1, as occurring in Corollary 9,
are called Weierstraß polynomials in ζn. So each ζn-distinguished element f∈
Tn is associated to a Weierstraß polynomial. Furthermore, if f is an arbitrary
non-zero element in Tn, we can assume by Lemma 7 that the indeterminates
ζ1,...,ζ n∈Tn are chosen in such a way that f isζn-distinguished of some order

Corollary 10 (Noether normalization). For any proper ideal a ⊊ Tn, there is
a K-algebra monomorphism Td−→Tn for some d∈ N such that the composi-
tion Td−→Tn−→Tn/a is a ﬁnite monomorphism . The integer d is uniquely
determined as the Krull dimension of Tn/a.
Proof. Assuming a⁄= 0, we can choose an element g⁄= 0 in a. Furthermore,
applying a suitable automorphism to Tn, we can assume by Lemma 7 that g
is ζn-distinguished of some order s≥ 0. By Weierstraß division we know that
anyf∈Tn is congruent modulo g to a polynomial r∈Tn−1⌈⌊ζn⌉⌋of degree <s .
In other words, the canonical morphism Tn−1−→Tn−→Tn/(g) is ﬁnite; in
fact using the uniqueness of Weierstraß division, Tn/(g) is a free Tn−1-module
generated by the residue classes of ζ 0
n,...,ζ s−1
n .
Now consider the composition Tn−1−→Tn/(g)−→Tn/a and write a1 for its
kernel. If a1 = 0, we are done. Else we can proceed with a1 andTn−1 in the same
way as we did with a and Tn. Then, as the composition of ﬁnite morphisms is
ﬁnite again, we will get a ﬁnite monomorphism Td−→Tn/a after ﬁnitely many
steps. That d equals the Krull dimension of Tn/a follows from commutative
algebra. □
Looking at the proof of Corollary 10, it should be pointed out that the
resulting monomorphism Td−→Tn/a does not necessarily coincide with the
canonical one sending ζi∈ Td to the residue class of ζi in Tn/a. In fact, this
canonical morphism will, in general, be neither injective n or ﬁnite, as can be
seen from simple examples.
Corollary 11. Let m⊂ Tn be a maximal ideal . Then the ﬁeld Tn/m is ﬁnite
over K.
Proof. Using Noether normailzation, there is a ﬁnite monomorphis mTd֒→Tn/m
for a suitable d∈ N. As Tn/m is a ﬁeld, the same in true for Td. So we must
haved = 0 and, hence, Td =K. □
A direct consequence of Corollary 11 is the following:

that the resulting map ϕ : Tn−→K is contractive in the sense that |ϕ(a)|≤| a|

We want to end this section by deriving some standard propert ies of Tn.
Proposition 13. Tn is noetherian ; i. e., each ideal a⊂Tn is ﬁnitely generated .
Proof. Proceeding by induction, we can assume that Tn−1 is noetherian. Now,
consider a non-trivial ideal a⊂Tn. Then we can choose a non-zero element g∈ a
which, by 2.7, we can assume to be ζn-distinguished. By Weierstraß division,
Tn/(g) is a ﬁnite Tn−1-module and, hence, a noetherian Tn−1-module, as Tn−1
is noetherian. Consequently, a/(g) is ﬁnitely generated over Tn−1 and, thus, a
is ﬁnitely generated on Tn. □
Proposition 14. Tn is factorial and , hence, normal ; i. e., integrally closed in
its ﬁeld of fractions .
Proof. Proceeding by induction, we may assume that Tn−1 is factorial and,
hence, by the Lemma of Gauß, that Tn−1⌈⌊ζn⌉⌋is factorial. Consider a non-zero
elementf∈Tn which is not a unit. Again, by Lemma 7, we may assume that f is
ζn-distinguished and, by Corollary 9, that f is, in fact, a Weierstraß polynomial.
Now consider a factorization f = ω1...ω r into prime elements ωi∈ Tn−1⌈⌊ζn⌉⌋.
Asf is a monic polynomial in ζn, we can assume the same for ω1,...,ω r. Then,
as|ωi|≥ 1, we have necessarily |ωi| = 1 for all i, since |f| = 1. So the ωi are
Weierstraß polynomials.

Proposition 15. Tn is jacobson ; i. e., for any ideal a⊂Tn its nilradical rad a
equals the intersection of all maximal ideals m∈ MaxTn containing a.
Proof. One knows from commutative algebra that the nilradical rad a of any

1.3 Ideals in Tate algebras 17
1.3 Ideals in Tate algebras
We know already from 1.2/13 that all ideals a ⊂ Tn are ﬁnitely generated.
Considering such an ideal a = (a1,...,a r), say with generators ai of absoulte
value|ai| = 1, we can ask if any f∈ a admits a representation f = ∑ r
i=1fiai
with elements fi∈ Tn satisfying|fi|≤| f|. If this is the case, we can easily
deduce that a is complete under the Gauß norm of Tn and, hence, that a is
closed in Tn. To establish these and other assertions, we will use a techn ique
involving normed vector spaces.
Deﬁnition 1. Let R be a ring . A ring norm on R is a map |·|: R−→ R≥0
satisfying
(i)|a| = 0⇐⇒a = 0,
(ii)|ab|≤| a||b|,
(iii)|a +b|≤ max(|a|,|b|),
(iv)|1|≤ 1.
The norm is called multiplicative if instead of (ii) we have
(ii′)|ab| =|a||b|.
We claim that that, instead of condition (iv), we actually ha ve|1| = 1 if R
is non-zero. In fact, we have |1|≤| 1|2 due to (ii) and, thus, |1| =|1|2, since
|1|≤ 1 by (iv). This implies |1| = 1 or |1| = 0. As 1 ⁄= 0 and, hence, |1|⁄= 0 by
(i) if R is non-zero, we can conclude that |1| = 1 in this case.
Deﬁnition 2. Let R be a ring with a multiplicative ring norm |·|such that
|a|≤ 1 for all a∈R.
(i) R is called a B-ring if
{a∈R ;|a| = 1}⊂ R∗.
(ii) R is called bald if
sup{|a| ; a∈R with|a|< 1}< 1.
We want to show the following assertion:
Proposition 3. LetK be a ﬁeld with a valuation and R its valuation ring . Then
the smallest subring R′⊂R containing a given zero sequence a0,a 1,... ∈R is
bald.
Proof. The smallest subring S⊂R equals either Z/pZ for some prime p, or Z. It
is bald, since any valuation on the ﬁnite ﬁeld Z/pZ is trivial and since the ideal
{a∈ Z ;|a|< 1}⊂ Z is principal. If there is an ε∈ R such that|an|≤ ε< 1 for
alln∈ N, we see for trival reasons that S⌈⌊a0,a 1,... ⌉⌋is bald. Thus, it is enough
to show that, for a bald subring S⊂R and an element a∈R of value|a| = 1,
=====PAGE 20=====
18 1. Classical Rigid Geometry
the ring S⌈⌊a⌉⌋is bald. To do this, we may localize S by all elements of value
1 and thereby assume that S is a B-ring. Then S contains a unique maximal
ideal m, and ˜S =S/m is a ﬁeld. If the reduction ˜a∈k is transcendental over ˜S,
it follows that S⌈⌊a⌉⌋is bald for trivial reasons. Namely then, for any polynomial
p = ∑ r
i=0ciζi∈ S⌈⌊ζ⌉⌋, we have |p(a)| < 1 if and only if ∑ r
i=0 ˜ci˜ai = 0; i. e., if
and only if ˜ci = 0 for all i. Thus,|p(a)|< 1 implies
|p(a)|≤ sup{|c| ; c∈S,|c|< 1}< 1,
and we are done.
It remains to consider the case, where ˜ a is algebraic over ˜S. Let g = ζn +
c1ζn−1 + ... + cn ∈ S⌈⌊ζ⌉⌋be a polynomial of minimal degree such that its
reduction ˜g annihilates ˜a or, in equivalent terms, such that |g(a)|< 1. Let ε< 1
be the supremum of |g(a)| and of all values |c| with c∈ S and|c| < 1. Now
consider a polynomial f∈ S⌈⌊ζ⌉⌋with|f (a)| < 1; we want to show |f (a)|≤ ε.
Using Euklid’s division, we get a decomposition f = qg +r with q,r ∈ S⌈⌊ζ⌉⌋
and degr <n = degg. Since|g(a)|≤ ε, we may assume f =r. If all coeﬃcients
of r have value < 1, this value must be ≤ ε and we are done. On the other
hand, if one of the coeﬃcients of r has value 1, the reduction ˜ r of r is non-
trivial. But then we have ˜ r(˜a) = 0, and this contradicts the deﬁnition of g,
since deg ˜r <deg ˜g and ˜g, annihilating ˜a, was chosen of minimal degree. Thus,
|f (a)|< 1 implies|f (a)|≤ ε, and we are done. □
Let us mention that in the situation of Proposition 3 we can al ways lo-
calize R′ by all elements of value 1 and thereby assume that R′ is a B-ring.
Furthermore, if R is complete, we may switch to the completion of R′. As the
completion of a B-ring yields a B-ring again, we may even assume that R′ is a
complete B-ring.
Next we want to look at vector spaces and norms on them. As a pro totype,
we can consider a Tate algebra Tn with its Gauß norm and forget about multi-
plication. In the following, let K be a ﬁeld with a complete non-Archimedean
valuation.
Deﬁnition 4. LetV be aK-vector space. A norm onV is a map|·|: V−→ R≥0,
such that
(i)|x| = 0⇐⇒x = 0,
(ii)|x +y|≤ max(|x|,|y|),
(iii)|cx| =|c||x| for c∈K and x∈V .
Deﬁnition 5. LetV be a complete normed K-vector space. A system (xν)ν∈N of
elements in V , where N is ﬁnite or at most countable , is called a (topological)
orthonormal basis of V if the following hold :
(i)|xν| = 1 for all ν∈N.
(ii) Each x∈V can be written as a convergent series x = ∑
ν∈Ncνxν with
coeﬃcientscν∈K.
=====PAGE 21=====
1.3 Ideals in Tate algebras 19
(iii) For each equation x = ∑
ν∈Ncνxν as in (ii) we have |x| = maxν∈N|cν|.
In particular , the coeﬃcients cν in (ii) are unique.
For example, the monomials ζν∈Tn form an orthonormal basis if we con-
sider Tn =K⟨ζ⟩as a normed K-vector space. For any normed K-vector space
V , we will use the notations

1.4 Aﬃnoid algebras
So far we have viewed the elements of Tn as functions Bn(K)−→K. If a⊂Tn
is an ideal, we can look at its zero set
V (a) ={x∈ Bn(K) ; f (x) = 0 for all f∈ a}
and restrict functions on Bn(K) to V (a). Thereby we get a homomorphism
vanishing on a from Tn to the K-algebra of all maps V (a)−→K. Thus, we
may interpret the quotient A = Tn/a as an algebra of “functions” on V (a).
However note that, as we will conclude later from the fact tha t Tn and, hence,
A are jacobson, an element f∈A induces the zero function on V (a) if and only
if f is nilpotent in A. The purpose of this section is to study algebras of type
A =Tn/a which we call aﬃnoid K-algebras.
Deﬁnition 1. A K-algebra A is called an aﬃnoid K-algebra if there is an
epimorphism α : Tn−→A for some n∈ N.
As an immediate consequence of 1.2/10, 1.2/13, 1.2/15 we get :
Proposition 2. Let A be an aﬃnoid K-algebra. Then:
(i) A is noetherian .
(ii) A is jacobson .
(iii) A satisﬁes Noether normalization ; i. e., there is a ﬁnite monomorphism
Td֒→A for some d∈ N.
Proposition 3. Let A be an aﬃnoid K-algebra and q⊂ A an ideal whose
nilradical is a maximal ideal in A. Then A/q is of ﬁnite vector space dimension
over K.
Proof. Let m = rad q. Applying Noether normalization, there is a ﬁnite
monomorphism Td֒→A/q for some d∈ N. However, we must have d = 0,
since dividing out nilpotent elements yields a ﬁnite monomo rphismTd֒→A/m.
As A/m is a ﬁeld, the same must be true for Td. □
=====PAGE 25=====
1.4 Aﬃnoid algebras 23
AﬃnoidK-algebras can easily be endowed with a topology (which is uni que,
as we will see later). Just note that for any epimorphism α : Tn−→A, the Gauß
norm|·|of Tn induces a residue norm |·|α on A given by
|α(f )|α = inf
a∈kerα
|f−a|.
We can say that|f|α for some f∈A is the inﬁmum of all values |f| withf∈Tn
varying over all inverse images of f.
Proposition 4. Let a⊂ Tn be an ideal , and consider the quotient A = Tn/a
as an aﬃnoid K-algebra via the projektion map α : Tn−→Tn/a. Then the map
|·|α : Tn/a−→ R≥0 satisﬁes the following properties :
(i)|·|α is a K-algebra norm; i. e., a ring norm and a K-vector space norm. It
induces the quotient topology of Tn on Tn/a, and α : Tn−→Tn/a is continuous
and open .
(ii) Tn/a is complete under |·|α.
(iii) For any f∈Tn/a, there is an inverse image f∈Tn such that|f|α =|f|.
In particular , for any f∈Tn/a, there is an element c∈K with|f|α =|c|.
Proof. That|·|α is aK-algebra norm is easily veriﬁed; note that |f|α = 0 implies
f = 0, since a is closed in Tn by 1.3/8. It follows more generally from 1.3/9 that
anyf∈Tn/a admits an inverse image f∈Tn with|f| =|f|α. From this we see
immediately that α maps an ε-neighborhood of 0 ∈Tn onto an ε-neighborhood
0∈Tn/a and, thus, is open. As it is continuous anyway, it induces the quotient
topology on Tn/a. Finally, as we can lift Cauchy sequences in Tn/a to Cauchy
sequences in Tn, we see that Tn/a is complete. □
Viewing the elements f of an aﬃnoid K-algebraTn/a asK-valued functions
on the zero set V (a)⊂ Bn(K), we can introduce the supremum |f|sup of all
values, which are assumed by f . The latter is ﬁnite, as can be seen from 1.2/5.
However, to be independent of a special representation of an aﬃnoidK-algebra
A as a certain quotient Tn/a, we prefer to set for elements f∈A
|f|sup = sup
x∈MaxA
|f (x)|.
Here MaxA is the spectrum of maximal ideals in A and, for any x∈ MaxA, we
writef (x) for the residue class of f inA/x. The latter is a ﬁnite ﬁeld extension
of K by 1.2/11, and the value |f (x)| is well-deﬁned, since the valuation of K
admits a unique extension to A/x. Usually|·|sup is called the supremum norm
onA. However, to be more precise, it should be pointed out that, i n the general
case,|·|sup will only be a K-algebra semi -norm, which means that it satisﬁes
the conditions of a norm, except for the condition that |f|sup = 0 implies f = 0.
We start by listing some properties of the supremum norm, whi ch are more or
less trivial.
Proposition 5. The supremum norm is power multiplicative ; i. e., we have
|fn|sup =|f|n
sup for any element f of an aﬃnoid K-algebra.
=====PAGE 26=====
24 1. Classical Rigid Geometry
Proposition 6. Let ϕ : B−→A be a morphism between aﬃnoid K-algebras.
Then|ϕ(b)|sup≤|b|sup for all b∈B.
Proof. If m is a maximal ideal in A, the quotient A/m is ﬁnite over K by 1.2/11.
Thus, writing n = ϕ−1(m) we get ﬁnite maps֒→B/n֒→A/m and we see
that n is a maximal ideal in B. As|b(n)| =|ϕ(b)(m)|, we are done. □
Proposition 7. On a Tate algebra Tn, the supremum norm |·|sup coincides
with the Gauß norm |·|.
Proof. It follows from the Maximum Principle 1.2/5 that
|f| = sup{|f (x)| ; x∈ Bn(K)}
for any f ∈ Tn. To x∈ Bn(K) we can always associate the maximal ideal
mx ={h∈Tn ; h(x) = 0} of Tn, as we have seen in 1.2/12. Then evaluation at
x yields an embedding Tn/mx֒→K, and we see that f (mx) = f (x) and, hence,
|f (mx)| =|f (x)|. Since x↦−→mx deﬁnes a surjection Bn(K)−→ MaxTn by
1.2/12, we are done. □
Proposition 8. Let A be an aﬃnoid K-algebra with a residue norm |·|α cor-
responding to some K-algebra epimorphism α : Tn−→A. Then|f|sup≤| f|α
for all f∈A. In particular,|f|sup is ﬁnite .
Proof. Consider a maximal ideal m⊂A and its inverse image n =α−1(m)⊂Tn.
Fixing an element f∈A, let g∈Tn be an inverse image satisfying |f|α =|g|.
Then
|f (m)| =|g(n)|≤| g| =|f|α
and, hence,|f|sup≤|f|α. □
Proposition 9. Let A be an aﬃnoid K-algebra. Then, for f∈A, the following
are equivalent :
(i)|f|sup = 0.
(ii) f is nilpotent .
Proof. Condition (i) is equivalent to f ∈ ⋂
m∈MaxA m. As A is jacobson by
Proposition 2, the ideal ⋂
m∈MaxA m equals the nilradical of A. Thus, (i) is
equivalent to (ii). □

We are now in a position to show that all residue norms on an aﬃn oid
K-algebra A are equivalent; i. e., they induce the same topology on A. In par-
ticular, this stresses again the fact that the notions of pow er boundedness as in
Theorem 16 and of topological nilpotency as in Corollary 17 a re independent
of the residue norm under consideration.
Lemma 18. Let A be an aﬃnoid K-algebra and consider elements fi∈ A,
i = 1,...,n .
(i) Assume there exists a K-morphism ϕ : K⟨ζ1,...,ζ n⟩−→A such that
ϕ(ζi) = fi, i = 1,...,n . Then|fi|sup≤ 1 for all i.
(ii) Conversely, if |fi|sup≤ 1 for all i, there exists a unique K-morphism
ϕ : K⟨ζ1,...,ζ n⟩−→A such that ϕ(ζi) = fi for all i. Furthermore, ϕ is con-
tinuous with respect to the Gauß norm on Tn and with repsect to any residue
norm on A.
Proof. As |ζi|sup =|ζi| = 1, assertion (i) follows from Proposition 6. To verify
(ii), ﬁx a residue norm |·|α on A and deﬁne ϕ by setting
ϕ(

∑

Due to Theorem 16, |fi|sup≤ 1 implies that the fi are power bounded with
respect to any residue norm on A. From this we see immediately that ϕ is
well-deﬁned and unique as a continuous morphism mapping ζi to fi. Thus, it
remains to prove that, apart from ϕ, there cannot exist any further K-morphism
ϕ′ : K⟨ζ1,...,ζ n⟩−→A mapping ζi to fi. Let us ﬁrst consider the case where
A, as a K-vector space, is of ﬁnite dimension over K. We show that, in this
case, any K-morphism ϕ′ : K⟨ζ1,...,ζ n⟩ −→A is continuous. As is known
for ﬁnite dimensional vector spaces over complete ﬁelds, an y K-vector space
norm on A induces the product topology in the sense that any isomorphi sm
of K-vector spaces A ∼−→Kd, where d = dimKA, is a homeomorphism. Now
viewing Tn/ kerϕ′ as an aﬃnoid K-algebra with canonical residue norm, it is
enough to show that the induced morphism Tn/ kerϕ′֒→A is continuous. How-
ever, the latter is clear, since linear forms V −→K on a ﬁnite dimensional
normed vector space V are continuous if V carries the product topology. Thus,
ϕ′ is continuous.
To deal with the general case, consider two K-morphisms ϕ,ϕ ′ : Tn−→A,
both mapping ζi tofi. Then, choosing a maximal ideal m⊂A and some integer
r >0, we know from Proposition 3 that A/mr is of ﬁnite vector space dimension
overK. Hence, by what we have seen before, the induced maps Tn−→A/mr
are continuous and, thus, coincide. Therefore it is enough t o show that any
f ∈ A satisfying f ≡ 0 mod mr for all m∈ MaxA and all r > 0 will be
trivial. To do this, apply Krull’s intersection theorem (se e for example 2.1/2) to
all localizations Am, m∈ MaxA. It states that ⋂
r∈N mrAm = 0. Therefore the
image off in any localization Am is trivial and, thus, f itself must be trivial. □
=====PAGE 33=====
1.5 Aﬃnoid spaces 31
Proposition 19. Any morphism B−→A between aﬃnoid K-algebras is con-
tinuous with respect to any residue norms on A and B. In particular, all residue
norms on an aﬃnoid K-algebra are equivalent .
Proof. Choose an epimorphism Tn−→B and consider the resulting composition
Tn−→B−→A. By Lemma 18 the latter is continuous with respect to any
residue norm on A. But then also B−→A is continuous. □
Alternatively, one can derive Proposition 19 from the Close d Graph Theo-
rem and the Open Mapping Theorem for Banach spaces (i. e., com plete normed
vector spaces); see [Bourbaki] for these results of functio nal analysis. The Open
Mapping Theorem can further be used to show that the supremum norm|·|sup
on any reduced aﬃnoidK-algebraA is equivalent to all possible residue norms.
However, also this result can be obtained in a more direct way , using (sophisti-
cated, though) techniques of aﬃnoidK-algebras. One shows that, after replacing
K by a suitable ﬁnite extension, the R-algebra{f∈A ;|f|sup≤ 1}, divided by
its nilradical, is ﬁnite over {f∈A ;|f|α≤ 1} for any residue norm |·|α on A.
Finally, let us mention that, although aﬃnoid K-algebras have been deﬁned
as quotients of Tate algebras without taking into account an y topology, their
handling nevertheless requires the use of a residue norm or t opology. Otherwise,
convergence will not be deﬁned, and we run already into troub les when we want
to give explicit constructions of simple things such as a mor phism Tn−→A
from a Tate algebra Tn into some aﬃnoid K-algebra A.
1.5 Aﬃnoid spaces
Let A be an aﬃnoid K-algebra. As we have seen, the elements of A can be
viewed as “functions” on Max A, the spectrum of maximal ideals of A. Namely,
for f ∈ A and x∈ MaxA, we deﬁne f (x) as the residue class of f in A/x.
EmbeddingA/x into an algebraic closure K ofK, the value f (x)∈K is deﬁned
up to conjugation over K, whereas the absolute value |f (x)| is well-deﬁned, as
it is independent of the chosen embedding֒→K.
In the following we will write Sp A for the set Max A together with its
K-algebra of “functions” A and call it the aﬃnoid K-space associated to A.
Frequently, we will use Sp A also in the sense of Max A and talk about the
spectrum of A. Usually, points in Sp A will be denoted by letters x,y,... , and
the corresponding maximal ideals in A by mx, my,... . One might ask, why we
restrict ourselves to maximal ideals instead of considerin g the spectrum of all
prime ideals in A, as is done in algebraic geometry. There is a simple reason
for this. In the next section, we will introduce a certain pro cess of localization
for aﬃnoid K-algebras, more precisely, of complete localization, sinc e we do
not want to leave the context of aﬃnoid K-algebras. Similarly as in algebraic
geometry, this localization process is used in order to endo w aﬃnoid K-spaces
with the structure of a ringed space. As only maximal ideals b ehave well with
=====PAGE 34=====
32 1. Classical Rigid Geometry
respect to localization in this sense, we must restrict ours elves to spectra of
maximal ideals. For example, considering such a localizati on A−→AS and a
(non-maximal) prime ideal q⊂AS, it can happen that there is no prime ideal
p⊂A satisfying q = pAS.
The Zariski topology on an aﬃnoid K-space SpA can be deﬁned as ususal.
For any ideal a⊂A we consider its zero set
V (a) ={x∈ SpA ; f (x) = 0 for all f∈ a} ={x∈ SpA ; a⊂ mx}
and call it a Zariski-closed subset of Sp A.
Lemma 1. LetA be an aﬃnoid K-algebra, and consider ideals a, b⊂A as well
as a family (ai)i∈I of ideals in A.
(i) a⊂ b =⇒V (a)⊃V (b).

i∈IV (ai).
(iii) V (ab) = V (a)∪V (b).
The proof of (i) and (ii) is straightforward. So let us look at (iii). We have
V (a)∪V (b)⊂ V (ab) by (i). To show the converse, consider a point x∈ SpA
which is neither in V (a), nor in V (b). So there are elements f∈ a and g∈ b
such that f (x)⁄= 0 and g(x)⁄= 0. Then f,g ⁄∈mx and, hence, fg⁄∈mx, since
mx is a prime ideal. So fg (x)⁄= 0, which implies x⁄∈V (ab). □
Assertions (ii) and (iii) show that there really is a topolog y on Sp A,
namely the Zariski topology, whose closed sets are just the s ets of type V (a).
Also note that, for any epimorphism α : Tn−→A, the map Sp A−→ SpTn,
m↦−→α−1(m), yields a homeomorphism with respect to Zariski topologie s be-
tween SpA and the Zariski-closed subset V (kerα)⊂ SpTn.

1.6 Aﬃnoid subdomains
The Zariski topology on an aﬃnoid K-space is quite coarse. In the present
section we want to introduce a ﬁner one which is directly indu ced from the
topology of K. Namely, we can think of an aﬃnoid K-space SpA as of a Zariski-
closed subspace of Sp Tn for some n∈ N, and the latter can be identiﬁed with
the unit ball Bn(K), at least if K is algebraically closed. Thereby we see that
the topology of the aﬃne n-space Kn gives rise to a topology on Sp A which,
as we will see, is independent of the particular embedding Sp֒→SpTn; it
will be referred to as the canonical topology of Sp A. If K is not necessarily
algebraically closed, we can proceed similarly by viewing S pTn as the quotient
of Bn(K) by the action of AutK(K), providing SpTn with the quotient topology.
To give a more rigorous approach, consider an aﬃnoid K-space X = SpA
and set
X(f ;ε) ={x∈X ;|f (x)|≤ ε}.
for f∈A and ε∈ R>0.
Deﬁnition 1. For any aﬃnoid K-spaceX = SpA, the topology which is gen-
erated by all sets of type X(f ;ε) with f∈A and ε∈ R>0 is called the canonical
topology of X.
Thus, a subset U ⊂ X is open with respect to the canonical topology if
and only if it is a union of ﬁnite intersections of sets of type X(f ;ε). Writing

on SpA, is equivalent to the fact that these functions generate the unit ideal in

Deﬁnition 9. Let X = SpA be an aﬃnoid K-space. A subset U⊂X is called
an aﬃnoid subdomain of X if there exists a morphism of aﬃnoid K-spaces
ι : X ′−→X such that ι(X ′)⊂U and the following universal property holds :
Any morphism of aﬃnoid K-spaces ϕ : Y −→ X satisfying ϕ(Y ) ⊂ U
admits a unique factorization through ι : X ′−→X via a morphism of aﬃnoid
K-spacesϕ′ : Y−→X ′.

Proposition 11. For any aﬃnoid K-space X = Sp A, Weierstraß, Laurent,
and rational domains in X are examples of open aﬃnoid subdomains . These
are called special aﬃnoid subdomains .
Proof. First, it follows from Lemma 8 that Weierstraß, Laurent, an d rational
domains are open in X. To show that they satisfy the deﬁning condition of
aﬃnoid subdomains, we start with a Weierstraß domain X(f )⊂ X, where f
stands for a tuple of functions f1,...,f r∈ A. Let A⟨ζ1,...,ζ r⟩be the aﬃnoid
K-algebra of strictly convergent power series in the variabl es ζ1,...,ζ r overA,
the topology of A being provided by some residue norm. Setting
A⟨f⟩=A⟨f1,...,f r⟩=A⟨ζ1,...,ζ r⟩/(ζi−fi ; i = 1,...,r ),
we obtain an aﬃnoid K-algebra. There is a canonical morphism of aﬃnoid
K-algebras ι∗ : A −→ A⟨f⟩and, associated to it, a morphism of aﬃnoid
K-spaces ι : Sp A⟨f⟩−→X. It has to be shown that ι has image in X(f ) and
that all other morphisms of aﬃnoid K-spaces ϕ : Y −→X with im ϕ⊂ X(f )
admit a unique factorization through ι.
To do this consider a morphism of aﬃnoid K-spacesϕ : Y−→X and let it
correspond to a morphism of aﬃnoid K-algebras ϕ∗ : A−→B. Then, for any
y∈Y , we get
|ϕ∗(fi)(y)| =|fi(ϕ(y))|, i = 1,...,r,
by looking at the inclusion A/mϕ(y)֒→B/my of ﬁnite extensions of K, which
is induced from ϕ∗. Therefore, ϕ(Y )⊂X(f ) is equivalent to |ϕ∗(fi)|sup≤ 1 for
all i. Since ι∗(fi) equals the residue class of ζi in A⟨f⟩, we have |fi|sup≤ 1 by
1.4/8. Thus, it follows that ι has image in X(f ), and it remains to show the
following universal property for ι∗:
Each morphism of aﬃnoid K-algebrasϕ∗ : A−→B with|ϕ∗(fi)|sup≤ 1 for
all i admits a unique factorization through ι∗ : A−→A⟨f⟩.
However, this is easy to do. Given such a morphism ϕ∗ : A−→B, we can
extend it to a morphism A⟨ζ⟩−→B by mapping ζi to ϕ∗(fi) for all i. Then
the elements ζi−fi belong to the kernel, and we get an induced morphism
A⟨f⟩−→B which is a factorization of ϕ∗ : A−→B through ι∗ : A−→A⟨f⟩.
That this factorization is unique follows from the fact that the image of A is
dense in A⟨f⟩.

Proposition 12 (Transitivity of aﬃnoid subdomains). Let X be an aﬃnoid
K-space, V an aﬃnoid subdomain in X, and U an aﬃnoid subdomain in V .
Then U is an aﬃnoid subdomain in X, too.
Proof. Consider a morphism of aﬃnoid K-spaces ϕ : Y −→X having image in
U. Then, as U⊂V andV is an aﬃnoid subdomain of X, there is a unique fac-
torizationϕ′ : Y−→V ofϕ through֒→X. Furthermore, ϕ′ admits a unique
factorization ϕ′′ : Y −→U through֒→V , as U is an aﬃnoid subdomain of
V . Then, of course, ϕ′′ is a factorization of ϕ through֒→֒→X which,
using the uniqueness of factorizations through֒→V and֒→X, is easily
seen to be unique. □

Proposition 14. Let X be an aﬃnoid K-space and let U,V ⊂ X be aﬃnoid
subdomains. Then U∩ V is an aﬃnoid subdomain of X. If U and V are

of X.

Corollary 15. Let X = SpA be an aﬃnoid K-space. Each Weierstraß domain
in X is Laurent, and each Laurent domain in X is rational .

Using Proposition 5, we can conclude from Proposition 17 in c onjunction
with Corollary 15 that, for any Weierstraß, Laurent, or rati onal subdomain U

Lemma 18. Let ϕ : Y−→X be a morphism of aﬃnoid K-spaces with associ-
ated morphism of aﬃnoid K-algebras ϕ∗ : A−→B, and let x∈ X be a point
corresponding to the maximal ideal m⊂A.
(i) Assume that ϕ∗ induces an epimorphism A/m−→B/mB. Then there
is a special aﬃnoid subdomain X ′֒→X containing x such that the resulting
morphism ϕ′ : Y ′ −→ X ′ induced from ϕ on Y ′ = ϕ−1(X ′) is a closed im-
mersion in the sense that the corresponding morphism of aﬃno id K-algebras
ϕ′∗ : A′−→B′ is an epimorphism .
(ii) Assume that ϕ∗ induces isomorphisms A/mn ∼−→B/mnB for all n∈ N.
Then there is a special aﬃnoid subdomain X ′֒→X containing x such that
the resulting morphism ϕ′ : Y ′−→X ′ induced from ϕ on Y ′ = ϕ−1(X ′) is an
isomorphism.
Proof. We start with a general remark. As A/m is a ﬁeld, the epimorphism
A/m−→B/mB is either an isomorphism or the zero mapping. Hence, mB
=====PAGE 48=====

Proposition 19. Let U−→X be a morphism of rigid K-spaces deﬁning U as
an aﬃnoid subdomain of X. Then U is open in X, and the canonical topology
of X restricts to the one of U.

Proof. By Lemma 10(ii), the morphism U −→ X satisﬁes the conditions of

To characterise the structure of general aﬃnoid subdomains in more precise
terms, we cite already at this place the following result:
Theorem 20 (Gerritzen - Grauert). Let X be an aﬃnoid K-space and U⊂X
an aﬃnoid subdomain . Then U is a ﬁnite union of rational subdomains of X.
A more general version of this theorem will be proved in Sect. 1.8; see
1.8/10 and 1.8/12. However, it should be noted that, in gener al, a ﬁnite union
of aﬀonid subdomains of X, even of Weierstraß domains, does not yield an
aﬃnoid subdomain again.
1.7 Germs of aﬃnoid functions
Let X be an aﬃnoid K-space. For any aﬃnoid subdomain U⊂ X we denote
byOX(U) the aﬃnoid K-algebra corresponding to U. Then, if U ⊂ V is an
inclusion of aﬃnoid subdomains of X, there is a canonical morphism between
corresponding aﬃnoid K-algebrasOX(V )−→O X(U) which we might inter-
pret as restriction of aﬃnoid functions on V to aﬃnoid functions on U. More
precisely,OX is a presheaf of aﬃnoid K-algebras on the category of aﬃnoid
subdomains of X. This means that OX associates to any aﬃnoid subdomain
U⊂ X an aﬃnoid K-algebraOX(U) and to any inclusion U⊂ V of aﬃnoid
subdomains in X a morphism of aﬃnoid K-algebras ρV
U :OX(V )−→OX(U)
(generally denoted by f↦−→f|U ) such that for subdomains U⊂V ⊂W of X
the following conditions are fulﬁlled:
(i) ρU
U = id ,
(ii) ρW

V .
The presheafOX will be referred to as the presheaf of aﬃnoid functions onX.
For any point x∈X the ring

where the limit runs over all aﬃnoid subdomains U⊂X containingx, is called
the stalk ofOX at x. Its elements are called germs of aﬃnoid functions at x.
To give a more explicit characterization of OX,x, we can say that any germ
fx∈O X,x is represented by some function f∈O X(U) for some aﬃnoid subdo-
main U⊂ X containing x and that two functions fi∈O X(Ui), i = 1, 2, with
x∈U1∩U2 represent the same germ fx∈O X,x if and only if there is an aﬃnoid

Proposition 1. LetX be an aﬃnoid K-space and x∈X a point corresponding
to the maximal ideal m⊂O X(X). ThenOX,x is a local ring with maximal ideal
mOX,x.
Proof. For any aﬃnoid subdomain U ⊂ X containing x the morphism of
aﬃnoid K-algebras OX(X) −→ OX(U) induces by 1.6/10 an isomorphism
OX(X)/m ∼−→O X(U)/mOX (U). Thus, passing to the direct limit, we get a
surjective map OX,x−→K ′, where K ′≃O X(X)/m is a ﬁeld which is ﬁnite
overK. The map may be viewed as evaluation at x, and we will use the notation
fx↦−→fx(x) for it. Its kernel n is a maximal ideal in OX,x, and we claim that
n = mOX,x. Clearly we have mOX,x⊂ n. To show the converse, consider an el-
ementfx∈O X,x represented by some f∈O X(U) for some aﬃnoid subdomain
U⊂ X. Then, if fx(x) = 0 we must have f (x) = 0 and, hence, f∈ mOX(U),
which implies fx∈ mOX,x. Alternatively we could have used the fact that lim−→preserves exact sequences.
That n is the only maximal ideal in OX,x is easy to see. Consider an element
fx∈O X,x− n represented by some f ∈O X(U) for some aﬃnoid subdomain
U⊂ X. Then f (x)⁄= 0 and, multiplying f by a suitable constant in K ∗, we
can even assume that |f (x)|≥ 1. But then U(f −1) contains x and is an aﬃnoid
subdomain of X such that f|U (f −1) is a unit. Consequently, fx is a unit in OX,x,
and n is the only maximal ideal in OX,x. □

Proposition 6. For any point x of an aﬃnoid K-spaceX, the local ring OX,x
is noetherian .
Proof. Let X = SpA and let m⊂A be the maximal ideal corresponding to x.
Then the local ring OX,x is m-adically separated; i. e., ⋂
n∈N mnOX,x = 0. In
fact, consider an element fx∈ ⋂
n∈N mnOX,x. There is an aﬃnoid subdomain
U⊂ X containing x such that fx is represented by some element f∈O X(U)
and it follows f∈ mnOX(U) for each n∈ N by Proposition 2; we may write
X = SpA instead of U again. Then it follows from Krull’s intersection theorem
that the image of f in Am is trivial. In particular, fx = 0.
In the same way we can show for any ﬁnitely generated ideal ax⊂O X,x that
the residue ring OX,x/ax is m-adically separated. Namely, ﬁxing a ﬁnite gener-
ating system of ax, we may assume that these generators extend to functions in
A and, hence, that ax is induced from an ideal a⊂ A. Then we can interpret
OX,x/ax as a stalk of the aﬃnoid space Sp A/a and see that it is m-acically
separated. The latter says that ﬁnitely generated ideals in OX,x are closed with
repsect to the m-adic topology.
Now consider an increasing sequence of ﬁnitely gnerated ide als
a1⊂ a2⊂... ⊂O X,x,
as well as the corresponding sequence of m-adic completions
ˆa1⊂ ˆa2⊂... ⊂ ˆOX,x.
We use that ˆOX,x = ˆAm is noetherian, as it is the maximal adic completion of
a noetherian local ring. So the chain in ˆOX,x becomes stationary. As OX,x is
m-adically separated, the canonical map OX,x−→ ˆOX,x is injective. But then
the m-adic closedness of the ideals ai implies that also the chain in OX,x must
become stationary. Thus,OX,x is noetherian. □

1.8 Locally closed immersions of aﬃnoid spaces
In the present section, we want to characterize aﬃnoid subdo mains of aﬃnoid
K-spaces in local terms and thereby provide a proof of the Theo rem of Gerritzen
and Grauert 1.6/20.
Deﬁnition 1. Let ϕ : X ′ −→ X be a morphism of aﬃnoid K-spaces. ϕ is
called a closed immersion if the corresponding morphism of aﬃnoid K-algebras
ϕ∗ :OX(X)−→OX′(X ′) is surjective. Furthermore,ϕ is called a locally closed
immersion ( resp. an open immersion) if it is injective and , for any x∈X ′, the
induced morphism ϕ∗
x :OX,ϕ(x)−→OX′,x is surjective (resp. bijective).
For example, any morphism of aﬃnoidK-spacesϕ : X ′−→X, which deﬁnes
X ′ as an aﬃnoid subdomain of X, is an open immersion, due to the transitivity
of aﬃnoid subdomains mentioned in 1.6/12. On the other hand, ifϕ is a closed
immersion, we see from 1.6/13 that ϕ is, in particular, a locally closed immer-
sion. Furthermore, any composition of locally closed (resp . closed, resp. open)
immersions is an immersion of the same type again.
Remark 2. Let ϕ : X ′−→X be a closed (resp. a locally closed , resp. an open )
immersion of aﬃnoid K-spaces. Then, for any aﬃnoid subdomain U⊂X, the
induced morphism ϕU : ϕ−1(U)−→U is an immersion of the same type .
Proof. The assertion is immediately clear for locally closed and o pen immersions
ϕ, since these are characterized locally on X, and since aﬃnoid subdomains
U⊂X are open, due to 1.6/19. Concerning closed immersions ϕ, the assertion
is easily derived for Weierstraß, Laurent, or rational doma ins U⊂ X, due to
their explicit description. The remaining case of a closed i mmersion ϕ and a
general aﬃnoid subdomain U⊂ X is settled with the help of 1.6/13 from the
fact that for a morphism of aﬃnoid K-algebrasϕ∗ : A−→A′ and a base change
morphism A −→ B with an aﬃnoid K-algebra B, the resulting morphism
ϕ ˆ⊗ idB : B−→A′ ˆ⊗AB is surjective, when ϕ∗ is surjective; see [BGR], 6.1.1/11.
□
More generally, one can show that closed (resp. locally clos ed, resp. open)
immersions are preserved under aﬃnoid base change.
Proposition 3. Let ϕ : X ′ −→ X be a locally closed immersion of aﬃ-
noid K-spaces, and assume that the corresponding homomorphism of aﬃnoid
K-algebras is ﬁnite . Then ϕ is a closed immersion .
Proof. Writing X ′ = Sp A′ and X = Sp A, the morphism ϕ induces for any

Next we introduce a particular class of locally closed immer sions, so-called
Runge immersions .
Deﬁnition 5. A morphism of aﬃnoid K-spacesϕ : X ′−→X is called a Runge
immersion if it is the composition of a closed immersion X ′−→W and an open
immersion W−→X deﬁning W as a Weierstraß domain in X.
From Remark 2 we can immediately deduce:
Remark 6. Letϕ : X ′−→X be a Runge immersion of aﬃnoid K-spaces. Then,
for any aﬃnoid subdomain U⊂ X, the induced morphism ϕU : ϕ−1(U)−→U
is a Runge immersion , too.
If σ : A−→A′ is a morphism of aﬃnoid K-algebras, we call ﬁnitely many
elements h1,...,h n ∈ A′ a system of aﬃnoid generators of A′ over A (with
respect to σ) if σ extends to an epimorphism
A⟨ζ1,...,ζ n⟩−→A′, ζ i↦−→hi.
Of course, the hi∈A′ are then necessarily power bounded.
Proposition 7. For a morphism of aﬃnoid K-algebras σ : A−→A′ the fol-
lowing are equivalent :
(i) The morphism of aﬃnoid K-spaces ϕ : Sp A′−→ SpA associated to σ
is a Runge immersion .
(ii) σ(A) is dense in A′.
(iii) σ(A) contains a system of aﬃnoid generators of A′ over A.
Proof. If ϕ is a Runge immersion, ϕ(A) is dense in A′, since the corresponding
fact is true for closed immersions and for Weierstraß domain s. Next, choose a
system h′
1,...,h ′
n of aﬃnoid generators of A′ overA. Then, if σ(A) is dense in
A′, we can approximate each h′
i by some hi∈ σ(A) in such a way that, using
Lemma 8 below, h1,...,h n will be a system of aﬃnoid generators of A′ overA.
Finally, assume that h1,...,h n∈σ(A) is a system of aﬃnoid generators of A′
overA. Then σ decomposes into the maps
A−→A⟨h1,...,h n⟩−→A′,
=====PAGE 57=====
1.8 Locally closed immersions of aﬃnoid spaces 55
where the ﬁrst map corresponds to the inclusion of the Weiers traß domain
X(h1,...,h n) into X = Sp A, and where the second is surjective and, hence,
corresponds to a closed immersion Sp A′−→X(h1,...,h n). Thus, ϕ is a Runge
immersion. □
As a consequence we see that the composition of ﬁnitely many R unge im-

In the general case, we can always enlarge the value group |K ∗| by passing
to a suitable ﬁnite algebraic extension L/K. This way, we can assume ε0∈|L∗|
and, in addition, that the last step in the above argumentati on works for some
ε>ε 0 contained in|L∗|. In other words, the assertion of the Extension Lemma
holds after replacing the base ﬁeld K by a suitable ﬁnite algebraic extension L
in the sense that we apply the base change functor
SpA↦−→SpA ˆ⊗KL,
to our problem, with ˆ⊗ denoting the completed tensor product. Thus, it is
enough to show that a morphism of aﬃnoid K-spacesX ′−→X is a Runge im-
mersion if the corresponding morphism of aﬃnoid L-spacesX ′ ˆ⊗KL−→X ˆ⊗KL
has this property or, equivalently, that a morphism of aﬃnoi d K-algebras
A−→A′ has dense image if the corresponding morphism of aﬃnoid L-algebras
A ˆ⊗KL−→A′ ˆ⊗KL has dense image. However, the latter is easy to see. Since the
completed tensor product commutes with topologically dire ct sums, it follows
that the canonical morphism A⊗K L−→A ˆ⊗KL is bijective for any aﬃnoid
K-algebra A and any ﬁnite extension L/K. Now consider a morphism of aﬃ-
noidK-algebrasσ : A−→A′, and let A′′⊂A′ be the closure of σ(A). Then the
morphismσ⊗KL : A⊗KL−→A′⊗KL factors through the closed subalgebra
A′′⊗KL⊂A′⊗KL. If σ⊗KL has dense image, we see that A′′⊗KL coincides
withA′⊗KL and, hence, by descent, that the same is true for A′′ andA′. Thus,
we are done. □
Next, let us look more closely at the structure of locally clo sed immersions.
We begin by stating the main structure theorem for such immer sions and by
deriving some of its consequences.
Theorem 10 (Gerritzen – Grauert). Let ϕ : X ′−→X be a locally closed im-
mersion of aﬃnoid K-spaces. Then there exists a covering X = ⋃ r
i=1Xi con-
sisting of ﬁnitely many rational subdomains Xi⊂X such that ϕ induces Runge
immersions ϕi : ϕ−1(Xi)−→Xi for i = 1,...,r .
Corollary 11. If, in the situation of Theorem 10, ϕ : X ′−→X is an open
immersion, then the maps ϕi deﬁne ϕ−1(Xi) as a Weierstraß domain in Xi,
i = 1,...,r .
Proof. It is enough to show that a Runge immersion ϕ : X ′−→X, which at the
same time is an open immersion, deﬁnes X ′ as a Weierstraß domain in X. Since
ϕ is the composition of a closed immersion X ′֒→W and of a Weierstraß domain
֒→X, we may assume W = X and thereby are reduced to the case, where
ϕ is a closed immersion. But then the assertion follows from Pr oposition 4. □
=====PAGE 61=====
1.8 Locally closed immersions of aﬃnoid spaces 59
Corollary 12. Let X be an aﬃnoid K-space and X ′⊂ X an aﬃnoid sub-
domain. Then there exists a covering X = ⋃ r
i=1Xi consisting of ﬁnitely many
rational subdomains Xi⊂X such that Xi∩X ′ is a Weierstraß domain in Xi
for i = 1,...,r . In particular,X ′ is a ﬁnite union of rational subdomains in X.
Proof. The inclusion X ′֒→X is an open immersion. Thus, we may apply Corol-
lary 11 and use the fact that all intersections Xi∩X ′ are rational subdomains
of X by 1.6/17. □

1.9 Tate’s Acyclicity Theorem
LetX be an aﬃnoid K-space and T = TX the category of aﬃnoid subdomains
inX, with inclusions as morphisms. A presheaf F (of groups, rings, . . . ) on T is
called a sheaf if for any object U∈ T and any covering U = ⋃
i∈IUi by objects
Ui∈ T the following hold:
(S1) If f∈F (U) satisﬁes f|Ui = 0 for all i∈I, then f = 0.
(S2) Given elements fi∈F (Ui) such that fi|Ui∩Uj =fj|Ui∩Uj for all i,j ∈I,
there is an f ∈F (U) (necessarily unique by (S 1)) such that f|Ui = fi for all
i∈I.
So if F is a sheaf, we can say that, in a certain sense, the elements of the
groups (or rings etc.) F (U) with U varying over T can be constructed locally.
In the present section we are interested in the case, where F equals the presheaf
OX of aﬃnoid functions on X. We know from 1.7/4 that condition (S 1) holds
forOX. However, due to the total disconnectedness of the canonica l topology
on X, condition (S 2) cannot be satisﬁed for OX, except for trivial cases. So, in
strict terms, OX cannot be called a sheaf. Nevertheless, we will see that OX
satisﬁes the sheaf condition (S 2) for ﬁnite coverings U = ⋃
i∈IUi. This fact,
which is a special case of Tate’s acyclicity theorem, is basi c for rigid geometry,
and we will give a direct proof for it.
Sheaf conditions (S 1) and (S 2) can be conveniently phrased by requiring
that the sequence
=====PAGE 66=====
64 1. Classical Rigid Geometry
OX(U)→
∏
i∈I
OX(Ui) ⇉
∏
i,j∈I
OX(Ui∩Uj),(∗)
f↦−→(f|Ui)i∈I, (fi)i∈I↦−→
{
(fi|Ui∩Uj )i,j∈I
(fj|Ui∩Uj )i,j∈I
be exact for any U∈ T and any covering U = (Ui)i∈I ofU by sets Ui∈ T. Note
that a sequence of maps A→B ⇉C is called exact if A is mapped bijectively
onto the subset of C consisting of all elements having same image under the
maps B ⇉ C. For a presheaf F on X and a covering U = (Ui)i∈I of X by
aﬃnoid subdomains Ui⊂X, we will say that F is a U-sheaf, if for all aﬃnoid
subdomains U⊂X the sequence (∗) applied to the covering U|U = (Ui∩U)i∈I
is exact.
Theorem 1 (Tate). Let X be an aﬃnoid K-space. The presheafOX of aﬃnoid
functions is a U-sheaf on X for any ﬁnite covering U = (Ui)i∈I of X by aﬃnoid
subdomains Ui⊂X.
The proof will be done by reducing to more simple coverings, where, ﬁna lly,
a direct computation is possible. We begin by discussing the necessary reduction
steps. Consider two coverings U = (Ui)i∈I and V = (Vj)j∈J of X. Then V is
called a reﬁnement of U if there exists a map τ : J−→I such that Vj⊂ Uτ (j)
for all j∈J. In the following, F will be any presheaf on X.
Lemma 2. Let U = (Ui)i∈I and V = (Vj)j∈J be coverings of X by aﬃnoid
subdomains, where V is a reﬁnement of U. Then, if F is a V-sheaf, it is a
U-sheaf, too.
Proof. We will show the exactness of ( ∗) for the covering U; the proof for its
restriction U|U on any aﬃnoid subdomain U⊂ X works in the same way. So
consider elementsfi∈F (Ui),i∈I, such thatfi|Ui∩Ui′ =fi′|Ui∩Ui′ for alli,i ′∈I.
Choosing a map τ : J−→I such that Vj⊂Uτ (j), set gj =fτ (j)|Vj for all j∈J.
Then we have
gj|Vj∩Vj′ = (fτ (j)|Uτ (j)∩Uτ (j′))|Vj∩Vj′ = (fτ (j′)|Uτ (j)∩Uτ (j′))|Vj∩Vj′ =gj′|Vj∩Vj′
and, asF is a V-sheaf, there is a unique element f∈F (X) such that f|Vj =gj
for all j∈J. We claim that f|Ui =fi for all i∈I. To do this, ﬁx an i∈I. Then
(f|Ui)|Ui∩Vj =f|Ui∩Vj =gj|Ui∩Vj
for j∈J. Furthermore,
fi|Ui∩Vj =fi|Ui∩Uτ (j)∩Vj =fτ (j)|Ui∩Uτ (j)∩Vj =gj|Ui∩Vj,
and, thus, we see that fi|Ui∩Vj = (f|Ui)|Ui∩Vj . Now, using the fact that F is a
V-sheaf when restricted to Ui, we see that necessarily f|Ui = fi for all i∈ I.
=====PAGE 67=====
1.9 Tate’s Acyclicity Theorem 65
Clearly,f is uniquely determined by these conditions, and it follows t hatF is
a U-sheaf. □
Lemma 3. Let U = (Ui)i∈I and V = (Vj)j∈J be coverings of X by aﬃnoid
subdomains. Assume that
(i)F is a V-sheaf, and
(ii) the restriction of F to Vj is a U|Vj -sheaf for all j∈J.
ThenF is a U-sheaf, too.
Proof. Again, we will show the exactness of the sequence ( ∗) for the covering U;
the proof for the restriction U|U on any aﬃnoid subdomain U⊂X works in the
same way. Thus, consider elements fi∈F (Ui) such that fi|Ui∩Ui′ =fi′|Ui∩Ui′ for
all i,i ′∈I. Then, ﬁxing j∈J, we have
fi|Ui∩Ui′ ∩Vj =fi′|Ui∩Ui′ ∩Vj,
and condition (ii) implies that there exists a unique gj ∈ F(Vj) such that
gj|Ui∩Vj =fi|Ui∩Vj for all i∈I. Fixing j,j ′∈J, we get
gj|Ui∩Vj ∩Vj′ =fi|Ui∩Vj ∩Vj′ =gj′|Ui∩Vj ∩Vj′
for all i∈ I. Hence, again by condition (ii), we have gj|Vj ∩Vj′ = gj′|Vj ∩Vj′ and,
by condition (i), there exists a unique element g∈F (X) satisfying g|Vj =gj for
allj∈J. Now by construction, g coincides with fi, when we restrict to Ui∩Vj,
for all i∈ I, j∈ J. But then, by condition (i), we must have g|Ui = fi for all
i∈I. As g is uniquely determined by these conditions, as is easily ver iﬁed, we
see thatF is a U-sheaf. □

We can now sum up the essence of Lemmata 2 to 6.
Proposition 7. LetF be a presheaf on the aﬃnoid K-space X. If F is a
U-sheaf on X for each Laurent covering U, it is , more generally , a V-sheaf for
each aﬃnoid covering V of X.
Proof. Start with a general aﬃnoid covering V of X. We have to show that
F is a V-sheaf, provided it is a U-sheaf for each Laurent covering U of X. By
Lemma 4 there is a rational covering which reﬁnes V and, using Lemma 2, we
may assume that V itself is a rational covering. Furthermore, using Lemma 3 in
conjunction with Lemma 5, we may even assume that V is a rational covering
which is generated by units in OX(X). But then Lemma 6 in conjunction with
Lemma 2, again reduces everything to the case where V is a Laurent covering
of X, and we are done. □

1.10 Grothendieck topologies
As we have already indicated at the end of Sect. 1.1, the presh eaf of aﬃnoid
functionsOX on an aﬃnoid K-spaceX cannot satisfy sheaf properties if we do
not restrict the variety of all possible open coverings. In f act, Tate’s acyclicity
theorem in the version of 1.9/1 or 1.9/10 is about the best one can expect
for general aﬃnoid spaces, and we will base the construction of global rigid
K-spaces by glueing local aﬃnoid parts on this result. As a tec hnical trick, we
generalize the notion of a topology.
Deﬁnition 1. A Grothendieck topology T consists of a category Cat T and a
set Cov T of families (Ui−→U)i∈I of morphisms in Cat T, called coverings,
such that the following hold :
(1) If Φ : U−→V is an isomorphism in Cat T, then (Φ)∈ Cov T.
(2) If (Ui−→U)i∈I and (Vij−→Ui)j∈Ji for i∈I belong to Cov T, then the
same is true for the composition (Vij−→Ui−→U)i∈I,j ∈Ji.
(3) If (Ui−→U)i∈I is in Cov T and if V −→U is a morphism in Cat T,
then the ﬁbred products Ui×UV exist in Cat T, and (Ui×UV−→V )i∈I belongs
to Cov T.
We may think of the objects of Cat T as of the open sets of our topology
and of the morphisms of Cat T as of the inclusions of open sets. Furthermore, a
family (Ui−→U)i∈I of Cov T has to be interpreted as a covering of U by the Ui
and a ﬁbred product of type Ui×UV as the intersection of Ui withV . Thinking
along these lines an ordinary topological space X is canonically equipped with
a Grothendieck topology: Cat T is the category of open subsets of X, with
inclusions as morphisms, and Cov T consists of all open covers of open subsets
ofX. However, there are more general examples, where the morphi sms of Cat T
are far from being monomorphisms, like the ´ etale topology, the fppf -topology,
or the fpqc-topology in algebraic geometry. It should be pointed out th at the
=====PAGE 75=====
1.10 Grothendieck topologies 73
“intersection” of “open” sets is dealt with in condition (3) of Deﬁnition 1,
whereas we have refrained from giving any sense to the union o f “open” sets. In
fact, even in examples, where the union of “open” sets does ma ke sense, such a
union will not necessarily yield an “open” set again.
The notion of a Grothendiek topology has been designed in suc h a way that
the notion of presheaf or sheaf can easily be adapted to such a situation:
Deﬁnition 2. Let T be a Grothendieck topology and C a category which admits
cartesian products. A presheaf on T with values in C is a contravariant functor
F : Cat T−→ C. We call F a sheaf if the diagram
F (U)→
∏
i∈I
F (Ui) ⇉
∏
i,j∈I
F (Ui×U Uj)
is exact for any covering (Ui−→U)i∈I in Cov T.
From now on we will exclusively consider Grothendieck topol ogies T of a
special type. Namely, the category Cat T will always be a category of certain
subsets of a given set X, with inclusions as morphisms. The objects of Cat T
will be referred to as the admissible open subsets of X. Likewise, the elements
of Cov T are called the admissible coverings, and we will only consider those
cases, where admissible coverings ( Ui−→U)i∈I are, indeed, true coverings of
U by admissible open sets Ui. To let the set X intervene, we will talk about a
Grothendieck topology T on X and call X a G-topological space. Of course, we
are interested in the case where X is an aﬃnoid K-space, and in Grothendieck
topologies on X with respect to which the presheaf OX of aﬃnoid functions
is actually a sheaf. A straightforward possibility to deﬁne such a Grothendieck
topology is as follows:
Deﬁnition 3. For any aﬃnoid K-spaceX, let Cat T be the category of aﬃnoid
subdomains of X with inclusions as morphisms , and Cov T the set of all ﬁnite
families (Ui −→ U)i∈I of inclusions of aﬃnoid subdomains in X such that
U = ⋃
i∈IUi. Then T is called the weak Grothendieck topology on X.
That we really get a Grothendieck topology on X is easily veriﬁed. It follows
from 1.6/8 and 1.6/19 that all admissible open subsets of X (in the sense of the
weak Grothendieck topology) are open with respect to the can onical topology,
as introduced in 1.6/1. Furthermore, if ϕ : Z−→X is a morphism of aﬃnoid
K-spaces, the inverse image ϕ−1(U) of any admissible open subset U⊂ X is
admissible open in Z by 1.6/13, and the inverse image of an admissible covering
inX yields an admissible covering in Z. Thus, we can say that ϕ is continuous
with respect to the weak Grothendieck topology on Z and X.
It has to be pointed out that the presheaf OX of aﬃnoid functions on any
aﬃnoid K-space X really is a presheaf in the sense of Deﬁnition 2, if X is
equipped with the weak Grothendieck topology. Even better, Tate’s acyclicity
theorem 1.9/1 says that OX is a sheaf in this context.
=====PAGE 76=====
74 1. Classical Rigid Geometry
There is a canonical way to enlarge the weak Grothendieck top ology on
aﬃnoid K-spaces by adding more admissible open sets and more admissi ble
coverings in such a way that morphisms of aﬃnoid K-spaces remain continuous
and sheaves extend to sheaves with respect to this new topolo gy. The resulting
Grothendieck topology is the strong Grothendieck topology on aﬃonid K-spaces
which we will deﬁne now.
Deﬁnition 4. LetX be an aﬃnoid K-space. The strong Grothendieck topology
on X is given as follows .
(i) A subset U⊂X is called admissible open if there is a (not necessarily
ﬁnite) covering U = ⋃
i∈IUi of U by aﬃnoid subdomains Ui⊂ X such that
for all morphisms of aﬃnoid K-spaces ϕ : Z−→X satisfying ϕ(Z)⊂ U the
covering (ϕ−1(Ui))i∈I of Z admits a reﬁnement , which is a ﬁnite covering of Z
by aﬃnoid subdomains .
(ii) A covering V = ⋃
j∈JVj of some admissible open subset V ⊂ X by
means of admissible open sets Vj is called admissible if for each morphism of
aﬃnoid K-spacesϕ : Z−→X satisfying ϕ(Z)⊂V the covering (ϕ−1(Vj))j∈J of
Z admits a reﬁnement , which is a ﬁnite covering of Z by aﬃnoid subdomains .
Note that any covering ( Ui)i∈I as in (i) is admissible by (ii). It is easily
checked that the strong Grothendieck topology on X really is a Grothendieck
topology such that any ﬁnite union of aﬃnoid subdomains of X is admissible
open. Furthermore, a direct veriﬁcation shows that it enjoy s certain complete-
ness properties which, as we will see, allow to construct Gro thendieck topologies
on global spaces from local ones.
Proposition 5. Let X be an aﬃnoid K-space. The strong Grothendieck topol-
ogy is a Grothendieck topology on X which satisﬁes the following completeness
conditions:
(G0)∅ and X are admissible open .
(G1) Let (Ui)i∈I be an admissible covering of an admissible open subset
U⊂X. Furthermore, let V⊂U be a subset such that V∩Ui is admissible open
for all i∈I. Then V is admissible open in X.
(G2) Let (Ui)i∈I be a covering of an admissible open set U⊂X by admissible
open subsets Ui⊂X which admits an admissible covering of U as reﬁnement .
Then (Ui)i∈I itself is admissible .
Again, let X be an aﬃnoid K-space. Then, if U ⊂ X is an aﬃnoid
subdomain, the strong Grothendieck topology on X restricts to the strong
Grothendieck topology on U, viewed as an aﬃnoid K-space of its own. More
generally, we show:
Proposition 6. Let ϕ : Y−→X be a morphism of aﬃnoid K-spaces. Then ϕ
is continuous with respect to strong Grothendieck topologi es on X and Y .
=====PAGE 77=====
1.10 Grothendieck topologies 75
Proof. Consider an admissible open set U ⊂ X and an admissible covering
U = (Ui)i∈I of U where all Ui are aﬃnoid subdomains of X; such a covering
exists since, starting out from an arbitrary admissible cov ering U′ of U, any
U ′∈ U′ must have an admissible covering consisting of aﬃnoid subdo mains of
X. To show that V =ϕ−1(U) is admissible open in Y , consider a morphism of
aﬃnoid K-spaces τ : Z−→Y such that τ (Z)⊂V . Then ϕ◦τ maps Z intoU
and we see that ( τ −1ϕ−1(Ui))i∈I is a covering of Z which is reﬁned by a (ﬁnite)
aﬃnoid covering of Z. But then, as the sets ϕ−1(Ui) are aﬃnoid subdomains of
Y coveringV , it follows that V is admissible open in Y .
More generally, if (Ui)i∈I is an arbitrary admissible covering of an admissible
open set U⊂ X, the same argument shows that ( ϕ−1(Ui))i∈I is an admissible
covering of ϕ−1(U). Thus, ϕ is continuous with respect to strong Grothendieck
topologies. □

As any Zariski open subset of an aﬃnoid K-spaceX is a ﬁnite union of sets
