Corollary 9. Let X be an aﬃnoid K-space. Then the strong Grothendieck
topology on X is ﬁner than the Zariski topology ; i. e., each Zariski open subset
U⊂X is admissible open , and each Zariski covering is admissible .
We end this section by some remarks on how to construct global Grothen-
dieck topologies from local data.
Proposition 10. Let T be a Grothendieck topology on a set X such that con-
ditions (G0), (G 1), and (G2) of Proposition 5 are satisﬁed . Let (Xi)i∈I be an
admissible covering of X. Then:
(i) A subset U⊂X is admissible open if and only if all intersections U∩Xi,
i∈I, are admissible open .
(ii) A covering (Uj)j∈J of some admissible open subset U⊂X is admissible
if and only if (Xi∩Uj)j∈J is an admissible covering of Xi∩U, for all i∈I.
The proof is straightforward. Assertion (i) is a direct consequence o f condi-
tion (G 1), whereas (ii) follows from (G 2), since (Xi∩U)i∈I and (Xi∩Uj)i∈I,j ∈J
are admissible coverings of U. □
Proposition 11. Let X be a set and (Xi)i∈I a covering of X. Furthermore,
let Ti be a Grothendieck topology on Xi,i∈I, such that conditions (G0), (G 1),
and (G2) of Proposition 5 are satisﬁed . For all i,j ∈ I, assume that Xi∩Xj
is Ti-open (i. e., admissible open with respect to Ti) in Xi and that Ti and Tj
restrict to the same Grothendieck topology on Xi∩Xj. Then there is a unique
Grothendieck topology T on X such that the following hold :
(i) Xi is T-open in X, and T induces Ti on Xi.
(ii) T satisﬁes conditions (G0), (G 1), and (G2) of Proposition 5.
(iii) (Xi)i∈I is a T-covering of X (i. e., admissible with respect to T).
Proof. Due to Proposition 10, there is at most one possibility to de ﬁne T.
Namely, call a subset U ⊂ X T-open if each intersection Xi∩U, i∈ I, is
=====PAGE 80=====
78 1. Classical Rigid Geometry
Ti-open. Similarly, we call a covering U = (Uj)j∈J consisting of T-open sets
Uj⊂ X a T-covering if, for each i∈ I, the covering U|Xi = (Xi∩Uj)j∈J is a
Ti-covering of Xi∩U. That T is a Grothendieck topology as required is easily
checked. □
1.11 Sheaves
In the following, let X be a G-topological space; i. e., a set with a Grothendieck
topology T on it. As in 1.10/2 we deﬁne a presheaf of groups, rings, etc. o n X
as a contravariant functor F from Cat T to the category of groups, rings, etc.
Furthermore,F is called a sheaf if for each admissible covering ( Ui)i∈I of an
admissible open set U⊂X the diagram
F (U)→
∏
i∈I
F (Ui) ⇉
∏
i,j∈I
F (Ui×U Uj)
is exact.
We are, of course, interested in the case where X is an aﬃnoid K-space.
Considering the weak Grothendieck topology on X, we have introduced the
presheafOX of aﬃnoid functions on X, and we have seen from Tate’s acyclicity
theorem 1.9/1 that OX even is a sheaf. One of the objectives of this section is
to pass on to the strong Grothendieck topology on X and to show that sheaves
extend canonically from the weak to the strong Grothendieck topology on X.
LetX be an arbitrary G-topological space again. For any presheaf F onX
and a point x∈X, we deﬁne
Fx = lim−→F (U),
where the limit runs over all admissible open U⊂X containingx, as the stalk
ofF at the point x. Next, let σ :F−→F ′ be a morphism of presheaves on X.
Thereby we mean a system of morphisms σU :F (U)−→F ′(U) with U varying
over all admissible open subsets of X, which is compatible with the restriction
morphisms ofF andF ′. Such a morphism induces for any x∈X a morphism
σx :Fx−→F ′
x on the level of stalks.
Deﬁnition 1. LetF be a presheaf on a G-topological spaceX. A sheaﬁﬁcation
of F is a morphism F −→F′, whereF ′ is a sheaf , such that the following
universal property is satisﬁed :
Each morphism F −→ G, where G is a sheaf , factors uniquely through
F−→F ′ via a morphism F ′−→G .
In the situation of Deﬁnition 1, F ′ is called the sheaf associated toF . Such a
sheaf can always be constructed, as we will see below. The cla ssical construction
of associated sheaves on a topological space X is to consider the disjoint union
E = ∐
x∈XFx which comes equipped with a canonical projection π : E−→X.
=====PAGE 81=====
1.11 Sheaves 79
For any open subset U ⊂ X, a map f : U −→E is called a section of π if
π◦f = id; so f associates to any point x∈ U an element fx∈F x. Now let
F ′(U) be the set of those sections f : U−→E of π such that, for any x∈ U,
there are an open neighborhood U(x)⊂ U of x and an element g∈F (U(x))
with the property that gy =fy for all y∈U(x). SoF ′(U) consists of all sections
over U which, locally, are induced from elements of the presheaf F , and it is
easily checked thatF−→F ′ is a sheaﬁﬁcation of F .
ForG-topological spaces X the classical construction cannot work properly,
since there are non-trivial sheaves on X having trivial stalks at all points x∈X.
To remedy this, one uses techniques from ˇCech cohomology. For any admissible
open subset U⊂X we set
ˇHq(U,F ) = lim−→Hq(U,F ), q ∈ N,
where the direct limit runs over all admissible coverings U ofU, using the rela-
tion of being ﬁner as a partial ordering on the set of these cov erings. The latter
set is clearly directed since any two such coverings ( Ui)i∈I, (Vj)j∈J admit a com-
mon admissible reﬁnement, for example ( Ui∩Vj)i∈I,j ∈J. To execute the direct
limit, we use, of course, the fact that, for a reﬁnement V of some admissible
covering U ofU, there is always a canonical morphism Hq(U,F )−→Hq(V,F )
(which, for the purpose of sheaﬁﬁcations, will only be neede d for q = 0). Fur-
thermore, varying U, we get the presheaf ˇHq(X,F ) which associates to any
admissible open subset U⊂ X the cohomology group ˇHq(U,F|U). Note that,
for any admissible covering ( Ui)i∈I of some admissible open subset U⊂X, we
have a canonical morphismF (U)−→H 0(U,F ) and, hence, varying U, a canon-
ical morphismF (U)−→ ˇH 0(U,F ). The morphisms of the latter type give rise
to a canonical morphism F−→ ˇH0(X,F ).
Proposition 2. LetF be a presheaf on a G-topological spaceX.
(i) The presheaf F + = ˇH0(X,F ) satisﬁes sheaf property (S1) of Sect . 1.9;
i. e., the canonical map F +(U)−→ ∏
i∈IF +(Ui) is injective for any admissible
covering (Ui)i∈I of an admissible open subset U⊂X.
(ii) If F satisﬁes sheaf property (S1) of Sect. 1.9,F + satisﬁes (S1) and (S2)
and, thus, is a sheaf .
(iii)F ++ = ˇH0(X, ˇH0(X,F )) is a sheaf , and the composition of canonical
morphismsF−→F +−→F ++ is a sheaﬁﬁcation of F .
The proof will be omitted; it is straightforward, although a little bit tech-
nical; cf. [BGR], 9.2.2/3 and 9.2.2/4. As an application of t he existence of as-
sociated sheaves, we can now deﬁne as usual the sheaf image of a morphism of
sheavesσ :F−→G . It is the sheaf associated to the presheaf U↦−→σU (F (U)),
where U varies over all admissible open subsets of X. Similarly, the quotient
F/F0 of a sheaf F by a subsheaf F0 is deﬁned as the sheaf associated to the
presheaf U↦−→F(U)/F0(U).
Finally, we want to attack the problem of extending sheaves f rom the weak
to the strong Grothendieck topology on aﬃnoid K-spaces.
=====PAGE 82=====
80 1. Classical Rigid Geometry
Proposition 3. Let X be a set with Grothendieck topologies T and T′ such
that:
(i) T′ is ﬁner than T.
(ii) Each T′-open set U⊂X admits a T′-covering (Ui)i∈I, where all Ui are
T-open in X.
(iii) Each T′-covering of a T-open subset U ⊂ X admits a T-covering as
reﬁnement.
Then each T-sheaf F on X admits an extension F ′ as a T′-sheaf on X.
The latter is unique up to canonical isomorphism .
We give only some indications on how to construct F ′. Namely, consider
the presheafF ′ with respect to T′ on X which is given by
U↦−→lim−→U
H 0(U,F ),
where the limit runs over all T′-coverings U = (Ui)i∈I ofU, consisting of T-open
sets Ui. Due to condition (iii), F ′ is an extension of F . Using the fact that F
is a sheaf, it is easily checked that F ′ is a sheaf, too. In fact, we may interpret
F ′ as the sheaf ˇH0(XT′,F ), just observing that, in order to construct the latter
object, we need only to know F on the T-open subsets of X.
As a direct consequence, we can state:
Corollary 4. Let X be an aﬃnoid K-space. Then any sheaf F on X with
respect to the weak Grothendieck topology admits a unique ex tension with respect
to the strong Grothendieck topology . The latter applies , in particular , to the
presheaf of aﬃnoid functions F =OX which is a sheaf with respect to the weak
Grothendieck topology by 1.9/1.
ExtendingOX with respect to the strong Grothendieck topology on X, we
will call the resulting sheaf the sheaf of rigid analytic functions on X and use the
notationOX for it again. Anyway, from now on we will always consider, unl ess
stated otherwise, the strong Grothendieck topology on aﬃno id K-spaces.
1.12 Rigid spaces
A ringed K-space is a pair ( X,OX) where X is a topological space and OX
a sheaf of K-algebras on it. This concept can be adapted in a natural way t o
G-topological spaces.
Deﬁnition 1. A G-ringed K-space is a pair (X,OX), where X is a G-topol -
ogical space and OX a sheaf of K-algebras on it . (X,OX) is called a locally
G-ringed K-space if, in addition , all stalks OX,x, x∈X, are local rings .
A morphism of G-ringed K-spaces (X,OX)−→ (Y,OY ) is a pair (ϕ,ϕ ∗),
where ϕ : X−→Y is a map which is continuous with respect to Grothendieck

Proposition 2. Let X and Y be aﬃnoid K-spaces. Then the map from

The assertion of Proposition 2 enables us to identify morphi sms of aﬃ-
noid K-spaces with morphisms of their associated locally G-ringed K-spaces.
In other words, the functor from the category of aﬃnoid K-spaces to the cat-
egory of locally G-ringed K-spaces which we have constructed is fully faithful.
Therefore, in general, we will make no notational diﬀerence between an aﬃnoid
K-space and its associated locally G-ringedK-space, writing simply X instead
of (X,OX). Also note that, due to our construction, an inclusion֒→X of
an aﬃnoid subdomain U into X gives rise to an open immersion of locally
G-ringed K-spaces (U,OU )−→ (X,OX). The latter means that U is an ad-
missible open subset of X, that OU is the restriction of OX to U, and that
(U,OU )−→ (X,OX) is the canonical morphism. Now it is easy to deﬁne global
objects which, locally, look like aﬃnoid K-spaces.
Deﬁnition 4. A rigid (analytic)K-space is a locally G-ringedK-space (X,OX)
such that
(i) the G-topology of X satisﬁes conditions (G0), (G 1), and (G2) of 1.10/5,
and
(ii) X admits an admissible covering (Xi)i∈I where (Xi,OX|Xi) is an aﬃnoid
K-space for all i∈I.
A morphism of rigid K-spaces (X,OX)−→ (Y,OY ) is a morphism in the
sense of locally G-ringed K-spaces.
It follows for an admissible open subset U ⊂ X that the induced locally
G-ringedK-space (U,OX|U ) is a rigid K-space again; we will call ( U,OX|U ) an
open subspace of (X,OX). In most cases, however, rigid K-spaces will simply
be denoted by a single symbol, say X, instead of (X,OX). As usual, global rigid
K-spaces can be constructed by glueing local ones.

More easy is the glueing of morphisms:
Proposition 6. Let X,Y be rigid K-spaces and let (Xi)i∈I be an admissible

such that ϕi|Xi∩Xj : Xi∩Xj−→Y coincides with ϕj|Xi∩Xj : Xi∩Xj−→Y for

satisfying ϕ|Xi =ϕi for all i∈I.
The proof is straightforward by using the sheaf property of OX.

Corollary 8. For two rigid K-spacesX,Y over a third one Z, the ﬁbred product

Proof. The category of aﬃnoid K-spaces admitts ﬁbred products, since, dually,

To show that rigid analytiﬁcations are independent of the ch oice of the con-
stantc∈K and of the representation of K-algebras of ﬁnite type as quotients
K⌈⌊ζ⌉⌋/a, we want to characterize them by a universal property.
Deﬁnition 3. Let (Z,OZ) be a K-scheme of locally ﬁnite type . A rigid analyti-
ﬁcation of (Z,OZ) is a rigid K-space (Z rig,OZ rig) together with a morphism of
locally G-ringed K-spaces (ι,ι ∗) : (Z rig,OZ rig)−→ (Z,OZ) satisfying the follow-
ing universal property :
Given a rigid K-space (Y,OY ) and a morphism of locally G-ringed K-spaces
(Y,OY )−→ (Z,OZ), the latter factors through (ι,ι ∗) via a unique morphism of
rigid K-spaces (Y,OY )−→ (Z rig,OZ rig).
Of course, it has to be shown that those spaces we have called r igid ana-
lytiﬁcations above, are true analytiﬁcations in the sense o f Deﬁnition 3. This is
easy to do. Fix an aﬃne K-scheme of ﬁnite type Z = SpecK⌈⌊ζ⌉⌋/a and consider

After these preparations, we can show without diﬃculties:
Proposition 4. Each K-scheme Z of locally ﬁnite type admits an analytiﬁca-
tion Z rig.
Proof. We know the assertion already if Z is aﬃne. In the general case we
choose a covering of Z by aﬃne open subschemes Zi. The latter admit analyti-
ﬁcations ιi : Z rig
i −→Zi. It follows then from the deﬁnition of analytiﬁcations
that ι−1
i (Zi∩Zj)−→Zi∩Zj is an analytiﬁcation of Zi∩Zj. But then we can
transport the glueing data we have on the Zi to the analytiﬁcations Z rig
i and
construct a rigid K-spaceZ rig from them using 1.12/5. Applying 1.12/6, we get
a morphism of locally G-ringedK-spaces Z rig−→Z, which is easily seen to be
an analytiﬁcation of Z. □
It follows from the deﬁning universal property of rigid anal ytiﬁcations that
morphisms between K-schemes of locally ﬁnite type admit well-deﬁned analyti-
ﬁcations, too. Thus we can state:
Corollary 5. Rigid analytiﬁcation deﬁnes a functor from the category of
K-schemes of locally ﬁnite type to the category of rigid K-spaces.
However, it should be noted that this functor is not fully fai thful in gen-
eral. Thus, for general K-schemes of locally ﬁnite type Y and Z, there will
exist morphisms of rigid K-spaces Y rig−→Z rig which cannot be obtained as
analytiﬁcations of morphisms Y−→Z.
To illustrate the process of rigid analytiﬁcation, let us co nsider some simple
examples. The analytiﬁcation A1,rig
K of the aﬃne 1-space A1
K is constructed by
glueing the increasing sequence of aﬃnoid spaces
SpT (0)
1֒→SpT (1)

1.14 Coherent modules
Consider an aﬃnoid K-space X = SpA and an A-module M. We can look at
the functorF from aﬃnoid subdomains in X to abelian groups, which associates
to any aﬃnoid subdomain Sp A′⊂X the tensor product M⊗AA′. The latter is,
of course, an abelian group, but we can also view it as an A-module or even as
an A′-module.F is a presheaf on X with respect to the weak G-topology, and
this presheaf is, in fact, a sheaf, as we have already remarke d within the context
of Tate’s acyclicity theorem in 1.9/11. In particular, usin g 1.11/3, we see that
F extends to a sheaf with respect to the strong G-topology, again denoted by
F .
It follows from the construction thatF is a so-calledOX-module. This means
that, for any admissible open U ⊂ X, the abelian group F (U) is equipped
with an OX(U)-module structure, in a way that all these module structure s
are compatible with restriction homomorphisms. We call F theOX-module
associated to the A-module M, writingF =M⊗AOX. Note that we have
F|X′ = (M⊗AA′)⊗A′OX|X′
for the restriction of F to any aﬃnoid subdomain X ′ = SpA′ in X.
Proposition 1. Let X = SpA be an aﬃnoid K-space.
(i) The functor
·⊗AOX : M↦−→M⊗AOX
from A-modules toOX-modules is fully faithful .
=====PAGE 94=====
92 1. Classical Rigid Geometry
(ii) The preceding functor commutes with the formation of kernel s, images,
cokernels, and tensor products .
(iii) A sequence of A-modules 0−→M ′−→M−→M ′′−→ 0 is exact if
and only if the associated sequence
0−→M ′⊗AOX−→M⊗AOX−→M ′′⊗AOX−→ 0
is exact .
Proof. It is clear that the canonical map
HomA(M,M ′)−→ HomOX (M⊗AOX,M ′⊗AOX)
is bijective, since any OX-morphism M⊗AOX −→ M ′⊗AOX is uniquely
determined by the inherent A-morphism between M = M⊗AOX(X) and
M ′ =M ′⊗AOX(X). Thus, the functor ·⊗AOX is fully faithful, which set-
tles assertion (i). Furthermore, by its construction, it co mmutes with tensor
products.
Next, if
0−→M ′−→M−→M ′′−→ 0
is an exact sequence of A-modules, the induced sequence
0−→M ′⊗AA′−→M⊗AA′−→M ′′⊗AA′−→ 0
is exact for any aﬃnoid subdomain Sp A′⊂ X, since the corresponding map
A−→A′ is ﬂat by 1.7/5. From this one easily concludes that the funct or of
taking associatedOX-modules is exact; i. e., carries short exact sequences over
to short exact sequences. Then assertion (ii) becomes clear and, furthermore,
also (iii), using the fact that an A-module M is trivial if and only if M⊗AOX
is trivial. □
Deﬁnition 2. Let X be a rigid K-space andF anOX-module.
(i)F is called of ﬁnite type, if there exists an admissble covering (Xi)i∈I
of X together with exact sequences of type
Osi
X|Xi−→F|Xi−→ 0, i ∈I.
(ii)F is called of ﬁnite presentation, if there exists an admissble covering
(Xi)i∈I of X together with exact sequences of type
Ori
X|Xi−→Osi
X|Xi−→F|Xi−→ 0, i ∈I.
(iii)F is called coherent if F is of ﬁnite type and if for any admissible open
subspaceU⊂X the kernel of any morphism Os
X|U−→F|U is of ﬁnite type .
For aﬃnoid K-spaces X = Sp A, we have Or
X = Ar⊗AOX. Furthermore,
as A is noetherian, we conclude from Proposition 1 that kernels a nd cokernels
=====PAGE 95=====
1.14 Coherent modules 93
of morphisms of type Or
X−→Os
X are associated to A-modules of ﬁnite type.
Therefore we can state:
Remark 3. AnOX-moduleF on a rigid K-spaceX is coherent if and only if
there exists an admissible aﬃnoid covering U = (Xi)i∈I of X such that F|Xi is
associated to a ﬁnite OXi(Xi)-module for all i∈I. More precisely, we will say
thatF is U-coherent in this case .
There is a basic result which fully clariﬁes the structure of coherent modules
on aﬃnoid K-spaces:
Theorem 4 (Kiehl). Let X = Sp A be an aﬃnoid K-space and F an
OX-module. Then F is coherent if and only if F is associated to a ﬁnite
A-module.
Before we give the proof, let us observe that this result allo ws a characteri-
zation of coherent OX-modules as follows:
Corollary 5. Let X be a rigid K-space and F an OX-module on it . The
following are equivalent :
(i)F is coherent; i. e.,F is U-coherent for some admissible aﬃnoid covering
U of X.
(ii)F is U-coherent for all admissible aﬃnoid coverings U of X.
Proof. We have only to show that (i) implies (ii). So assume that F is coherent.
In order to derive assertion (ii), we may assume that X is aﬃnoid, sayX = SpA.
But then, applying Theorem 4, F is associated to a ﬁnite A-module and (ii) is
obvious. □
To start the proof of Theorem 4, observe that the if-part of the assertion
is trivial. So assume that F is U-coherent for some admissible aﬃnoid covering
U of X. To show that F is associated to a ﬁnite A-module, we may apply
Lemmata 1.9/4, 1.9/5, and 1.9/6, and thereby restrict ourse lves to the case
where U is a Laurent covering of X. Furthermore, using an inductive argument,
it is only necessary to treat the case where U is a Laurent covering generated
by a single function f∈A. Then it is enough to establish the following facts:
Lemma 6. LetF be U-coherent. Then H 1(U,F ) = 0.
Lemma 7. Assume H 1(U,F ) = 0 for all U-coherentOX-modulesF . Then any
such module is associated to a ﬁnite A-module.
Proof of Lemma 6. Let U = (U1,U 2) with U1 =X(f ) and U2 =X(f −1). Due to

If ϕ : X −→ Y is a morphism of rigid K-spaces and F anOX-module,
we can construct its direct image ϕ∗F . In terms of abelian groups, the latter
sheaf associates to any admissible open subspace V ⊂ Y the abelian group
F (ϕ−1(V )). Clearly, F (ϕ−1(V )) is an OX(ϕ−1(V ))-module and, via the mor-
phism ϕ∗
V :OY (V ) −→ OX(ϕ−1(V )), also an OY (V )-module. Thereby the
sheaf ϕ∗F inherits the structure of an OY -module. The picture is quite sim-
ple for associated modules on aﬃnoid K-spaces. So assume that ϕ : X−→Y
is a morphism of aﬃnoid K-spaces, say X = Sp A and Y = Sp B, and let
F = M⊗AOX for some A-module M. Then the deﬁniton shows that ϕ∗F
coincides with the OY -module associated to M viewed as a B-module via the
morphism ϕ∗
Y : B−→A. In particular, if ϕ is ﬁnite in the sense that A is a
ﬁnite B-module via ϕ∗
Y , it follows that the direct image ϕ∗F is coherent if the
same is true for F . The latter statement is more generally true for so-called
proper morphisms of rigid K-spaces, as we will explain later.
Considering a morphism ϕ : X−→Y of rigid K-spaces again, we may view
ϕ∗ as a functor from OX-modules toOY -modules, a functor which is easily seen
to be left-exact. There is a so-called left-adjoint ϕ∗ of ϕ∗ which is right-exact.
Given an OY -moduleE, the OX-module ϕ∗E is uniquely characterized (up to
canonical isomorphism) by the equation
HomOX (ϕ∗E,F ) = Hom OY (E,ϕ ∗F ),
which is supposed to be functorial in F varying over all OX-modules. It is
called the inverse image ofE. Of course, one has to show that an OX-module
ϕ∗E satisfying these equations really exists. There is a genera l procedure for
showing the existence, which we will not explain at this plac e. We just look at
the special case, where X andY are aﬃnoid, say X = SpA andY = SpB, and
whereE is associated to a B-module N. In this situation, it is easy to see that
theOX-module associated to N⊗BA satisﬁes the above equations and, hence,
must coincide with ϕ∗E.
=====PAGE 101=====
1.15 Grothendieck cohomology 99
1.15 Grothendieck cohomology
In the present section we will be concerned with OX-modules on rigid K-spaces
X. As usual, the cohomology of such modules is deﬁned via deriv ed functors.
The functors we want to consider are the section functor
Γ (X,·) :F↦−→Γ (X,F ) =F (X),
which associates to any OX-moduleF the group of its global sections F (X)
and, for a morphism of rigid K-spaces ϕ : X−→Y , the direct image functor
ϕ∗ :F↦−→ϕ∗F,
which associates to any OX-moduleF its direct image ϕ∗F . Both functors are
left-exact and, to deﬁne their right-derived functors, we n eed injective resolu-
tions. For shortness, let us write C for the category of OX-modules.
Deﬁnition 1. An object F∈ C is called injective if the functor Hom(·,F ) is
exact; i. e., if for each short exact sequence
0−→E ′−→E−→E ′′−→ 0
in C also the sequence
0−→ Hom(E ′′,F )−→ Hom(E,F )−→ Hom(E ′,F )−→ 0
is exact .
As Hom(·,F ) is left-exact, the sequence
0−→ Hom(E ′′,F )−→ Hom(E,F )−→ Hom(E ′,F )
will always be exact, and we see that F is injective if and only if for any
monomorphismE ′֒E and any morphism E ′−→F , the latter admits a (not
necessarily unique) extension E−→F . Without proof we will use:
Proposition 2. The category C ofOX-modules on a rigid K-spaceX contains
enough injectives ; i. e., for each object F∈ C there is a monomorphism F֒I
into an injective object I∈ C.
The assertion of Proposition 2 is true for quite general cate gories C; cf.
Grothendieck, Sur quelques points d’alg` ebre homologique, Tˆ ohoku Math. J. 9,
119–221 (1957), Thm. 1.10.1.
Corollary 3. Any object F ∈C admits an injective resolution ; i. e., there is
an exact sequence

To compute higher cohomology groups via ˇCech cohomology, one needs special

Theorem 5. Let U be an admissible covering of a rigid K-spaceX and letF be
anOX-module. Assume Hq(U,F ) = 0 for q >0 and U any ﬁnite intersection
of sets in U. Then the canonical map
Hq(U,F )−→Hq(X,F )

102 1. Classical Rigid Geometry
is bijective for all q≥ 0.
Theorem 6. Let X be a rigid K-space,F anOX-module, and S a system of
admissible open subsets of X satisfying the following conditions :
(i) The intersection of two sets in S is in S again.
(ii) Each admissible covering of an admissible open subset of X admits an
admissible reﬁnement consisting of sets in S.
(iii) ˇHq(U,F ) = 0 for q >0 and U∈ S.
Then the canonical homomorphism
ˇHq(X,F )−→Hq(X,F )
is bijective for q≥ 0.
For example, let us look at an aﬃnoid K-spaceX and let S be the system
of all aﬃnoid subdomains of X. Then the conditions of Theorem 6 are satisﬁed
for the structure sheaf F =OX or for anyOX-module which is associated to an
OX(X)-module; for condition (iii), see Tate’s Acyclicity Theor em 1.9/10 and
Corollary 1.9/11. Thus, we can conclude:
Corollary 7. Let X be an aﬃnoid K-space. Then
Hq(X,OX) = 0 for q >0.
The same is true for any OX-moduleF in place of OX which is associated to
anOX(X)-module.
1.16 The Proper Mapping Theorem
To state the Proper Mapping Theorem, we need to introduce pro per morphisms
of rigid K-spaces. To do this, we start with closed immersions and sepa rated
morphisms.
Deﬁnition 1. A morphism of rigid K-spaces ϕ : X−→Y is called a closed
immersion if there exists an admissible aﬃnoid covering (Vj)j∈J of Y such that,
for all j∈J, the induced morphism ϕj : ϕ−1(Vj)−→Vj is a closed immersion
of aﬃnoid K-spaces. The latter means that ϕj is a morphism of aﬃnoid spaces ,
say ϕ−1(Vj) = Sp Aj and Vj = SpBj, and that the corresponding morphism of
aﬃnoid K-algebras Bj−→Aj is an epimorphism .
If ϕ : X −→Y is a closed immersion, we can view ϕ∗OX as a coherent
OY -module in the sense of 1.14/3. Using Kiehl’s Theorem 1.14/4 in conjunction
with 1.14/1, one can show that the condition in Deﬁnition 1 is independent of
the chosen admissible aﬃnoid covering ( Vj)j∈J. In fact, a morphism of aﬃnoid
K-spaces SpA−→ SpB is a closed immersion if and only if the corresponding
morphism B−→A is an epimorphism.
=====PAGE 105=====
1.16 The Proper Mapping Theorem 103
Deﬁnition 2. (i) A rigid K-space X is called quasi-compact if it admits a
ﬁnite admissible aﬃnoid cover . A morphism of rigid K-spaces ϕ : X−→Y is
called quasi-compact if for each quasi-compact open subspace Y ′⊂Y its inverse
image ϕ−1(Y ′) is quasi-compact.
(ii) A morphism of rigid K-spaces ϕ : X−→Y is called separated ( resp.
quasi-separated) if the diagonal morphism ∆ : X−→X×Y X is a closed im-
mersion (resp. a quasi-compact morphism ).
(iii) A rigid K-space X is called separated ( resp. quasi-separated) if the
structural morphism X−→ SpK is separated (resp. quasi-separated).
Of course, any separated morphism of rigid K-spaces is quasi-separated,
since closed immersions are quasi-compact. As in algebraic geometry, one shows:
Proposition 3. Each morphism of aﬃnoid K-spaces ϕ : Sp A−→ SpB is
separated.
Proposition 4. Let ϕ : X−→Y be a separated (resp. quasi-separated) mor-
phism of rigid K-spaces and assume that Y is aﬃnoid . Then, for any open
aﬃnoid subspaces U,V ⊂ X, the intersection U∩V is aﬃnoid (resp. quasi-
compact).
In algebraic geometry, one knows for a morphism of schemes ϕ : X−→Y
that the diagonal morphism ∆ : X −→ X×Y X is always a locally closed
immersion. Furthermore, ∆ is a closed immersion and, hence, ϕ is separated,
if and only if the image of ∆ is closed in X×Y X. In rigid analytic geometry
the diagonal morphism ∆ : X−→X×Y X is still a locally closed immersion,
but the characterization of separated morphisms is a bit mor e complicated; see
[BGR], 9.6.1/7 in conjunction with [BGR], 9.6.1/3:
Proposition 5. A morphism of rigid K-spacesϕ : X−→Y is separated if and
only if the following hold :
(i) ϕ is quasi-separated.
(ii) The image of the diagonal morphism ∆ : X −→X×Y X is a closed
analytic subset in X×Y X; i. e., locally on open aﬃnoid parts W⊂X×Y X,
it is a Zariski closed subset of W .
Considering a rigidK-spaceY as a base space, a morphism of rigid K-spaces
X−→Y is quite often referred to as a rigid Y -space. We need to introduce a
notion of relative compactness over such a base Y .
Deﬁnition 6. LetX be a rigid Y -space, where the base space Y is aﬃnoid , and
let U⊂U ′⊂X be open aﬃnoid subspaces. We say that U is relatively compact
in U ′ and write U ⋐Y U ′ if there exist aﬃnoid generators f1,...,f r of OX(U ′)
over OY (Y ) ( in the sense that the structural morphism OY (Y )−→O X(U ′)
extends to an epimorphism OY (Y )⟨ζ1,...,ζ r⟩−→OX(U ′) mapping ζi to fi)
=====PAGE 106=====
104 1. Classical Rigid Geometry
such that
U⊂{x∈U ′ ;|fi(x)|< 1}
or, in equivalent terms , such that there is an ε∈
√
|K ∗|, 0<ε< 1, satisfying
U⊂U ′(ε−1f1,...,ε −1fr).
The notion of relative compactness behaves in a quite reason able way:
Lemma 7. Let X1,X 2 be aﬃnoid spaces over an aﬃnoid K-space Y and let
Ui⊂Xi, i = 1, 2, be aﬃnoid subdomains . Then:
(i) U1 ⋐Y X1 =⇒ U1×Y X2 ⋐Y X1×Y X2.
(ii) Ui ⋐Y Xi, i = 1, 2, =⇒ U1×Y U2 ⋐Y X1×Y X2.
(iii) Ui ⋐Y Xi,i = 1, 2, =⇒ U1∩U2 ⋐Y X1∩X2 if X1,X 2 are open subspaces
of some rigid K-spaceX over Y and the morphism X−→Y is separated.
Now we can deﬁne proper morphisms of rigid K-spaces.
Deﬁnition 8. A morphism of rigid K-spaces ϕ : X−→Y , or X as a rigid
Y -space, is called proper if the following hold :
(i) ϕ is separated.
(ii) There exist an admissible aﬃnoid covering (Yi)i∈I of Y and for each
i∈I two ﬁnite admissible aﬃnoid coverings (Xij)j=1...ni, (X ′
ij)j=1...ni of ϕ−1(Yi)
such that Xij ⋐Yi X ′
ij for all i and j.
It can easily be shown that properness, just like separatedn ess, behaves well
with respect to base change on Y and with respect to ﬁbred products over Y ; cf.
Lemma 7. However, it seems to be quite diﬃcult to show that the composition
of two proper morphisms is proper again.
Of course, ﬁnite morphisms of rigid K-spaces are examples of proper mor-
phisms. Furthermore the projective space Pn
K, viewed as the rigid analytiﬁcation
of the corresponding K-scheme, is a prototype of a proper rigid K-space. More
gnerally, if ϕ : X −→ Y is a morphims of K-schemes of locally ﬁnite type,
one can show that the corresponding rigid analytiﬁcation ϕrig : X rig−→Y rig
is proper if and only if ϕ is proper in the sense of algebraic geometry; see
U. K ¨opf, ¨Uber eigentliche Familien algebraischer Variet ¨aten ¨uber aﬃnoiden
R¨aumen, Schriftenr. Math. Inst. Univ. M ¨unster, 2. Serie, Heft 7 (1974); Satz
2.16.
We want to present now Kiehl’s version of the Proper Mapping T heorem
(see R. Kiehl, Der Endlichkeitssatz f ¨ur eigentliche Abbildungen in der nichtar-
chimedischen Funktionentheorie, Inv. math. 2, 191–214 (19 67)), as well as some
of its applications. The proof of this theorem will be postpo ned until the next
section.
=====PAGE 107=====
1.16 The Proper Mapping Theorem 105
Theorem 9 (Kiehl). Let ϕ : X−→Y be a proper morphism of rigid K-spaces
andF a coherent OX-module. Then the higher direct images Rqϕ∗(F ), q≥ 0,
are coherentOY -modules.
A basic lemma, which has to be established on the way, is the fo llowing one:
Lemma 10. If, in the situation of Theorem 9, Y is aﬃnoid , say Y = Sp B,
and if Y ′ = SpB′⊂Y is an aﬃnoid subdomain , then
Γ (Y ′,Rqϕ∗(F )) = Hq(ϕ−1(Y ′),F ) = Hq(X,F )⊗BB′, q ≥ 0.
There is a lot of applications of the Proper Mapping Theorem a nd, before
concluding this section, we want to discuss some of them. Let ϕ : X−→Y be a
proper morphism of rigid K-spaces. Then, for any closed analytic subset A⊂X
(i. e., locally on open aﬃnoid parts of X, one requires that A is Zariski closed
in X), the image ϕ(A) is a closed analytic subset of Y . Furthermore, there is
the so-called Stein factorization of ϕ: The coherent OY -module ϕ∗(OX) gives
rise to a rigid K-space Y ′ which is ﬁnite over Y . Thus, ϕ splits into a proper
morphism X−→Y ′ with connected ﬁbres and a ﬁnite morphism Y ′−→Y .
Finally, we want to present the resulting theorems which app ly to the
GAGA-functor, dealt with in Sect. 1.13. Note that, for a K-scheme of locally
ﬁnite type X, anyOX-moduleF gives rise to an OXrig-moduleF rig on the rigid
analytiﬁcation X rig of X, and one can show that F rig is coherent if and only if
the same is true for F .
Theorem 11. Let X be a proper K-scheme and F a coherent OX-module.
Then the canonical maps
Hq(X,F )−→Hq(X rig,F rig), q ≥ 0,
are isomorphisms .
Theorem 12. Let X be a proper K-scheme and F,G coherentOX-modules.
Then the canonical map
HomOX (F,G)−→ HomOXrig (F rig,Grig)
is an isomorphism .
Theorem 13. Let X be a proper K-scheme and F ′ a coherent OXrig -module.
Then there is a coherent OX-moduleF satisfyingF rig =F ′; furthermore,F is
unique up to canonical isomorphism .
It should be mentioned that the last 3 theorems generalize to the relative
GAGA-functor, where one works over an aﬃnoid K-algebra as base instead of
K. For details, see U. K ¨opf, ¨Uber eigentliche Familien algebraischer Variet ¨aten
=====PAGE 108=====
106 1. Classical Rigid Geometry
¨uber aﬃnoiden R ¨aumen, Schriftenr. Math. Inst. Univ. M ¨unster, 2. Serie, Heft
7 (1974).
One may apply Theorem 13 to the case where X equals the projective
n-space Pn
K and where F ′ is a coherent ideal I ′⊂O Xrig . As the zero sets of
such coherent ideals are precisely the closed analytic subs ets of X rig, we obtain
the analogue of Chow’s Theorem, namely that each analytic su bset of Pn,rig
K is
algebraic.
1.17 Proof of the Proper Mapping Theorem
In this section we will prove Kiehl’s Theorem 1.16/9 which st ates that all higher
direct images of a coherent sheaf under a proper morphism are coherent again.
To give a short preview on the method we will use, consider a pr oper mor-
phism of rigid K-spaces ϕ : X−→Y , where Y is aﬃnoid, and assume that the
following (slightly stronger) condition for ϕ is satisﬁed:
(†) There exist two ﬁnite admissible aﬃnoid coverings U = (Ui)i=1,...,s and
V = (Vi)i=1,...,s of X such that Vi ⋐Y Ui for all i.
Note that a separated morphism ϕ is proper if and only if there is an
admissible aﬃnoid covering of Y such that condition ( †) is satisﬁed for the
inverse images of the members of this covering.
Now letF be a coherentOX-module. As a main step of proof, we will show
thatHq(X,F ) is a ﬁnite module over B =OY (Y ) for all q≥ 0. Applying 1.15/5
in conjunction with 1.15/7, we may look at ˇCech cohomology and use the fact
that the canonical morphisms

subtracting rq, the resulting map fq, although not necessarily surjective any

Deﬁnition 1. A continuous B-linear homomorphism f : M −→N is called
completely continuous, if it is the limit of a sequence (fi)i∈N of continuous
B-linear homomorphisms such that im(fi) is a ﬁnite B-module for all i∈ N.
Furthermore, if there is an element c∈ R−{ 0} such that , for all i∈ N, the
B◦-module cfi(M ◦) is contained in a ﬁnite B◦-submodule of N ◦, which may
depend on i, then f is called strictly completely continuous.
We want to give a basic example of a strictly completely conti nuous homo-
morphism.
Proposition 2. Let f : B⟨ζ⟩ −→A be a morphism of aﬃnoid K-algebras,
where ζ = (ζ1,...,ζ n) is a system of variables . Consider on B⟨ζ⟩the Gauß

|·|such that f|B : B−→A is contractive. Then, if |f (ζi)|sup < 1 for all i, the

B-modules.

We start the approximation process alluded to above by estab lishing a The-
orem of L. Schwarz.
Theorem 3. Let f,g : M −→N be continuous homomorphisms of complete
normed B-modules where, as above,B is an aﬃnoid K-algebra equipped with a
certain residue norm . Assume that
(i) f is surjective , and
(ii) g is completely continuous .
Then the image im(f +g) is closed in N, and the cokernel N/ im(f +g) is
a ﬁnite B-module.
Proof. We can view f as a continuous linear map between K-Banach spaces.
Thus, by Banach’s Theorem (see Bourbaki, Espaces vectoriel s topologiques,
Chap. I), f is open, and there exists a constant t∈K ∗ such that tN ◦⊂f (M ◦).
In other words, replacing t byct for some c∈K ∗ with|c|< 1, we see that for
anyy∈N, there is some x∈M satisfying
f (x) = y and |x|≤| t|−1|y|.
Now consider the special case, where |g| =α|t| for some α< 1. We claim that,
under such an assumption, f +g is still surjective. Indeed, given y∈N−{ 0},
we can pick x∈M as before with f (x) = y,|x|≤| t|−1|y|, and write
(f +g)(x) = y +y′,
where y′ =g(x)∈N satisﬁes|y′|≤ α|y|. Then, proceeding with y′ in the same
way as we did with y, an iteration argument in combination with a limit process
shows that, indeed, f +g is surjective.
To deal with the general case, we use the fact that g is completely continuous
and, hence, can be uniformly approximated by an inﬁnite sum o f continuous
B-linear maps having module-ﬁnite image. By the just conside red special case,
we may assume that this sum is, in fact, ﬁnite and, hence, that g has module-
ﬁnite image. Then M/ kerg may be viewed as a ﬁnite B-module, and we can
consider the commutative diagram
M

where the lower row is induced from f and, hence, all arrows are epimorphisms.
It follows that N/f (kerg) is a ﬁnite B-module and, since
f (kerg) = (f +g)(kerg)⊂ (f +g)(M),
that the same is true for N/(f +g)(M).
=====PAGE 111=====
1.17 Proof of the Proper Mapping Theorem 109
To show that im(f +g) is closed in M, observe that ker g is closed in M and
that we can provide M/ kerg with the canonical residue norm derived from the
norm of M. Using the assertion of 1.3/10, one can show that any submodu le
of such a ﬁnite B-module is closed. In particular, ker f is closed, and we can
consider the residue norm via f on N/f (kerg). On the other hand, we can
assume, due to Banach’s Theorem (see above), that the norm of N coincides
with the residue norm via f . Then it follows that the norm on N/f (kerg)
coincides with the residue norm via N−→N/f (kerg). In particular, the latter
map is continuous. Since ( f +g)(M) can be interpreted as the inverse of a
submodule of N/f (kerg), and since any such submodule is closed, as we have
seen, it follows that ( f +g)(M) is closed in N. □
Recalling the maps
fq +rq : Cq−1(V,F )⊕Zq(U,F )−→Zq(V,F ), q ≥ 0,
as introduced at the beginning of the section, we would like t o apply Theorem 3
to the maps f =fq +rq and g =−rq, for all q. Certainly, f is surjective then,
but we do not know if g will be completely continuous. Basing our information
about complete continuity upon the example given in Proposi tion 2, we need a
slight generalization of the Theorem of Schwarz, as follows :
Theorem 4. Let f,g : M −→N be continuous homomorphisms of complete
normed B-modules where, as above,B is an aﬃnoid K-algebra equipped with a
certain residue norm . Assume that
(i) f is surjective , and
(ii) g is part of a sequence M♭ p

g

phisms of complete normed B-modules, where p is an epimorphism and j iden-
tiﬁes N with a closed submodule of N♯, and where the composed map j◦g◦p
is strictly completely continuous .
Then the image im(f +g) is closed in N, and the cokernel N/ im(f +g) is
a ﬁnite B-module.
The proof of the Theorem requires some preparations.
Lemma 5. Let E be a ﬁnite B◦-module and E′⊂ E a B◦-submodule. Then,
for any constant 0< α <1, there is a ﬁnite B◦-submodule E′′⊂E′ such that
aE′⊂E′′ for all a∈R with|a|≤ α.
Proof. If π : Tn = K⟨ζ⟩ −→B with a system of variables ζ = ( ζ1,...,ζ n)

coeﬃcients in R. If R is a discrete valuation ring, R⟨ζ⟩is noetherian by EGA
Ineu, Chap. 0, Prop. 7.5.2. Thus we are done in this case, since E is noetherian
then.

After these preparations, the proof of Theorem 4 is easy to achieve. First
observe that the epimorphism p : M♭−→M is not really relevant, since compo-
sition with such a continuous (and, hence, by Banach’s Theor em, open) B-linear
map p does not change the image of f +g. Assume ﬁrst that M♭ is topolog-
ically free, as needed in Lemma 6. Then, with the help of this l emma, the
assertion follows from Theorem 3. If M♭ is not topologically free, we can com-
pose our situation with a continuous B-linear epimorphism M♭♭−→M♭ and
apply the reasoning used before. To obtain such an epimorphi sm, consider a
bounded generating system ( xλ)λ∈Λ forM♭ as ordinary B-module, and let M♭♭
be the completion of B(Λ), the free B-module generated by Λ, with respect to
the canonical maximum norm. Then M♭♭ is topologically free, and there is a
canonical continuous epimorphism M♭♭−→M♭, as required. □
Going back to Kiehl’s Theorem 1.16/9, we consider a proper mo rphism of

=====PAGE 121=====
2. Formal Geometry
2.1 Adic rings
In classical rigid geometry, one works over a ﬁeldK, carrying a non-Archimedean
absolute value. The strategy of the formal approach to rigid geometry is to
replace K by its valuation ring R. For example, one starts with R-algebras
R⟨ζ1,...,ζ n⟩of strictly convergent power series having coeﬃcients in R, and
considers quotients with respect to ﬁnitely generated idea ls. This way one ob-
tains R-algebras which may be viewed as R-models of aﬃnoid K-algebras. In
fact, taking the generic ﬁbre of such an R-model, i. e., tensoring it with K over
R, yields an aﬃnoid K-algebra.
We want to look at rings R which are more general than just valuation
rings as occurring above. Let us call a ring R together with a topology on it
a topological ring if addition and multiplication on R yield continuous maps
R×R−→R; of course, R×R is endowed with the product topology. There
is a fundamental example. Let R be an arbitrary ring (commutative, and with
identity) and a⊂ R an ideal. There is a unique topology on R making it a
topological ring such that all ideals an,n∈ N, form a basis of neighborhoods of
0 in R. Namely, call a subset U⊂R open if for each x∈U there is an n∈ N
such that x + an⊂ U. The resulting topology is called the a-adic topology on
R. (In Grothendieck’s terminology [EGA], this is the a-preadic topology; the
latter is called adic if it is separated and complete.) Note that all ideals an are
open and, being subgroups of R, also closed in R. A topological ring R is called
an adic ring if its topology coincides with the a-adic one for some ideal a⊂R.
Any such ideal a is called an ideal of deﬁnition .
There are similar notions for modules. A module M over a topological ring
R, together with a topology on M, is called a topologicalR-module if addition
M×M−→M and multiplication R×M−→M are continuous. Furthermore,
for anyR-moduleM and an ideal a⊂R, we can deﬁne the a-adic topology onM:
we endow R with its a-adic topology as described above and consider on M the
unique topology making it a topological R-module, for which the submodules
anM, n∈ N form a basis of neighborhoods. Again, all these submodules a re
open and closed in M.
Remark 1. Consider a ring R and an R-module M with a-adic topologies for
some ideal a⊂R.
=====PAGE 122=====
120 2. Formal Geometry
(i) R is separated (i. e., Hausdorﬀ ) if and only if ⋂ ∞
n=0 an = 0.
(ii) M is separated if and only if ⋂ ∞
n=0 anM = 0.
Proof. We have ⋂ ∞
n=0 an = 0 if and only if, for each x∈ R−{ 0}, there is an
n∈ N such that x⁄∈an. As an is open and closed in R, assertion (i) follows by
a translation argument; (ii) is derived in the same way. □
For noetherian rings, adic topologies have nice properties . Let us recall the
basic facts from Commutative Algebra.

Apart from noetherian rings we will look at valuation rings . Recall that an
integral domain R with ﬁeld of fractions K is called a valuation ring if for any
x∈K we have x∈R or x−1∈R.
Remark 6. Let R be a valuation ring .
(i) Any ﬁnitely generated ideal in R is principal .
(ii) For two ideals a, b⊂R we have a⊂ b or b⊂ a.
In particular, R is a local ring .
Proof. For two elements a,b ∈ R we must have ab−1∈ R or a−1b∈ R; i. e., b
divides a or a dvides b in R. This shows (i). To verify (ii) assume a⁄⊂b and
b⁄⊂a. Then there are elements a∈ a− b and b∈ b− a. If a divides b, we have
=====PAGE 124=====
122 2. Formal Geometry
b∈ a, and if b divides a, we must have a∈ b. However, both is excluded, and
we get a contradiction. □
The length of a maximal chain of prime ideals in a valuation ri ngR is called
the height of R. For example, starting with a non-Archimedean absolute val ue
on a ﬁeld K, the corresponding valuation ring R ={x∈ K ;|x|≤ 1} is of
height 1. However, there are valuation rings of higher, even inﬁnite height. For
any prime ideal p of a valuation ring R, the localization Rp is a valuation ring
again. In fact, the map p↦−→Rp deﬁnes a bijection between prime ideals of R
and intermediate rings between R and its ﬁeld of fractions K. Let us mention
without proof that the concept of valuations and absolute va lues carries over to
the ﬁeld of fractions of valuation rings of arbitrary height . Then Γ = K ∗/R∗,
with its attached canonical ordering, serves as the value gr oup of K ∗, and the
canonical maps ν : K−→Γ∪{∞} , resp.|·|: K−→Γ∪{ 0} are viewed as a
valuation, resp. an absolute value on K. The valuation ring R is of height 1 if
and only if Γ , together with its ordering, can be realized as a subgroup of the
additive group R, resp. the multiplicative group R>0. In precisely these cases,
the valuation corresponds to a valuation or a non-Archimede an absolute value
on K, as we have deﬁned them in Sect. 1.1.
Any valuation ring R may be viewed as a topological ring by taking the
system of its non-zero ideals as a basis of neighborhoods of 0 . Then R is au-
tomatically separated, unless R is a ﬁeld. We are only interested in valuation
rings which are adic.
Remark 7. Let R be a valuation ring and assume that R is not a ﬁeld . Then
the following are equivalent :
(i) R is adic with a ﬁnitely generated ideal of deﬁnition .
(ii) There exists a minimal non-trivial prime ideal p⊂R.
If the conditions are satisﬁed , the topology of R coincides with the t-adic
one for any non-zero element t∈ p.
Proof. To begin, let us show that, for any non-unit t∈R, the ideal rad( t)⊂R
is prime. To verify this, consider elements a,b ∈ R satisfying ab ∈ rad(t),
and look at the ideals rad( a) and rad( b). Using Remark 6(ii), we may assume
rad(a) ⊂ rad(b). Then b divides some power of a, and ab ∈ rad(t) implies
a∈ rad(t). Thus, rad(t) is prime.
Now assume condition (i). Due to Remark 6(i), the topology of R coincides
with the t-adic one for some non-zero element t∈R. As any non-zero ideal of
R must contain a power of t, we see that any non-zero prime ideal in R will
contain the ideal rad( t). The latter is prime by what we have shown and, thus,
it is minimal among all non-zero prime ideals in R.
Conversely, assume (ii); i. e., that there is a minimal non-z ero prime ideal
p⊂ R. Let t∈ p be a non-zero element and let a⊂ R be any non-zero ideal.
We have to show that a contains a power of t. To do this, we may assume
that a is principal, say a = (a). Comparing rad(t) with rad(a), both ideals are
=====PAGE 125=====
2.1 Adic rings 123
prime. Thus, we must have rad( t)⊂ rad(a), and it follows that a power of t is
contained in (a) = a. □
Now let us turn to general adic rings again; let R be a such a ring with
a⊂ R as ideal of deﬁnition. As the a-adic topology on R is invariant under
translation, convergence in R can be deﬁned in a natural way. We say that a
sequence cν∈R converges to an element c∈ R if, for each n∈ N, there is an
integerν0∈ N such thatcν−c∈ an for allν≥ν0. Similarly,cν is called a Cauchy
sequence if, for each n∈ N, there is an integer ν0∈ N such that cν−cν′∈ an
for all ν,ν ′≥ν0. As usual, R is called complete if every Cauchy sequence in R
is convergent. A separated completion ˆR of R can be constructed by dividing
the ring of all Cauchy sequences in R by the ideal of all zero sequences.
For adic rings there is a nice description of completions. Na mely, consider
the projective system
... −→R/an−→... −→R/a2−→R/a1−→ 0,
where a is an ideal of deﬁnition of R. Then its projective limit
ˆR = lim←−n

is seen to be the (separated) completion of R. The topology on this limit is the
coarsest one such that all canonical projections πn : ˆR−→R/an are continuous,
whereR/an carries the discrete topology (the one for which all subsets ofR/an
are open). Thus, a subset of ˆR is open if and only if it is a union of certain ﬁbres
of the πn, with varying n, and it follows that a basis of neighborhoods of 0 ∈ ˆR
is given by the ideals ker πn⊂ ˆR. We claim that ker πn equals the closure of an
in ˆR. In fact, ker πn is closed in ˆR by the deﬁnition of the topology on ˆR, and
an is dense in ker πn, as for any f∈ kerπn and any m∈ N there is an element
fm∈ an such that f−fm∈ kerπm+n. Just choose fm∈R as a representative of
πm+n(f )∈R/am+n. If the ideal a⊂R is ﬁnitely generated, say a = (a1,...,a r),
it is easy to see that its closure in ˆR equals a ˆR. First, a is clearly dense in a ˆR,
since a ˆR⊂ kerπ1 and a is dense in ker π1. Furthermore, if f = ∑ ∞

2.2 Formal schemes
Formal schemes are locally topologically ringed spaces, wh ere all occurring rings
have to be viewed as objects of the category of topological ri ngs. Just as ordinary
schemes, they are built from local aﬃne parts. To deﬁne such a ﬃne formal
schemes, consider an adic ring A; from now on, we will always assume that adic
rings are complete and separated. Let a be an ideal of deﬁnition of A. We denote
by SpfA the set of all open prime ideals p⊂A. As a prime ideal in A is open if
and only if it contains some power of a and, hence, a itself, we see that Spf A is
canonically identiﬁed with the closed subset Spec A/a⊂ SpecA, for any ideal
of deﬁnition a of A.
This way the Zariski topology on Spec A induces a topology on Spf A. As
usual, let D(f ) for f∈A be the open subset in Spf A wheref does not vanish.
Then

covering (D(fi))i of D(f ), the diagram

showing that mx is a maximal ideal in Ox containing jxOx.
To see that mx is the only maximal ideal in Ox, we show that Ox− mx
consists of units. Thus, ﬁx an element gx∈O x− mx, say represented by an
elementg∈A⟨f −1⟩for some f∈A satisfying f⁄∈jx. Then g cannot belong to
jx⟨f −1⟩and, hence, using a⊂ jx, its residue class g∈A/a⌈⌊f −1⌉⌋cannot belong
to jxA/a⌈⌊f −1⌉⌋. Multiplying g by a suitable power of f , we can even assume
that g belongs to A/a and, thus, admits a representative g′∈A, where g′⁄∈jx.
Thenfg ′∈A− jx, and we claim that the image of g is invertible in A⟨(fg ′)−1⟩,
which implies that it is invertible in Ox, too. To see this, consider the equation
g =g′(1−d) in A⟨(fg ′)−1⟩withd = 1−g′−1g, where we have written g,g ′ again
for the corresponding images in A⟨(fg ′)−1⟩. Thus, in order to show that g is
invertible inA⟨(fg ′)−1⟩, we need to know that 1−d is invertible. However, using
the geometric series, the latter is clear since dn is a zero sequence in A⟨(fg ′)−1⟩,
due to the fact that the image of d is trivial in A/a⌈⌊(fg ′)−1⌉⌋and, hence, the
image of dn is trivial in A/an⌈⌊(fg ′)−1⌉⌋for all n∈ N.
Finally, if a is ﬁnitely generated, an argument as used in the proof of 2.1/ 8
shows jx⟨f −1⟩= jxA⟨f −1⟩and, hence, mx = jxOx. □
Deﬁnition 2. For an adic ring A with ideal of deﬁnition a⊂A, set X = Spf A
and letOX be the sheaf of topological rings we have constructed above . Then the
locally ringed space (X,OX) ( where “ringed” has to be understood in the sense
of topological rings ) is called the aﬃne formal scheme of A. It is denoted by
SpfA, again.
There is a slight problem with this deﬁnition. If we consider an aﬃne formal
schemeX = SpfA and a basic open subset U =D(f )⊂ SpfA for some f∈A,
it makes sense to say that ( U,OX|U ) is isomorphic to the aﬃne formal scheme
SpfA⟨f −1⟩, although we do not know in general if A⟨f −1⟩, which is deﬁned
as the a-adic completion of A⌈⌊f −1⌉⌋, is an adic ring again. Due to 2.1/8, no
problems arise, when a is ﬁnitely generated, since then the topology of A⟨f −1⟩
coincides with the a-adic one.
When such a ﬁniteness condition is to be avoided, aﬃne formal schemes
SpfA should be constructed for slightly more general topologica l rings than just
adic ones. One needs that A is an admissible ring in the sense of Grothendieck.
This means that:
(i) A is linearly topologized; i. e., there is a basis of neighborh oods (Iλ)λ∈Λ
of 0 consisting of ideals in A; such ideals are automatically open.
(ii) A has an ideal of deﬁnition; i. e., there is an open ideal a⊂A such that
an tends to zero in the sense that, for each neighborhood U⊂A of 0, there is
an n∈ N satisfying an⊂ U. (This does not necessarily imply that an is open
for n> 1.)
(iii) A is separated and complete.
If A is an admissible ring with a family of ideals ( Iλ)λ∈Λ forming a basis of
neighborhoods of 0, then A = lim←−λA/Iλ, and each Iλ is an ideal of deﬁnition in
=====PAGE 130=====
128 2. Formal Geometry
A. Admissible rings can be dealt with in essentially the same w ay as adic ones,
just replacing the system of powers ( an)n∈N for an ideal of deﬁnition a⊂A by
the system (Iλ)λ∈Λ. However, for our purposes, it will be enough to restrict to
complete and separated adic rings, as later we will always su ppose that there is
an ideal of deﬁnition which is ﬁnitely generated.
When working with aﬃne formal schemes, morphisms are, of cou rse, meant
in the sense of morphisms of locally topologically ringed sp aces. So all inherent
ring homomorphisms are supposed to be continuous. Just as in the scheme case
or in the case of aﬃnoid K-spaces, one shows that morphisms of locally topo-
logically ringed spaces Spf A−→ SpfB correspond bijectively to continuous
homomorphisms B−→A.
Deﬁnition 3. A formal scheme is a locally topologically ringed space (X,OX)
such that each point x∈X admits an open neighborhood U where (U,OX|U ) is
isomorphic to an aﬃne formal scheme SpfA, as constructed above .
As usual, global formal schemes can be constructed by gluein g local ones.
In particular, ﬁbre products can be constructed by glueing l ocal aﬃne ones.
Similarly as for schemes or rigid K-spaces, the ﬁbre product of two aﬃne formal
schemes SpfA and Spf B over a third one Spf R is given by Spf(A ˆ⊗RB), where
A ˆ⊗RB = lim←−A/an⊗RB/bn
with ideals of deﬁnition a of A and b of B is the complete tensor product of A
and B over R. The latter is the ( a, b)-adic completion of the ordinary tensor
product A⊗RB. By 2.1/8, A ˆ⊗RB is an adic ring again with ideal of deﬁnition
generated by the image of a⊗R B + A⊗R b, provided a and b are ﬁnitely
generated.
We end this section by mentioning a fundamental example, nam ely the
formal completion of a scheme X along a closed subscheme Y ⊂ X. So Y is
deﬁned by a quasi-coherent idealJ⊂O X. Then consider the sheafOY obtained
from restricting the projective limit lim←−nOX/Jn to Y . It follows that ( Y,OY )
is a locally topologically ringed space, the desired formal completion of X along
Y . Locally, the construction looks as follows: Let X = SpecA and assume that
J is associated to the ideal a⊂A. Then
(Y,OY ) = Spf lim←−n
A/an = Spf ˆA,
where ˆA is the a-adic completion of A. Even more explicitly, assume A =R⌈⌊ζ⌉⌋,
whereζ is a system of n variables,R a complete valuation ring of height 1, and
where a = (t) for some non-unit t∈ R−{ 0}. So X coincides with the aﬃne
n-space An
R, and Y (pointwise) with its special ﬁbre. The formal completion of
X along Y then yields the formal aﬃne n-space Spf R⟨ζ⟩. Writing K =Q(R),
the latter admits the aﬃnoid K-space Sp K⟨ζ⟩= Sp(R⟨ζ⟩⊗RK) as “generic
ﬁbre”, as we will explain later. As a general phenomenon, thi s generic ﬁbre
should be viewed as an open subspace of An,rig

2.3 Algebras of topologically ﬁnite type
Let R be a (complete and separated) adic ring with a ﬁnitely genera ted ideal
of deﬁnition I⊂R. We will assume that R does not have I-torsion; i. e., that
the ideal
(I-torsion)R ={r∈R ; Inr = 0 for some n∈ N}
is trivial, a condition which, apparently, is independent o f the choice ofI. Choos-
ing generators g1,...,g r ofI, we see that R does not have I-torsion if and only
if the canonical map
R−→
r∏
i=1
R⌈⌊g−1
i ⌉⌋
is injective. We will admit only the following two classes of rings:
(V) R is an adic valuation ring with a ﬁnitely generated ideal of de ﬁnition
(which automatically is principal by 2.1/6).
(N) R is a noetherian adic ring with an ideal of deﬁnition I where R does
not have I-torsion.
In the following, let R be of type (V) or (N). As usual, we deﬁne the
R-algebra R⟨ζ1,...,ζ n⟩of restricted power series in the variables ζ1,...,ζ n as
the subalgebra of the R-algebraR⌈⌊⌈⌊ζ1,...,ζ n⌉⌋⌉⌋of formal power series, consisting
of all series ∑
ν∈Nncνζν with coeﬃcients cν∈R constituting a zero sequence in
R. Of course, R⟨ζ1,...,ζ n⟩equals the I-adic completion of the ring of polyno-
mials R⌈⌊ζ1,...,ζ n⌉⌋.
Remark 1. R⟨ζ1,...,ζ n⟩is noetherian if R is of class (N).
Proof. EGA I neu, Chap. 0, Prop. 7.5.2. □
Remark 2. R⟨ζ1,...,ζ n⟩is ﬂat over R.
Proof. A module M over a ring R is ﬂat if and only if, for each ﬁnitely generated
ideal a⊂R, the canonical map a⊗RM−→M is injective. If R is an integral
domain and if any ﬁnitely generated ideal in R is principal, the latter condition
is equivalent to the fact that M does not admit R-torsion. Thus, if R is of class
(V), we see from 2.1/6 that R⟨ζ1,...,ζ n⟩is ﬂat over R.
On the other hand, if R is of class (N), the map R−→R⌈⌊ζ1,...,ζ n⌉⌋is
ﬂat being module-free. Furthermore, the map from R⌈⌊ζ1,...,ζ n⌉⌋into its I-adic
completion is ﬂat by Bourbaki, Alg. Comm., Chap. 3, § 5, no. 4, Cor. of Prop. 3.
□
Having deﬁned restricted power series with coeﬃcients in R, let us introduce
now the analogues of aﬃnoid algebras.
Deﬁnition 3. A topological R-algebra A is called
=====PAGE 132=====
130 2. Formal Geometry
(i) of topologically ﬁnite type if it is isomorphic to an R-algebra of type
R⟨ζ1,...,ζ n⟩/a, endowed with the I-adic topology.
(ii) of topologically ﬁnite presentation if, in addition to (i), a is ﬁnitely
generated,
(iii) admissible if, in addition to (i) and (ii), A does not have I-torsion.
It is a fundamental fact, which will be used extensively in th e sequel, that
an R-algebra of topologically ﬁnite type, which is ﬂat over R, is automatically
of topologically ﬁnite presentation. Properties of this ty pe are proved using the
ﬂattening techniques of Raynaud and Gruson; see Crit` eres d e platitude et de
projectivit´ e. Inv. math. 13, 1–89 (1971), 3.4.6.
Theorem 4 (Raynaud-Gruson). Let A be an R-algebra of topologically ﬁnite
type and M a ﬁnite A-module, which is ﬂat over R. Then M is an A-module
of ﬁnite presentation ; i. e., M is isomorphic to the cokernel of some A-linear
map Ar−→As.
Proof. As an R-algebra of topologically ﬁnite type, A is a quotient of some alge-
bra of restricted power series R⟨ζ1,...,ζ n⟩. Viewing M as a module over such a
power series ring, we may assume that A is of topologically ﬁnite presentation
overR. In the noetherian case (N), nothing has to be shown, since A is noethe-
rian then. If R is an adic valuation ring of type (V), we can choose an element
t generating an ideal of deﬁnition of R. Then A/tA is an R/(t)-algebra of ﬁnite
presentation, and M/tM is a ﬁnite A/tA-module, which is ﬂat over R/(t). It
follows then from the above cited result of Raynaud and Gruso n that M/tM is
an A/tA-module of ﬁnite presentation. Now consider a short exact se quence of
A-modules
0−→N−→As−→M−→ 0.
SinceM is ﬂat over R, the sequence remains exact when tensoring it with R/(t)
overR. Since M/tM is an A/tA-module of ﬁnite presentation, N/tN is a ﬁnite
A/tA-module. But then a standard approximation argument shows t hatN is a
ﬁnite A-module and, hence, that M is an A-module of ﬁnite presentation.
In the most interesting case, where R is an adic valuation ring of height 1,
the Theorem is accessible by more elementary methods. First , one reduces to
the case where A = R⟨ζ⟩, for a ﬁnite system of variables ζ = (ζ1,...,ζ n), as
indicated above. Then, as before, consider a short exact seq uence
0−→N−→ (R⟨ζ⟩)s−→M−→ 0
of R⟨ζ⟩-modules. Since M is ﬂat over R, there is no R-torsion in M and, con-
sequently, looking at the inclusion map ( R⟨ζ⟩)s֒→( R⟨ζ⟩)s⊗RK = Ts
n, where
K is the ﬁeld of fractions of R, we get
N⊗RK∩ (R⟨ζ⟩)s =N.
Applying 1.3/10 to theTn-moduleN⊗RK, we see thatN is a ﬁniteR⟨ζ⟩-module
and, hence, that M is an R⟨ζ⟩-module of ﬁnite presentation. □

2.3 Algebras of topologically ﬁnite type 131
Corollary 5. Let A be an R-algebra of topologically ﬁnite type . If A has no
I-torsion, A is of topologically ﬁnite presentation .
Proof. The assertion is trivial in the noetherian case. So assume t hat R is of
class (V). Interpreting A as a residue algebra R⟨ζ⟩/a with a system of variables
ζ, we can view A as an R⟨ζ⟩-module via the canonical projection R⟨ζ⟩−→A.
If A has no I-torision, it is ﬂat over R and, thus, by Theorem 4, a ﬁnitely
presented R⟨ζ⟩-module. But then a must be ﬁnitely generated so that A is of
topologically ﬁnite presentation. □
Recall that, similarly as in 1.14/2, a module M over a ring A is called
coherent ifM is ﬁnitely generated and if any ﬁnite submodule of M is of ﬁnite
presentation.A itself is called a coherent ring if it is coherent as a module over
itself; i. e., if each ﬁnitely generated ideal a⊂ A is of ﬁnite presentation. One
can show that all members of a short exact sequence of A-modules
0−→M ′−→M−→M ′′−→ 0
are coherent as soon as two of them are.
Corollary 6. Let A be an R-algebra of topologically ﬁnite presentation . Then
A is a coherent ring .
In particular, any A-module of ﬁnite presentation is coherent .
Proof. We may assume thatR is of class (V). Let us ﬁrst consider the case, where
A does not have I-torsion and, hence, is ﬂat over R. Then any ﬁnitely generated
ideal in A is ﬂat over R and, hence, of ﬁnite presentation by Theorem 4. Thus,
A is coherent in this case.
In the general case, we can write A as a quotient R⟨ζ⟩/a with a system
of variables ζ and a ﬁnitely generated ideal a. The algebra R⟨ζ⟩is coherent,
as we have seen. Thus, a is coherent, too, and it follows that A = R⟨ζ⟩/a is
coherent. □
We want to draw some further conclusions from Theorem 4.
Lemma 7. Let A be an R-algebra of topologically ﬁnite type , M a ﬁnite
A-module, and N⊂M a submodule . Then:
(i) If N is saturated in M in the sense that
Nsat ={x∈M ; there is an n∈ N such that Inx⊂N}
coincides with N, then N is ﬁnitely generated .
(ii) The I-adic topology of M restricts to the I-adic topology on N.
Proof. If R is of class (N), assertion (i) is trivial, and assertion (ii) follows from
the lemma of Artin-Rees; cf. 2.1/5. So assume that R is of class (V). If N is
=====PAGE 134=====
132 2. Formal Geometry
saturated in M, the quotient M/N does not admit I-torsion and, hence, is ﬂat
overR, sinceR is a valuation ring. Thus M/N, as a ﬁnite A-module which is ﬂat
overR, is of ﬁnite presentation by Theorem 4, and there is an exact s equence
of A-modules
0−→K−→F−→M/N−→ 0,
whereF is ﬁnite free and K is ﬁnite. As M is ﬁnitely generated, we may assume
that F−→M/N factors through M via an epimorphism F−→M. But then
this map restricts to an epimorphism K−→N, and we see that N is ﬁnitely
generated. This veriﬁes (i).
To verify assertion (ii), we can consider the saturation Nsat⊂M ofN; it is
ﬁnitely generated by (i). Thus, there is an integer n∈ N such that InNsat⊂N,
and we have
Im+nM∩N⊂ImN⊂ImM∩N
for all m∈ N. So we are done. □
Proposition 8. Let A be an R-algebra of topologically ﬁnite type and M a
ﬁnite A-module. Then M is I-adically complete and separated .
Proof. We may replace A by an algebra of restricted power series R⟨ζ⟩and
thereby assume that A is I-adically complete and separated. Then, using
Lemma 7(ii), M is I-adically complete for trivial reasons. To show that it is
also I-adically separated, consider an element x∈ ⋂ ∞
n=0InM, and look at the
submodule N = Ax⊂ M. Using Lemma 7(ii), there is an integer n∈ N such
that N = InM∩N⊂ IN . Hence there is an equation (1 −c)x = 0 for some
c∈I. However, using the geometric series, we see that 1 −c is a unit in R and,
hence, that x must be zero. □
Corollary 9. Any R-algebra of topologically ﬁnite type is I-adically complete
and separated.
In particular, if A is any R-algebra of topologically ﬁnite type, we can
identify A with lim←−nA/InA. To abbreviate, we will write Rn = R/In+1 and
An =A/In+1 =A⊗RRn forn∈ N. Similar notions will be used for R-modules.

over R0.

presentation over Rn for all n∈ N.

2.4 Admissible formal schemes
LetA be anR-algebra which isI-adically complete and separated. We have seen
in 2.3/13 that the condition of A being of topologically ﬁnite type, of topologi-
cally ﬁnite presentation, or admissible, can be tested loca lly on localizations of
type A⟨f −1⟩. This enables us to extend these notions to formal R-schemes.
Deﬁnition 1. Let X be a formal R-scheme.X is called locally of topologically
ﬁnite type ( resp. locally of topologically ﬁnite presentation, resp. admissible) if
there is an open aﬃne covering (Ui)i∈J of X with Ui = Spf Ai where Ai is an
R-algebra of topologically ﬁnite type (resp. of topologically ﬁnite presentation ,
resp. an admissible R-algebra).
As an immediate consequence we get from 2.3/13:
Remark 2. Let A be an R-algebra which is I-adically complete and separated ,
and let X = Spf A be the associated formal R-scheme. Then the following are
equivalent:
=====PAGE 138=====
136 2. Formal Geometry
(i) X is locally of topologically ﬁnite type (resp. locally of topologically ﬁnite
presentation, resp. admissible).
(ii) A is of topologically ﬁnite type (resp. of topologically ﬁnite presentation ,
resp. admissible) as R-algebra.
Similarly as in the scheme case, a formal R-schemeX is called of topologi-
cally ﬁnite type if it is locally of topologically ﬁnite type and quasi-compa ct. It
is called of topologically ﬁnite presentation if it is locally of topologically ﬁnite
presentation, quasi-compact, and quasi-separated. Recal l that X is called quasi-
separated if the diagonal embedding X−→X×RX is quasi-compact. If X is
locally of topologically ﬁnite type, the quasi-separatedn ess of X is automatic if
R is noetherian, since X, as a topological space, is locally noetherian then. The
same is true for R a complete valuation ring of height 1. Namely, in this case,
if A is an R-algebra of topologically ﬁnite type and m is the maximal ideal of
R, then, as a topological space, Spf A coincides with Spec( A⊗R R/m). Since
A⊗RR/m is of ﬁnite type over the ﬁeld R/m, its spectrum Spec( A⊗RR/m) is
a noetherian space.
Let X be a formal R-scheme, which is locally of topologically ﬁnite type,
and let OX be its structure sheaf. Then we can look at the ideal J ⊂ OX
representing the I-torsion of OX, where J (U), for any open subset U ⊂ X,
consists of all sections f ∈O X(U) such that there is an open aﬃne covering
(Uλ)λ∈Λ ofU with the property that each restriction f|Uλ is killed by some power
In of the ideal of deﬁnition I⊂R. It is clear from the deﬁnition that J really
is an ideal sheaf in OX. Note that if U⊂X is an aﬃne open formal subscheme,
sayU = Spf A, then we see from 2.3/13 that
J (U) = (I-torsion)A ={f∈A ; Inf = 0 for some n∈ N}.
In fact, we clearly have ( I-torsion)A⊂J (U), and the quotient A/(I-torsion)A
does not have I-torsion locally on Spf A, due to 2.3/13. We can now replace
the structure sheaf OX by the quotient OX/J and restrict X to the support
Xad ofOX/J . Thereby we get a formal R-scheme Xad which still is locally of
topologically ﬁnite type and whose structure sheaf does not haveI-torsion. Then
Xad is locally of topologically ﬁnite presentation by 2.3/5 and , thus, admissible.
We call Xad the admissible formal R-scheme induced from X.
For a moment, let us look at the classical rigid case, where R consists of a
complete valuation ring of height 1 with ﬁeld of fractions K. To simplify our
terminology, let us assume in the following that all formal R-schemes are at least
locally of topologically ﬁnite type , unless stated otherwise. We want to deﬁne a
functor “rig” from the category of formal R-schemes to the category of rigid
K-spaces, which will be interpreted as associating to a forma l R-schemeX its
generic ﬁbre Xrig. On aﬃne formal R-schemes Spf A this functor is deﬁned by
rig :X = SpfA↦−→Xrig = Sp(A⊗RK),
where we claim thatA⊗RK is an aﬃnoidK-algebra. To justify this claim, we set
S =R−{0} and interpretA⊗RK as the localization S−1A. By our assumption,

A is of topologically ﬁnite type and, thus, isomorphic to a quo tient R⟨ζ⟩/a,
where R⟨ζ⟩is an algebra of restricted power series in ﬁnitely many vari ables
ζ = (ζ1,...,ζ n), and a is an ideal in R⟨ζ⟩. Since A⊗RK =S−1(R⟨ζ⟩)/(a), it is
enough to show that S−1(R⟨ζ⟩) = K⟨ζ⟩. However, the latter is clear by looking
at the canonical inclusions

and by observing that, for any series f = ∑

to the fact that lim cν = 0. Thus A⊗RK really is an aﬃnoid K-algebra and,
for any aﬃne formal R-scheme X = Spf A, the corresponding rigid K-space
Xrig = SpA⊗RK is well-deﬁned.
Next, if ϕ : Spf A −→ SpfB is a morphism of aﬃne formal R-schemes,
we know from Sect. 2.2 that it is induced from a unique R-homomorphism
ϕ∗ : B−→A. Then, by 1.12/2, the corresponding generic ﬁbre

determines a well-deﬁned morphism of aﬃnoid K-spaces

which we deﬁne as the image of ϕ under the functor rig. Furthermore, let us
observe that this functor commutes with complete localizat ion. Namely, for any
R-algebra of topologically ﬁnite type A and any f∈A we get
A⟨f −1⟩⊗RK =⌈⌊A⟨ζ⟩/(1−fζ )⌉⌋⊗RK

⌈⌊A⟨ζ⟩/(1−fζ )⌉⌋⊗RK as a localization of A⟨ζ⟩/(1−fζ ) and by representing A

138 2. Formal Geometry
of the generic ﬁbre associated to X. More generally, it follows that rig maps
any open immersion of aﬃne formal R-schemes Spf A′−→ SpfA to an open
immersion of aﬃnoid K-spaces Sp(A′⊗RK)−→ Sp(A⊗RK).
Now, to extend the functor rig to global formal R-schemes, let us look at
such a schemeX and assume ﬁrst that X is separated and, hence, that the inter-
section of two open aﬃne formal subschemes of X is aﬃne again. Fixing an open
aﬃne covering (Ui)i∈J ofX, all intersections Ui∩Uj are aﬃne again. Hence, we
can glue the generic ﬁbres Ui,rig via the “intersections” ( Ui∩Uj)rig to produce
a global rigid K-space Xrig. It is easily checked that the latter is independent
of the chosen aﬃne open covering ( Ui)i∈J and that any morphism of separated
formalR-schemesX−→Y leads to a canonical morphism Xrig−→Yrig so that
we really get a functor. In particular, as aﬃne formal R-schemes are separated,
the functor rig is deﬁned on all open formal subschemes U of an aﬃne for-
mal R-scheme X. Furthermore, since such a U is necessarily quasi-compact,
the generic ﬁbre Urig is admissible open and, thus, an open subspace of Xrig.
Therefore, to extend the functor rig to the category of all fo rmalR-schemes, we
can repeat the above construction, now interpreting an arbi trary global formal
R-scheme X by glueing open aﬃne parts Ui via arbitrary open subspaces of
these. Hence, we have shown:
Proposition 3. Let R be a complete valuation ring of height 1 with ﬁeld of
fractions K. Then the functor A↦−→A⊗R K on R-algebras A of topologi-
cally ﬁnite type gives rise to a functor X↦−→Xrig from the category of formal
R-schemes, which are locally of topologically ﬁnite type , to the category of rigid
K-spaces.
As indicated above, Xrig is called the generic ﬁbre of the formal R-scheme
X. In an aﬃne situation, say X = Spf A, it coincides pointwise with the set
of all closed points of Spec( A⊗RK), the latter being the generic ﬁbre of the
ordinary scheme Spec A. This way the generic ﬁbre of the formal scheme Spf A
can be exhibited, although, on the level of points, it is not v isible in Spf A. As
we have explained already in Sect. 1.5, the restriction to closed points of the
generic ﬁbre is necessary, as non-closed points of Spec( A⊗RK) do not behave
well with respect to complete localization of A.
In view of Proposition 3, one would like to describe all forma l R-schemes
X whose generic ﬁbre Xrig coincides with a given rigid K-spaceXK. To answer
this question, observe ﬁrst that the functor X ↦−→Xrig factors through the
category of admissible formal R-schemes, since the tensor product with K over
R kills any R-torsion and, hence, the generic ﬁbres for a formal R-scheme X
and its induced admissible formal R-schemeXad coincide. Thus, we are reduced
to the problem of describing all admissible formal R-schemes X admitting a
given rigid K-space Xrig as generic ﬁbre. To solve this problem, the notion of
admissible formal blowing-up, which will be introduced in t he Sect. 2.6, plays
a central role.

2.5 Coherent modules
Now, let us return to the general situation, where R is an adic ring of type (V)
or (N), with a ﬁnitely generated ideal of deﬁnition I. So R is a noetherian adic
ring or an adic valuation ring with a ﬁnitely generated ideal of deﬁnition.
Let A be an R-algebra of topologically ﬁnite type and X = Spf A the
associated formal R-scheme. There is a functor M ↦−→M∆ which associates
to any A-module M anOX-module M∆ as follows: for any basic open subset
D(f )⊂X, given by some f∈A, set
M∆ (D(f )) = lim←−n∈N
M⊗AAn⌈⌊f −1⌉⌋,
where, as usual, An = A/In+1A. As lim←−is left-exact, we get a sheaf which is
extended to all open subsets of X by the usual procedure. In fact we may say
that M∆ is the inverse limit of the modules ˜Mn where the latter is the module
induced on Xn = SpecAn from the An-module Mn =M⊗AAn. If M is a ﬁnite
A-module, the sheaf M∆ can be described in more convenient terms:
Proposition 1. Let X = Spf A be a formal R-scheme of topologically ﬁnite
type. Then, for any ﬁnite A-module M, the sheaf M∆ coincides on basic open
subsets Df⊂X, f∈A, with the functor
Df↦−→M⊗AA⟨f −1⟩.
Proof. As A⟨f −1⟩is anR-algebra of topologically ﬁnite type, by 2.1/9 or 2.3/13,
we know from 2.3/8 that M⊗A A⟨f −1⟩, which is a ﬁnite A⟨f −1⟩-module, is
I-adically complete and separated. By the deﬁnition of M∆ (Df ), we may view
it as the I-adic completion of M⊗AA⌈⌊f −1⌉⌋. However, since the latter is dense
in M⊗AA⟨f −1⟩, we are done. □
Corollary 2. Let X = SpfA be a formal R-scheme of topologically ﬁnite type .
(i) The functor M ↦−→M∆ from the category of ﬁnite A-modules to the
category of OX-modules is fully faithful and exact .
(ii) Assume that X is of topologically ﬁnite presentation and , hence by 2.3/6,
that A is coherent . Then, on the category of coherent A-modules, the functor
M ↦−→M∆ commutes with the formation of kernels , images, cokernels and
tensor products. Furthermore, a sequence of coherent A-modules
0−→M ′−→M−→M ′′−→ 0
is exact if and only if the associated sequence of OX-modules
0−→M ′∆ −→M∆ −→M ′′∆ −→ 0
is exact .

Proof. We use the same argument as the one given in 1.14/1. First, it is clear
that the canonical map
HomA(M,M ′)−→ HomOX (M∆ ,M ′∆ )
is bijective, since any OX-morphism M∆ −→M ′∆ is uniquely determined by
its inherent A-morphism between M =M∆ (X) and M ′ =M ′∆ . Next, if
0−→M ′−→M−→M ′′−→ 0
is an exact sequence of ﬁnite A-modules, then, for any f∈ A, the associated
sequence of A⟨f −1⟩-modules
0−→M ′⊗AA⟨f −1⟩−→M⊗AA⟨f −1⟩−→M ′′⊗AA⟨f −1⟩−→0
is exact, since A⟨f −1⟩is ﬂat over A by 2.3/12. Thus, the sequence
0−→M ′∆ −→M∆ −→M ′′∆ −→ 0
is exact, showing that the functor M↦−→M∆ is exact.
Now, let us consider the situation of (ii) and assume that X is of topolog-

for any ﬁnite A-module. If M −→N is a morphism of coherent A-modules,
we know that its kernel, image, and cokernel are coherent aga in. Thus, we see
from assertion (i) that the functor M↦−→M∆ commutes with the formation
of these modules. Furthermore, it follows from Proposition 1, that it commutes
with tensor products.

Remark 4. Let X be a formal R-scheme, which is locally of topologically ﬁnite
presentation, and let F be anOX-module. Then the following are equivalent :
(i)F is coherent,
(ii)F is of ﬁnite presentation .
(iii) There is an open aﬃne covering (Xi)i∈J of X such that F|Xi is associ-
ated to a ﬁnite OXi(Xi)-module for all i∈J.
Proof. That (i) implies (ii) is immediately clear from the deﬁniti ons. Next,
assume thatF is of ﬁnite presentation as in (ii). Then, in order to derive ( iii),
it is only necessary to consider the case where X is aﬃne, say X = Spf A with

The latter is a ﬁnite A-module so that (ii) implies (iii).
Finally, letF satisfy condition (iii). To show that F is coherent, we may
assume, similarly as before, that X is aﬃne, say X = SpfA withA of topolog-

associated to an A-linear map As−→M. Since A is coherent by 2.3/6, the
kernel of this map is, in particular, of ﬁnite type, and the sa me is true for its
associatedOX-module. As the latter coincides with ker ϕ, we are done. □

2.6 Admissible formal blowing-up
To handle admissible formal blowing-up, we need an auxiliar y ﬂatness result
which is due to O. Gabber. It extends certain results on the ﬂa tness of adic
completions, as contained in Bourbaki, Alg. Comm., Chap. II I, 5.4, to the non-
noetherian sitations we need.
Lemma 1. Let M be a module over some ring A, and let π∈A be an element ,
which is not a zero-divisor in A. Then the following are equivalent :
(i) M is ﬂat over A.
(ii) The torsion (π-tor)M of π in M is trivial , M/πM is ﬂat over A/πA,
and M⊗AA⌈⌊π−1⌉⌋is ﬂat over A⌈⌊π−1⌉⌋.
Proof. Assume ﬁrst that M is ﬂat over A. Then the multiplication by π is
injective on M, since it is injective on A. Furthermore, the ﬂatness assertions
in (ii) for M/πM and M⊗AA⌈⌊π−1⌉⌋follow by base change.
Conversely, assume condition (ii). Using several steps, we will show that
TorA

for q >0, we conclude using step (d) that Tor A

Finally, that condition (ii) of the lemma implies the ﬂatnes s of M over A
follows from steps (d) and (e) if we consider the long Tor-seq uence associated
to the short exact sequence
0−→ (π-tor)N−→N−→N/(π-tor)N−→ 0.
□
Lemma 2 (Gabber). As in Sect . 2.3, let R be an adic ring of type (V) or (N).
Furthermore, let A be an R-algebra of toplogically ﬁnite type and C anA-algebra
of ﬁnite type . Then the I-adic completion ˆC of C is ﬂat over C.
Proof. IfR is of type (N), then A and, hence,C are noetherian, and the assertion
of the lemma is well-known; see Bourbaki, Alg. Comm. Chap. II I, 5.4, Cor. of
Prop. 3. Therefore, we can assume that R is an adic valuation ring of type (V).
Let I = (π) be an ideal of deﬁnition of R.
We start with the special case, where C = A⌈⌊ζ⌉⌋, with a ﬁnite system of
variablesζ = (ζ1,...,ζ r). The the π-adic completion ˆC ofC =A⌈⌊ζ⌉⌋equals the
algebraA⟨ζ⟩of restricted power series in ζ with coeﬃcients in A. Furthermore,
we assume that ˆC does not admit π-torsion. Then, by Lemma 1, we have only to
show that ˆC⊗RR⌈⌊π−1⌉⌋is ﬂat over C⊗RR⌈⌊π−1⌉⌋. Observing that K =R⌈⌊π−1⌉⌋
is a ﬁeld, we may interpret K as the ﬁeld of fractions of the valuation ring which
is obtained by localizing R at its minimal non-zero prime ideal rad( I) = rad(π).
=====PAGE 147=====
2.6 Admissible formal blowing-up 145
Thus, K is the ﬁeld of fractions of a valuation ring of height 1. Conse quently,
we may view AK = A⊗R K as an aﬃnoid K-algebra, and we can use the
identiﬁcations
C⊗RK =AK⌈⌊ζ⌉⌋, ˆC⊗RK =AK⟨ζ⟩,
where AK⟨ζ⟩is the K-algebra of strictly convergent power series over AK in
the sense of classical rigid geometry. In order to show that AK⟨ζ⟩is ﬂat over
AK⌈⌊ζ⌉⌋, it is enough to show that, for any maximal ideal m⊂ AK⟨ζ⟩and its
restriction n to AK⌈⌊ζ⌉⌋, the canonical morphism AK⌈⌊ζ⌉⌋n−→AK⟨ζ⟩m is ﬂat.
Since we are dealing with noetherian rings, see 1.4/2, we may apply the above
mentioned result of Bourbaki and thereby are reduced to show ing that the pre-
ceding map induces an isomorphism between the n-adic completion of AK⌈⌊ζ⌉⌋n
and the m-adic completion of AK⟨ζ⟩m. Thus, it is enough to show that the in-
clusion of AK⌈⌊ζ⌉⌋into AK⟨ζ⟩induces isomorphisms AK⌈⌊ζ⌉⌋/nn ∼−→AK⟨ζ⟩/mn
for n∈ N.
To do this, we proceed similarly as in the proof of 1.6/10. For any maximal
ideal m⊂ AK⟨ζ⟩, we know from 1.4/3 that the quotient AK⟨ζ⟩/m is of ﬁnite
vector space dimension over K. Then, being a subspace of AK⟨ζ⟩/m, the same
is true for AK⌈⌊ζ⌉⌋/n, and it follows that the latter is a ﬁeld. Therefore, n is a
maximal ideal in AK⌈⌊ζ⌉⌋. The same argument shows that n∩AK is a maximal
ideal in AK. From this we can conclude that dim KAK⌈⌊ζ⌉⌋/nn <∞ for all n.
Indeed, the restriction nn∩AK has radical n∩AK inAK, and the latter implies
dimKAK/(nn∩AK) <∞, again by 1.4/3. Hence, AK⌈⌊ζ⌉⌋/nn is a K-algebra
of ﬁnite type, which is local, and it follows from Noether nor malization, that
dimKAK⌈⌊ζ⌉⌋/nn <∞.
Now look at the following commutative diagram
AK⌈⌊ζ⌉⌋

AK⌈⌊ζ⌉⌋/nn
