where the square consists of canonical maps and where the map α still has to
be explained. Fixing a residue norm on the aﬃnoid K-algebra AK, we con-
sider on AK⌈⌊ζ⌉⌋and AK⟨ζ⟩the associated Gauß norms, as well as on the quo-
tients AK⌈⌊ζ⌉⌋/nn and AK⟨ζ⟩/nnAK⟨ζ⟩the corresponding residue norms. Then
all maps of the square are continuous, and AK⌈⌊ζ⌉⌋/nn is complete, since it is
of ﬁnite vector space dimension over K and K is complete. Thus, we can ex-
tend the projection p : AK⌈⌊ζ⌉⌋−→AK⌈⌊ζ⌉⌋/nn to a continuous homomorphism
α : AK⟨ζ⟩−→AK⌈⌊ζ⌉⌋/nn such that the upper triangle of the diagram is commu-
tative. By a density argument, the lower triangle will be com mutative, too. But
then the surjectivity of p′ implies the surjectivity of σ. Furthermore, since ker α
must contain the ideal generated by nn, it follows that σ is injective and, hence,
bijective. In particular, we see that AK⟨ζ⟩/nAK⟨ζ⟩is a ﬁeld, since the same is
true for AK⌈⌊ζ⌉⌋/n, and it follows that m = nAK⟨ζ⟩, as well as mn = nnAK⟨ζ⟩for
=====PAGE 148=====
146 2. Formal Geometry
all n∈ N. Thus, σ : AK⌈⌊ζ⌉⌋/nn ∼−→AK⟨ζ⟩/mn is an isomorphism as required,
settling the assertion of Gabber’s Lemma in the special case whereC =A⌈⌊ζ⌉⌋for
some R-algebra of topologically ﬁnite type A, which does not admit π-torsion.
In the general case we choose an epimorphism A′ −→A where A′ is an
R-algebra of topologically ﬁnite type without π-torsion. For example, A′ could
be an R-algebra of restricted power series over R. The epimorphism can be
extended to an epimorphism of type γ : A′⌈⌊ζ⌉⌋−→C, since C is of ﬁnite type
over A. Then, by the above special case, A′⟨ζ⟩is ﬂat over A′⌈⌊ζ⌉⌋, and we see
by base change that A′⟨ζ⟩⊗A′[ζ] C is ﬂat over C. It remains to exhibit the
latter tensor product as the π-adic completion of C. To do this, let a = kerγ
so that C = A′⌈⌊ζ⌉⌋/a and, hence A′⟨ζ⟩⊗A′[ζ]C = A′⟨ζ⟩/aA′⟨ζ⟩. Now look at
the canonical map ϕ : A′⌈⌊ζ⌉⌋/a−→A′⟨ζ⟩/aA′⟨ζ⟩. Tensoring it with R/(πn) over
R yields an isomorphism ϕ⊗R R/(πn), for any n. Since A′⟨ζ⟩/aA′⟨ζ⟩is an
R-algebra of topologically ﬁnite type, it is π-adically complete and separated
by 2.3/8. It follows that A′⟨ζ⟩/aA′⟨ζ⟩is the π-adic completion of C =A′⌈⌊ζ⌉⌋/a
and we are done. □

called open, if locally on X, it contains powers of type InOX.

Deﬁnition 12. A topological spaceX is called quasi-paracompact if there exists
an open covering X = ⋃
i∈JXi such that :
(i) Xi is quasi-compact for all i∈J.
(ii) The covering is of ﬁnite type; i. e., for any index i∈J, the intersection
Xi∩Xj is non-empty for at most ﬁnitely many indices j∈J.
Proposition 13. Let X be an admissible formal R-scheme which is quasi-

coherent open ideal A⊂O X. Furthermore, we can construct A in such a way
thatA|V coincides with OX|V for any formal open subscheme V ⊂X disjoint
from U.
In particular , any admissible formal blowing-up on U admits an extension
on X.

2.7 Rig-points in the classical rigid setting
In this section, we want to deal with admissible formal R-schemes in the classical
rigid case, where R is a complete valuation ring of height 1 with ﬁeld of fraction s
K. We denote by |·|: K−→ R≥0 a corresponding absolute value. Then, as in
Sect. 2.4, we can consider the functor
rig : (admissible formal R-schemes)−→ (rigid K-spaces),
which is constructed by associating to an aﬃne admissible fo rmal R-scheme
SpfA the aﬃnoid K-space SpA⊗RK. Any point x∈ SpA⊗RK is given by a
maximal ideal in A⊗RK and, since A⊗RK is a localization of A, is induced
from a well-deﬁned prime ideal p⊂A. Of course, p cannot be an open ideal in
A. However, we will see that there is a unique maximal ideal m⊂ A which is
open and contains p. Thereby we get a specialization map from the points of
SpA⊗RK to the (closed) points of Spf A. To describe this map in convenient
terms, we introduce the notion of rig-points.
Deﬁnition 1. Let X be an admissible formal R-scheme. A closed (resp. lo-
cally closed) rig-point of X is a morphism u : T −→X of admissible formal
R-schemes such that
(i) u is a closed (resp. locally closed ) immersion, and
(ii) T is aﬃne , T = Spf B, with B a local integral domain of dimension 1.
The ﬁeld of fractions of B is called the residue ﬁeld of u.
=====PAGE 158=====
156 2. Formal Geometry
Similarly as in the scheme case, a morphism of admissible for malR-schemes
ϕ : Y −→ X is called a closed immersion if there is an aﬃne open cover
(Xi)i∈J of X such that ( ϕ−1(Xi))i∈J deﬁnes an open aﬃne cover of Y and
the induced morphisms ϕ−1(Xi)−→Xi, i∈ J, correspond to epimorphisms
ϕ#
i :OX(Xi)−→OY (ϕ−1(Xi)). Note that then the kernel ker ϕ#
i is saturated
in OX(Xi) in the sense of Lemma 2.3/7 and, hence, is ﬁnitely generated .
In particular, ϕ∗(OY ) is a coherent OX-module via the canonical morphism
ϕ# :OX −→ϕ∗OY , and the kernel I = ker ϕ# is a coherent ideal in OX. It
follows for any aﬃne open formal subscheme U ⊂ X that the inverse image
ϕ−1(U) is aﬃne open in Y . More generally, a morphism of admissible formal
R-schemesϕ : Y−→X is called a locally closed immersion if it factors through
a closed immersion Y−→U⊂X, where U is an open formal subscheme of X.
However, we will see in Lemma 3 below that, in the situation of Deﬁnition 1,
any locally closed rig-point of X is already closed.
First we want to check, which type of rings B can occur within the context
of the above deﬁnition. As usual, let I⊂R be an ideal of deﬁnition.
Lemma 2. Let T = Spf B be an admissible formal R-scheme, where B is a
local integral domain of dimension 1. Then B is ﬁnite over R, and the integral
closure of B in its ﬁeld of fractions Q(B) is a valuation ring .
Proof. First, let us note that the maximal ideal of B is open, since B isI-adically
separated. In particular, B⊗Rk is a local ring, where k is the residue ﬁeld of R.
Due to the fact that B is of topologically ﬁnite type over R, it follows thatB⊗Rk
is of ﬁnite type over k. Hence, by Noether normalization, it must be module-
ﬁnite over k. Now choose an epimorphism of R-algebrasσ : R⟨ζ1,...,ζ r⟩−→B
such that the residue classes of the elements xi = σ(ζi), i = 1,...,r , form a
k-basis in B⊗Rk. There is an element π∈R, 0 <|π|< 1, such that
σ(ζiζj) = xixj∈
r∑
i=1
Rxi +πB, i,j = 1,...,r.
In addition, we may assume 1 ∈ ∑ r
i=1Rxi +πB. Then it follows by iteration
that
B =
r∑
i=1
Rxi +πB,
and by π-adic approximation, that B = ∑ r
i=1Rxi.
Hence, B is ﬁnite over R, and its ﬁeld of fractions Q(B) is ﬁnite over the
ﬁeld of fractions K =Q(R). Let B⊂Q(B) be the valuation ring corresponding
to the unique extension of the absolute value from K toQ(B). By construction
of the latter, B is integral over R and, being normal, it equals the integral
closure of R in Q(B). Then B must contain B and, hence, equals the integral
closure of B in Q(B). □
As a consequence, we can observe:
=====PAGE 159=====
2.7 Rig-points in the classical rigid setting 157
Lemma 3. Any locally closed rig-point of an admissible formal R-scheme X
is already closed . In particular , there is no need to make a diﬀerence between
closed and locally closed rig-points of X.
Proof. It is enough to look at the case where X is aﬃne. Thus, consider an
aﬃne admissible formal R-scheme X = Spf A, and an open formal subscheme
SpfA⟨f −1⟩⊂X induced by some element f∈A, as well as a closed rig-point
u : Spf B−→A⟨f −1⟩. Then there is a canonical commutative diagram
A
τ - A⟨f −1⟩
B
u#
?-
whereu# is surjective. We have to show that the composition u#◦τ : A−→B
is surjective, too. To do this, write B′ for the image of A in B. Then B′ is an
R-algebra of topologically ﬁnite type and, hence, by 2.3/8, I-adically complete
and separated. Since B is ﬁnite over R by Lemma 2, it is ﬁnite over B′. In
particular, if f is the residue class in B off∈A⟨f −1⟩, we know that its inverse
f −1∈ B is integral over B′. Considering an integral equation of f −1 over B′,
the usual trick shows f −1∈B′, since we know f∈B′. But then, as the I-adic
topology of B restricts to the I-adic topology of B′ (use Lemma 2.3/7, or a
direct argument involving absolute values), B′ is dense in B and, thus, must
coincide with B. It follows that the composition u#◦τ : A−→B is surjective.
□
In the following, we will consider rig-points only up to cano nical isomor-
phism. To be more precise, call two rig-points u : T −→X and u′ : T ′−→X
of an admissible formal R-scheme X equivalent if there is an R-isomorphism
σ : T ∼−→T ′ such that u =u′◦σ. The set of equivalence classes of rig-points of
X will be denoted by rig-pts( X).

The construction of the map rig- ϕ in Proposition 4 shows that the residue
ﬁeld of a rig-point u : T −→X can shrink under this map. The latter is not
possible if ϕ is an admissible formal blowing-up.
Proposition 5. Let ϕ : X ′−→X be an admissible blowing-up of an admissible
formal R-scheme X. Then the associated map
rig-ϕ : rig-pts(X ′)−→ rig-pts(X)
is bijective and respects residue ﬁelds .
Proof. We may assume that X is aﬃne, say X = Spf A. Let a = (f0,...,f r)⊂A
be a coherent open ideal such that ϕ is the formal blowing-up of a on X. In
order to exhibit an inverse of rig- ϕ, consider a rig-point u : Spf B−→X. Then
a becomes invertible over the integral closure B of B in Q(B), since B is a
valuation ring by Lemma 3, and since any ﬁnitely generated id eal of a valuation
ring is principal. Interpreting B as a direct limit of ﬁnite extensions of B and
using the fact that a is ﬁnitely generated, we can ﬁnd a ﬁnite subextension
B′ ⊂ B over B such that the ideal aB′ ⊂ B′ is invertible. Clearly, B′ is a
local ring of dimension 1, just as R and B are. Furthermore, it is I-adically
complete and separated by 2.3/8. Thus, using the universal p roperty of the
formal blowing-up ϕ, the morphism
=====PAGE 161=====
2.7 Rig-points in the classical rigid setting 159
SpfB′−→ SpfB−→X
factors through a unique morphism u′ : Spf B′−→X ′. More precisely, if aB′
is generated by fi and Spf Ai is the open formal subscheme of X ′, where the
invertible sheaf aOX′⊂O X′ is generated by fi, then u′ maps SpfB′ into SpfAi.
ReplacingB′ by the image of the attached map Ai−→B′, we may even assume
that u′ : Spf B′−→X ′ is a closed immersion and, therefore, is a rig-point of
X ′. Thus, associating to any u∈ rig-pts(X) the rig-point u′∈ rig-pts(X ′), as
just constructed, we obtain a map rig-pts( X)−→ rig-pts(X ′) which clearly is
an inverse of rig- ϕ. □
We want to show that the rig-points of an admissible formal R-scheme X
correspond bijectively to the points of the associated rigi d K-space Xrig.
Lemma 6. LetX = SpfA be an aﬃne admissible formal R-scheme. Then there
are canonical bijections between the following sets of poin ts:
(i) Rig-points of SpfA, up to identiﬁcation via natural isomorphism .
(ii) Non-open prime ideals p⊂A with dimA/p = 1.
(iii) Maximal ideals in A⊗RK.
In more detail , the stated bijections can be describes as follows :
(a) Given a point of type (i), i. e. a rig-point u : Spf B−→ SpfA deﬁned by
an epimorphism u# : A−→B, associate to it the prime ideal p = keru#⊂ A
as point of type (ii).
(b) Given a point of type (ii) represented by a prime ideal p⊂A, associate
to it the ideal generated by p in A⊗RK as point of type (iii).
(c) Given a point of type (iii), represented by a maximal ideal m⊂A⊗RK,
let p = m∩A and associate to m the canonical morphism SpfA/p−→ SpfA
as point of type (i).
Proof. First we show that the maps described above are well-deﬁned in the sense
that they produce points of the stated types. Starting with p oints of type (i),
let u : Spf B−→ SpfA be a rig-point of X, and let u# : A−→B be the asso-
ciated epimorphism of R-algebras. Then p = keru# is a prime ideal satisfying
dimA/p = dimB = 1. Furthermore, since A/p containsR as a subring, p can-
not be open. It follows that p is a point of type (ii). Next, consider a prime
ideal p⊂A giving rise to a point of type (ii), and assume that there is a p rime
ideal q⊂ A with p ⊊ q. Then q is a maximal ideal, due to dim A/p = 1. Fur-
thermore, such a maximal ideal must be open in A, since otherwise we would
have πA + q = A for π∈ R, 0 <|π| < 1, thus, implying an equation of type
1−aπ = q for some elements a∈ A and q∈ q. But then, due to the geomet-
ric series, q would be invertible which, however, is impossible. It follo ws that
p·(A⊗RK) is a maximal ideal in A⊗RK and, thus, a point of type (iii).
Finally, consider a point of type (iii); i. e., a maximal idea l m⊂ A⊗RK.
Then K ′ = (A⊗RK)/m is a ﬁeld which is ﬁnite over K by 1.2/11 and, using
2.3/5, the image of A in K ′ is an admissible R-algebra, which we denote by
B. Extending the absolute value of K to K ′, which is possible in a unique
=====PAGE 162=====
160 2. Formal Geometry
way, let R′ ⊂ K ′ be the corresponding valuation ring. Then R′ equals the
integral closure of R in K ′, and we claim that B ⊂ R′. In fact, choose an
epimorphism R⟨ζ⟩−→A, where ζ is a ﬁnite system of variables, and consider
on the aﬃnoid K-algebra A⊗RK the residue norm which is derived from the
induced epimorphism K⟨ζ⟩−→A⊗RK. Fixing some π∈R, 0 <|π|< 1, the
topology of A⊗RK restricts to the π-adic topology of A; the latter is true, since
A, as an admissible R-algebra, does not admit π-torsion and, thus, embedds into
A⊗RK. But then, by continuity, any bounded part of A⊗RK, such as A, must
be mapped into a bounded part of K ′, and it follows that B⊂ R′. Since the
extensions R⊂ B⊂ R′ are integral and R, R′ are local rings of dimension 1,
the same must be true for B. It follows that A−→B gives rise to a rig-point
SpfB−→ SpfA. Furthermore, writing p = m∩A, we see that the quotient A/p
is isomorphic to B.
To show that the above described canonical maps are, indeed, bijections,
note that these maps are all injective by deﬁnition. Further more, going from
points of type (i) to points of type (ii), then of type (iii) an d, ﬁnally, of type (i)
again, we get the identity map on points of type (i). This is en ough to conclude
that all three maps are bijective. □
Using the map from points of type (i) in Lemma 6 to those of type (iii), we
obtain the following statement:
Proposition 7. Let X be an admissible formal R-scheme and let Xrig be the
associated rigid K-space. Then there is a canonical bijection
rig-pts(X) ∼−→Xrig
between sets of points , which is functorial in X and associates to a rig-point
T−→X the image of the corresponding closed immersion Trig−→Xrig.
Choosing an element π ∈ R, 0 < |π| < 1, we may use I = (π) as an
ideal of deﬁnition of R. As usual, we set Aℓ = A/Iℓ+1A for any R-algebra A
and let Xℓ =X⊗RR/Iℓ+1 for any formal R-scheme X. Then the underlying
topological spaces of the schemes Xℓ are canonically identiﬁed via the closed
immersions Xℓ−→Xℓ+1. If k is the residue ﬁeld of R, we may, in terms of
underlying topological spaces, identify each Xℓ even with Xℓ⊗Rℓ k =X⊗Rk.
The latter is a k-scheme of ﬁnite type if X is a formal R-scheme of topologically
ﬁnite type; it will be denote by Xk. Let us call Xk the special ﬁbre of X. Since
each rig-point u : T −→X of some admissible formal R-scheme X induces a
closed immersion uk : Tk−→Xk, we see that each rig-point u of X determines
a closed point of the special ﬁbre Xk. Thus, using Proposition 7, we get for any
admissible formal R-schemeX a canonical specialization map
sp :Xrig−→Xk,
and we claim:
=====PAGE 163=====
2.7 Rig-points in the classical rigid setting 161
Proposition 8. For any admissible formal R-scheme X, the specialization map
sp :Xrig−→Xk is surjective onto the set of closed points of Xk.
Proof. We may assume that X is aﬃne, say X = Spf A, and we ﬁrst look at
the special case where A is an algebra of restricted power series, say A =R⟨ζ⟩
with a ﬁnite set of variables ζ = (ζ1,...,ζ n). Given any closed point x∈Xk, let
m⊂Ak =A⊗Rk be the associated maximal ideal. We set k′ =Ak/m and choose
a ﬁnite ﬁeld extension K ′/K lifting the extensionk′/k. ThenK ′ is endowed with
an absolute value extending the one given on K, and we denote by R′⊂K ′ the
corresponding valuation ring; it equals the integral closu re of R inK ′. In order
to show that x belongs to the image of the specialization map sp : Xrig−→Xk,
it is enough to show that the canonical projection pk : A−→Ak−→k′ can be
lifted to an R-homomorphism p : A−→R′. Then, since R′ is integral over R
and, hence, over p(A), we see that p(A) is a local ring of dimension 1. Therefore
the epimorphism A−→p(A) gives rise to a rig-point of X specializing into the
closed point x∈Xk.
To construct the desired lifting p of pk is easy. Choose a representative
ai∈R′ of pk(ζi) for each i and deﬁne a lifting of pk by
p : R⟨ζ1,...,ζ n⟩−→R′, ζ i↦−→ai, i = 1,...,n.
In the general case, we choose an epimorphism τ : R⟨ζ⟩−→A, where R⟨ζ⟩
is an R-algebra of restricted power series as before. We can extend τ to an epi-
morphismτK : K⟨ζ⟩−→A⊗RK between associated aﬃnoid K-algebras. Then
A may be interpreted as the subring in A⊗RK which consists of all elements
a∈ A⊗RK such that a has residue norm ≤ 1 with respect to the projection
τK; use 1.3/9. Due to Noether normalization 1.2/10, there is a K-morphism
ιK : K⟨η⟩−→K⟨ζ⟩
with a ﬁnite set of variables η = (η1,...,η d) such that the composition
τK◦ιK : K⟨η⟩−→A
is a ﬁnite monomorphism. Since ιK is contractive with respect to the Gauß
norm, ιK restricts to an R-morphism ι : R⟨η⟩ −→R⟨ζ⟩. We claim that the
resulting R-morphism τ◦ι : R⟨η⟩−→A is ﬁnite. This fact is readily checked
by redoing the proof of Noether normalization in 1.2/10, usi ng coeﬃcients in R
instead of K. The important fact is the estimate provided by the Weierstr ass
division formula in 1.2/8: given any ζn-distinguished element g∈K⟨ζ⟩of Gauß
norm 1 and of order s, any element f∈K⟨ζ⟩can uniquely be written as
f =qg +r with q∈K⟨ζ⟩, r ∈K⟨ζ1,...,ζ n−1⟩⌈⌊ζn⌉⌋,
where degζnr<s and|q|,|r|≤| f|.
We want to apply the Going-down Theorem to the ﬁnite monomorp hism
τ◦ι : R⟨η⟩−→A. In order to do this, assume that A is an integral domain.
=====PAGE 164=====
162 2. Formal Geometry
Furthermore, we need to know that R⟨η⟩is a normal ring. The latter follows
from the fact that K⟨η⟩is a normal ring; see 1.2/14. In fact, observe that the
fraction ﬁeld Q ofR⟨η⟩coincides with the one of K⟨η⟩and consider an element
q∈Q which is integral over R⟨η⟩. Then q∈K⟨η⟩by 1.2/14, and if we look at
an integral equation
qs +c1qs−1 +... +cs = 0
with coeﬃcients ci∈R⟨η⟩, it follows that q has necessarily Gauß norm≤ 1 and,
thus, belongs to R⟨η⟩. Therefore R⟨η⟩is a normal ring.
Now, as in the beginning, let m be the maximal ideal in A corresponding
to the given closed point x∈Xk. Set n = m∩R⟨η⟩. Using the characterization
of rig-points in Lemma 6, we know from the above considered sp ecial case that
there is a non-open prime ideal q⊂ n satisfying dimR⟨η⟩/q = 1. Furthermore,
by the Going-down Theorem, there is a prime ideal p⊂ m⊂ A such that
p∩ R⟨η⟩= q. Clearly, p is non-open and satisﬁes dim A/p = 1. Thus, by
Lemma 6, p gives rise to a rig-point of Spf A which specializes into the point
corresponding to the given maximal ideal m⊂A.

2.8 Rigid spaces in terms of formal models
We consider again the classical rigid situation, where R is a complete valuation
ring of height 1 with ﬁeld of fractions K. As usual, let k be the residue ﬁeld of R,
and choose a non-unit π∈R−{ 0} so that the topology of R coincides with the
π-adic one. If X is an admissible formal R-scheme and Xrig its associated rigid
K-space, we call X a formal R-model ofXrig. Given any rigid K-spaceXK, one
may ask if there will always exist a formal R-modelX ofXK, and if yes, in which
way such formal models will diﬀer. Putting some mild ﬁnitene ss conditions,
we will work out satisfying answers to these questions, and w e thereby obtain
=====PAGE 165=====
2.8 Rigid spaces in terms of formal models 163
a characterization of the category of rigid K-spaces (with certain ﬁniteness
conditions) as a localization of an appropriate category of admissible formal
R-schemes.
To begin with, let us explain the process of localization of c ategories.
Deﬁnition 1. Let C be a category and S a class of morphisms in C. Then a
localization of C by S is a category CS together with a functor Q : C−→ CS
such that :
(i) Q(s) is an isomorphism in CS for any s∈S.
(ii) If F : C−→ D is a functor such that F (s) is an isomorphism for any
s∈S, then F admits a unique factorization as follows :
C
Q - CS
D
F
?
G
To be more precise, the commutativity of the diagram, as well as the unique-
ness of G are meant up to natural equivalence of functors. Without any further
assumption, one can show that localizations of categories d o always exist.
Proposition 2. The functor
rig : (F/R)−→ (R/K), rig :X↦−→Xrig,
from the category of admissible formal R-schemes to the category of rigid
K-spaces factors through the localization of (F/R) by admissible formal blowing-
ups.
Proof. We just have to show that the functor rig transforms an admis sible formal

Under certain mild conditions we can strengthen Propositio n 2 to yield
an equivalence of categories. To give a precise statement, r ecall that a formal
R-schemeX is called quasi-paracompact if it admits an open covering of ﬁnite
type by quasi-compact open subschemes Ui⊂X, i∈J; i. e., such that each Ui
is disjoint from almost all other Uj, j∈ J. Similarly, dealing with admissible
coverings of ﬁnite type, the notion of quasi-paracompactness is deﬁned for rigid
K-spaces. Furthermore, see 1.16/2 and 1.16/4 for the charact erization of quasi-
separated rigid K-spaces.
Theorem 3 (Raynaud). Let R be a complete valuation ring of height 1 with
ﬁeld of fractions K. Then the functor rig induces an equivalence between
(i) (FSch/R)S, the category of all quasi-paracompact admissible formal
R-schemes, localized by the class S of admissible formal blowing-ups , and
(ii) (Rig/K), the category of all quasi-separated quasi-paracompact rig id
K-spaces.
The proof will consist in establishing the following steps:
(a) The functor rig transforms admissible formal blowing-ups into isomor-
phisms.
(b) Two morphisms ϕ,ψ : X−→Y of admissible formal R-schemes coincide
if the associated rigid morphisms ϕrig,ψ rig coincide.
(c) Let X,Y be admissible formal R-schemes which are quasi-paracompact ,
and let ϕK : Xrig−→Yrig be a morphism between the associated rigid K-spaces.
Then there exist an admissible formal blowing-up τ ′ : X ′−→X and a morphism
of formal R-schemes ϕ′ : X ′−→Y such that ϕ′
rig =ϕK◦τ ′
rig.
(d) Assume that ϕK : Xrig−→Yrig as in (c) is an isomorphism and that X,Y
are quasi-compact. Then we can choose ϕ′ : X ′−→Y satisfying ϕ′
rig =ϕK◦τ ′
rig
with the additional property that it is an admissible formal blowing-up of Y .
(e) Each rigid K-spaceXK, which is quasi-separated and quasi-paracompact,
admits a quasi-paracompact admissible formal R-scheme X as a formal model ;
i. e., with X satisfying Xrig≃XK.
First note that, in the classical rigid case, an admissible f ormal R-scheme
X is automatically quasi-separated, since its special ﬁbre i s a scheme of locally
ﬁnite type over the residue ﬁeld k of R; see Sect. 2.4. Therefore it is clear that

Lemma 5. Let A be an admissible R-algebra. Consider A as a subring of
the associated aﬃnoid algebra Arig = A⊗R K, and let f1,...,f n ∈ Arig be
elements satisfying |fi|sup≤ 1 for i = 1,...,n . Then A′ = A⌈⌊f1,...,f n⌉⌋is

τ : Spf A′−→ SpfA can be viewed as the formal blowing-up of the coherent open
ideal a = (c,cf 1,...,cf n)⊂A on SpfA.
Proof. We choose an epimorphism R⟨ζ⟩−→A for a ﬁnite system of variables ζ
and consider on Arig the residue norm with respect to the induced epimorphism
K⟨ζ⟩−→Arig. Then A consists of all elements a∈Arig with|a|≤ 1, use 1.4/3,
and we see from 1.4/16 that A′ is integral and, hence, ﬁnite over A, since it is
of ﬁnite type over A. Furthermore, as cA′⊂ A, it is easily seen that A′ is an
R-algebra of topologically ﬁnite type. Then A′ is an admissible R-algebra by
2.3/5, since it does not admit π-torsion
In order to show that τ : Spf A′ −→ SpfA is the formal blowing-up of
the ideal a⊂ A, it is enough to show that τ satisﬁes the universal property
of admissible formal blowing-up. To do this note that the ide al aA′ ⊂ A′ is
generated byc and, hence, is invertible, since c is not a zero divisor in A′⊂Arig.
Furthermore, consider a homomorphism of admissible R-algebrasA−→D such
that the ideal aD⊂ D is invertible. Let us write fi for the image of fi in D
again. If aD is generated by c, then cfi ∈ cD and, hence, fi ∈ D for all i,
sincec is not a zero divisor in D. But then A−→D admits a unique extension
A′−→D. If, on the other hand, aD is generated by cfi for some i, then look

follows from (b) again that α1 and α2 are inverse to each other. But then,
using 2.6/11, namely that in the quasi-compact case the comp osition of two
admissible formal blowing-ups yields an admissible formal blowing-up again,
the assertion (d) follows.
Finally, it remains to settle the existence of formal R-models, as stated in
(e). Thus, consider a quasi-separated and quasi-paracompa ct rigid K-spaceXK
and an admissible covering of ﬁnite type (Xi,K)i∈J ofXK by quasi-compact open
subspaces Xi,K⊂XK. We may even assume that Xi,K is aﬃnoid for all i∈J.
Since XK is quasi-separated, any ﬁnite union of aﬃnoid open subspace s of XK
deﬁnes a quasi-compact open subspace of XK, and we will start by showing
that any such subspace admits a formal R-model. Thus, we may assume that J
is ﬁnite, and we can proceed by induction on the cardinality o fJ. If J consists
of just one element, XK is aﬃnoid, say XK = Sp AK. Fixing an epimorphism
of type α : K⟨ζ1,...,ζ r⟩−→AK, let A = α(R⟨ζ1,...,ζ r⟩). Then X = Spf A
is a formal R-model of XK. Now assume that XK = U1,K∪U2,K with quasi-
compact admissible open subspaces Ui,K⊂ XK which admit formal R-models
Ui for i = 1, 2, and let WK = U1,K∩U2,K. Since XK is quasi-separated, WK
is quasi-compact. Thus, there is a ﬁnite admissible aﬃnoid c overing of WK,
and the latter can be enlarged to yield a ﬁnite admissible aﬃn oid covering
of U1,K. Then, applying Lemma 4, there is an admissible formal blowi ng-up

We still have to consider the case of an admissible aﬃnoid cov ering of ﬁnite
type (Xi,K)i∈J of XK, where J is inﬁnite. To simplify things, we may assume
that XK and, furthermore, all Xi,K are connected. Then J will be countable.
More precisely, we will construct a countable admissible co vering (Un,K)n∈N
of XK, which consists of quasi-compact open subspaces Un,K⊂ XK with the
additional property that
Un,K∩Um,K =∅ for m<n − 1.
Indeed, start with U0,K = Xi0,K for some i0∈ J and deﬁne Un+1,K for n∈ N
inductively as the union of all Xi,K which are not yet contained in the union
Vn,K =U0,K∪... ∪Un,K,
=====PAGE 174=====
172 2. Formal Geometry
but which meet Un,K. As ( Xi,K)i∈J is a covering of ﬁnite type, Un+1 consists

easily seen that ⋃ ∞

with XK. Therefore ( Un,K)n∈N is an admissible covering of XK consisting of
admissible open subsets which are quasi-compact.
To construct a formal R-model X of XK, we proceed by induction on n
via the procedure we have used above in the quasi-compact cas e. Thus, assume
that we have already obtained a formal R-model Vn of the union Vn,K, together
with open immersions Ui֒→Vn fori≤n, which represent the open immersions
Ui,K֒→Vn,K. To obtain a formal R-model of Vn+1,K =Vn,K∪Un+1,K, we start
out from the formal R-model Un of Un,K and a certain formal R-model Un+1
of Un+1,K which exists, since Un+1,K is quasi-compact. Then, in order to glue
Un to Un+1, we need to perform suitable admissible formal blowing-ups on Un
and Un+1 ﬁrst. Since Un+1,K does not meet any Ui,K for i < n, we can glue
Un+1 to Vn after extending the blowing-up on the side of Un to all of Vn. Now,
due to 2.6/13, such an extension exists and can be chosen in su ch a way that
it is an isomorphism over any open V ⊂ Vn disjoint from Un. In particular,
the extension of the blowing-up leaves all Ui with i < n− 1 unchanged. From
this it follows that the R-models Vn of Vn,K “converge” towards a well-deﬁned
R-model X of XK, as n progresses towards inﬁnity. This settles assertion (e)
and thereby also the proof of Theorem 3. □
In Sect. 1.13 we have associated to any K-scheme of locally ﬁnite type X a
rigid K-space Xrig, called the rigid analytiﬁcation of X. We want to show:
Proposition 6. Let X be a separated K-scheme of ﬁnite type . Then the as-
sociated rigid analytiﬁcation Xrig is a separated and quasi-paracompact rigid
K-space and, hence, admits a formal R-model X.
Proof. The explicit construction of rigid analytiﬁcations in Sec t. 1.13 shows that
for any closed immersion f : X−→ Y of K-schemes (of locally ﬁnite type) the
associated rigid analytiﬁcation frig : Xrig−→ Yrig is a closed immersion. Fur-
thermore, it is seen in the same way that rigid analytiﬁcatio n respects cartesian
products (in fact, more generally, ﬁbred products). Theref ore, if X is separated,
its rigid analytiﬁcation will be separated, too.
To show that Xrig is quasi-paracompact, we use the fact that X, as a sepa-
rated scheme of ﬁnite type, admits a so-called Nagata compac tiﬁcation X. This is
a proper K-scheme containing X as an open dense subscheme. Then, by Chow’s
Lemma, there is an epimorphism ω : P−→ X from a projective scheme P onto
X. Now consider the associated morphism of rigid K-spacesωrig : Prig−→ Xrig,
which is surjective, too. As we have seen in Sect 1.13, the rig id analytiﬁcation
Pn,rig

is ﬁnite. Likewise, the same is true for Prig, and it follows from the surjectivity
of ωrig that any admissible aﬃnoid covering of Xrig admits a ﬁnite reﬁnement.

2.9 Relative rigid spaces
So far we have considered formal schemes over adic base rings R of the following
types:
=====PAGE 176=====
174 2. Formal Geometry
(V) R is an adic valuation ring with a ﬁnitely generated ideal of de ﬁnition
(which automatically is principal by 2.1/6).
(N) R is a noetherian adic ring with an ideal of deﬁnition I where R does
not have I-torsion.
Instead of S = Spf R we can just as well work over more global bases. The
following types of formal base schemes S will be of interest:
(V′)S is an admissible formal R-scheme, where R is an adic valuation ring
of type (V) as above. Thus, the topology of OS is generated by the ideal πOS,
where π∈R is a suitable element generating the adic topology of R.
(N′) S is a noetherian formal scheme (of quite general type) such th at the
topology of its structure sheaf OS is generated by a coherent ideal I⊂O S and
such thatOS does not admit I-torsion.
Over base schemesS of this type, it is possible to consider admissible formal
S-schemes, or just formal S-schemes, which are locally of topologically ﬁnite
presentation, and the Theorem of Raynaud 2.8/3 enables us to extend the notion
of rigid spaces to such more general situations:
Deﬁnition 1. Let S be a formal scheme of type (V′) or (N′), as deﬁned above ,
and let (FSch/S) be the category of admissible formal S-schemes. Then the
category (Rig/S) of rigidS-spaces is deﬁned as the localization of (FSch/S) by
admissible formal blowing-ups .
Thus, as object, a rigid S-space is the same as an admissible formal
S-scheme, whereas on the level of morphisms, admissible form al blowing-ups
are viewed as isomorphisms. One may generalise the concept o f rig-points, as
developed in Sect. 2.7, to the formal scheme S and view any rigid S-space X
as a family of classical rigid spaces Xs over the rig-points s∈S.
We want to work out in more detail how the category (Rig /S) of rigid
S-spaces is constructed from (FSch /S), assuming that we restrict ourselves to
formal S-schemes which are quasi-separated and quasi-paracompact. To deﬁne
(Rig/S), take as objects the objects of (FSch /S). Furthermore, for two objects
X,Y of (Rig/S), a morphism X −→ Y is given by an equivalence class of
diagrams in (FSch/S) of type

2.10 The Zariski-Riemann space
In the following, let S be a formal scheme of type (V ′) or (N ′), as intro-
duced in Sect. 2.9, and let X be an admissible formal S-scheme, where we
will always assume that X is quasi-separated and quasi-paracompact. Then
we can consider the family ( XA)A∈B (X) of all admissible formal blowing-ups
XA−→X, parametrized by the set B(X) of coherent open ideals A⊂O X. For
A,B∈ B(X) we write A≤B if the ideal A becomes invertible on XB. Due to
the universal property of admissible blowing-up 2.6/9, the latter is equivalent
to the fact that the blowing-up XB−→X factors through a unique morphism
ΦAB : XB −→XA. Furthermore, given A,B ∈B(X), we have AB ∈B(X),
as well as A≤AB andB≤AB . It is clear that the XA together with the
morphismsΦAB deﬁne a projective system of S-morphisms so that we can look
at the projective limit
⌈⌊X⌉⌋= lim←−
A∈B (X)
XA.
This projective limit is, ﬁrst of all, meant in terms of topol ogical spaces. Fur-
thermore, for eachA∈ B(X), there is a canonical projection πA :⌈⌊X⌉⌋−→XA,
and we can consider
O[X] = lim−→
A∈B (X)
π−1(OXA)
=====PAGE 181=====
2.10 The Zariski-Riemann space 179
as a sheaf of rings on ⌈⌊X⌉⌋. It is not hard to see that the stalks
O[X],x = lim−→
A∈B (X)
OA,πA(x), x ∈⌈⌊X⌉⌋,
being direct limits of local rings, are local again so that ⌈⌊X⌉⌋= (⌈⌊X⌉⌋,O[X]) is a
locally ringed space.
Deﬁnition 1. Let X be an admissible formal S-scheme. Then the associated
locally ringed space ⌈⌊X⌉⌋= lim←−A∈B (X)XA is called the Zariski-Riemann space 1
associated to X.
Without proof, let us mention a few facts on the topology of ⌈⌊X⌉⌋.
Proposition 2. Let X be a formal S-space and ⌈⌊X⌉⌋the associated Zariski-
Riemann space. Then:
(i)⌈⌊X⌉⌋is non-empty if X is non-empty .
(ii)⌈⌊X⌉⌋is a T0-space, but not necessarily Hausdorﬀ .
(iii)⌈⌊X⌉⌋is quasi-compact if X is quasi-compact.
Passing fromX to the associated rigid S-spaceXrig, we see that the Zariski-
Riemann space⌈⌊X⌉⌋, in a certain sense, takes into account all formal S-models
ofXrig, just as Xrig itself does. Thus, one can well imagine that there is a certai n
equivalence betweenXrig and⌈⌊X⌉⌋, although one must be aware of the fact that,
in the classical rigid case, Xrig is a locally ringed space with respect to just a
Grothendieck topology, whereas ⌈⌊X⌉⌋is a locally ringed space with respect to
an ordinary topology. However, this diﬀerence is compensat ed by the fact that
⌈⌊X⌉⌋consists of “much more” points than Xrig.
To make this precise, let us restrict to the classical rigid c ase, where S
consists of an adic valuation ring R of height 1 with ﬁeld of fractions K. As we
have shown in Sect. 2.7, there is a well-deﬁned specializati on map
sp :Xrig−→X,
mapping a point of Xrig to a closed point of X. Since sp is functorial, the map
factors through all formal models XA, asA varies in B(X), thus, giving rise to
a specialization map
sp :Xrig−→⌈⌊X⌉⌋.
Proposition 3. In the classical rigid case , let X be an admissible formal
R-scheme. Then the specialization map sp :Xrig−→⌈⌊X⌉⌋enjoys the following
properties:
(i) sp is injective .
1 Apparently, the terminology of Zariski-Riemann space goes back to Nagata, who consid-
ered for a given function ﬁeld F the projective limit of all projective varieties with funct ion
ﬁeld equal to F .
=====PAGE 182=====
180 2. Formal Geometry
(ii) The image of sp is dense in ⌈⌊X⌉⌋with respect to the constructible topol-
ogy.
There are examples of abelian sheaves F on a rigid K-space XK, where all
stalksFx forx∈XK are trivial, although F is not trivial itself. This is a clear
indication for the fact that, in order to handle general abel ian sheaves, rigid
K-spaces are not equipped with suﬃciently many points. That t he Zariski-
Rieman space associated to a formal model of XK can resolve this problem,
becomes clear from the following fact:
Proposition 4. In the classical rigid case , let XK be a rigid K-space with
a formal R-model X. Then the specialization map sp :XK −→⌈⌊X⌉⌋induces
a natural equivalence between the category of abelian sheav es on XK and the
category of abelian sheaves on ⌈⌊X⌉⌋.
Without giving proofs, let us just give some indications on h ow to pass back
and forth between abelian sheaves on XK and⌈⌊X⌉⌋. For any admissible open
subset UK⊂XK, consider the open subset
sp∗(UK) ={W⊂⌈⌊X⌉⌋; W open and W∩ sp(XK)⊂ sp(UK)}
of the Zariski-Riemann space ⌈⌊X⌉⌋. For example, if UK is quasi-compact and,
thus, is represented by some open formal subscheme U ′⊂ XA for a coherent
open ideal A∈ B(X), then sp ∗(UK) = π−1
A (U ′). Furthermore, one shows for
any admissible open subset UK ⊂ XK that a given union UK = ⋃
i∈JUi,K,
consisting of admissible open subsets Ui,K⊂ UK, is an admissible covering of
UK if and only if sp ∗(UK) = ⋃
i∈J sp∗(Ui,K). Now start with an abelian sheaf
FK on XK and set F (sp∗(UK)) = FK(UK) for any quasi-compact admissible
open subset UK⊂XK. Since the associated sets of type sp ∗(UK) deﬁne a basis
of the topology on ⌈⌊X⌉⌋, we can view F as a sheaf on ⌈⌊X⌉⌋. Conversely, given
any abelian sheaf F on⌈⌊X⌉⌋, we can deﬁne an abelian sheaf FK on XK by
settingFK(UK) = F (sp∗(UK)) for any quasi-compact admissible open subset
UK ⊂ XK. It is not hard to see that the described correspondence betw een
abelian sheaves on Xrig and⌈⌊X⌉⌋is an equivalence of categories.
2.11 Some advanced results on formal models
In the following we want to dicuss certain properties of rigi d spaces and the
possibility of extending them to suitable formal models. De tails can be found
in a sequence of articles entitled “Formal and rigid geometr y”:
(1) S. Bosch, W. L ¨utkebohmert: Rigid spaces, Math. Ann. 295, 291–317
(1993)
(2) S. Bosch, W. L ¨utkebohmert: Flattening techniques, Math. Ann. 295,
403–429 (1993)
=====PAGE 183=====
2.11 Some advanced results on formal models 181
(3) S. Bosch, W. L ¨utkebohmert, M. Raynaud: The relative maximum prin-
ciple, Math. Ann. 302, 1–29 (1995)
(4) S. Bosch, W. L ¨utkebohmert, M. Raynaud: The reduced ﬁbre theorem,
Invent. math. 119, 361–398 (1995)
Let S be a formal base scheme of type (V ′) or (N ′), as in Sect. 2.9, and let
Xrig be a rigid S-space in the sense of 2.9/1. Without explicitly saying so, w e
will always assume such rigid spaces, as well as their formal S-models, to be
quasi-separated and quasi-paracompact.
If (P ) is a property applicable to schemes or formal schemes, we ca n basically
proceed in two ways in order to extend the notion of ( P ) to rigid S-spaces like
Xrig. The ﬁrst possibility is to say that Xrig satisﬁes (P ) if there is a formal
S-model X of Xrig satisfying (P ). For example, on the level of morphisms,
one can proceed like this with open (resp. closed) immersion s. Thus, call a
morphism of rigid S-spacesτrig : Urig−→Xrig an open immersion (resp. a closed
immersion) if τrig admits an open (resp. closed) immersion of admissible forma l
S-schemes τ : U−→X as a formal S-model. That such a deﬁnition coincides
with the usual one in the classical rigid case, follows from 2 .8/4.
Another more direct possibility is to look at the validity of (P ) on the
“complement” of the special ﬁbre of formal S-models X associated to Xrig. To
be more precise, let I ⊂OS be an ideal of deﬁnition. Then, for any formal
S-model X, the scheme X0 = X⊗SOS/I is called the special ﬁbre of X. If
(Ui)i∈J is an aﬃne open covering of X, say Ui = Spf Ai, and if, on Ui, the
coherent open ideal IOX ⊂O X is associated to the ideal ai⊂ Ai, we view
the ordinary scheme Spec Ai−V (ai), locally on Ui, as the complement of the
special ﬁbre, although such a complement is not well-deﬁned globally. Now,
if (P ) is a scheme property, we can say that Xrig satisﬁes (P ) if all schemes
SpecAi−V (ai) satisfy ( P ). Of course, in order that ( P ) deﬁnes a reasonable
property on the associated rigid S-spaceXrig, one has to check that the validity
of (P ) is independent of the chosen covering ( Ui)i∈J of X and invariant under
admissible formal blowing-up. Then, in general, it is a true ly demanding venture,
to ﬁnd out, whether or not a rigid S-space satisfying ( P ) will always admit a
formal S-model satisfying (P ).
As a ﬁrst example which can successfully be handled along the se lines, let
us mention the property ( P ) of being ﬂat, for morphisms of rigid S-spaces or
coherent modules on rigid S-spaces. This notion of ﬂatness is compatible with
the usual one, known for classical rigid spaces. The main res ult on ﬂatness is
the existence of ﬂat formal models, due to Raynaud and Gruson ; see the above
mentioned article (2).
Theorem 1. Let ϕ : X −→ Y be a quasi-compact morphism of admissible
formal S-schemes, and assume that the associated morphism of rigid S-spaces
ϕrig : Xrig−→Yrig is ﬂat. Then there exists a commutative diagram of admissible
formal S-schemes

on X.
Let us mention that X ′ can also be viewed as the strict transform of X
with respect to the admissible formal blowing-up Y ′−→Y . The latter is con-
structed from the ﬁbered product X ′′ =X×Y Y ′ (a formal S-scheme of locally
topologically ﬁnite presentation, but not necessarily admissible) by dividing the
structure sheaf by all torsion with respect to the ideal gene rated by the pull-back
ofA. The existence of ﬂat models has an interesting consequence for classical
rigid spaces.
Corollary 2. In the classical rigid case , let R be an adic valuation ring of
height 1 with ﬁeld of fractions K. Furthermore, let ϕK : XK−→YK be a ﬂat
morphism of quasi-compact and quasi-separated rigid K-spaces. Then its image
ϕK(XK) is admissible open in YK.
Proof. Due to Theorem 1, there exists a ﬂat formal R-model ϕ : X−→Y of
ϕK. Tensoring ϕ with the residue ﬁeld k of R yields a morphism of k-schemes
ϕk : Xk−→Yk, which is ﬂat and of ﬁnite presentation. It is known that the
image of ϕk is a quasi-compact open subscheme Vk⊂Yk; see [EGA IV, 2.4.6].
Now, if V⊂Y is the corresponding open formal subscheme of Y , then, clearly,
ϕ factors through V , and the induced morphism X −→V is faithfully ﬂat.
Finally, a local consideration involving rig-points, as in troduced in Sect. 2.7,
shows that ϕK must map XK onto the admissible open subspace Vrig⊂ XK,
which is associated to V . □
Another property (P ), which can be deﬁned on general rigid S-spaces Xrig
by requiring (P ) to be satisﬁed on the “complement” of the special ﬁbre, is th e
notion of smoothness. Also in this case, one may ask if any smo oth (or even ´ etale)
morphism of rigid S-spaces will admit a smooth (resp. ´ etale) formal S-model.
However, the answer will be negative in general. To give a sim ple example, one
may look at the classical rigid situation, where R is an adic valuation ring of
height 1 with a fraction ﬁeld K, which is algebraically closed. Then, for any
q∈R, 0 <|q|< 1, the annulus XK = SpK⟨ζ,qζ −1⟩is a smooth rigid K-space,
which does not admit a smooth R-model. A canonical R-model of XK is given
by the formal R-scheme X = Spf R⟨ζ,η⟩/(ζη−q), which is not smooth. If
there were a smooth formal R-model X of XK, it would be connected, since
XK is connected. Furthermore, starting out from an aﬃne open co vering of X,
we would get a ﬁnite aﬃnoid covering ( Ui,K)i∈J on XK such that there is a
multiplicative residue norm on each of the aﬃnoid K-algebrasAi =OXK (Ui,K).
=====PAGE 185=====
2.11 Some advanced results on formal models 183
However, over an algebraically closed ﬁeld K, the aﬃnoid subdomains of the
unit ball B1
K are well-known, and one knows that such a covering cannot exi st.
Thus, expecting the existence of smooth formal S-models of smooth rigid
S-spaces would be too much. Stepping back a bit, one may replac e smoothness
by the weaker property ( P ) that the structural morphism Xrig −→Srig has
geometrically reduced ﬁbres. Here is an advanced result on t he existence of
formal S-models with such a property ( P ):2

X/S is ﬂat and Xrig/Srig has reduced geometric ﬁbres, equidimensional of dimen-

Classical valuation theory
In the following, letK be a ﬁeld with a non-Archimedean absolute value denoted
by|·|: K−→ R≥0. Furthermore, let V be a K-vector space. A K-vector space
norm on V is a map ||·||: V −→ R≥0 satisfying the following conditions for
elementsx,y∈V and α∈K:
(i)||x|| = 0⇐⇒x = 0,
(ii)||αx|| =|α||| x||,
(iii)||x +y||≤ max{||x||,||y||}.
When no confusion is possible, we will ususally make no notat ional diﬀerence
between the absolute value|·|onK and the vector space norm ||·||onV , thus
always writing|x| instead of||x|| for elements x∈V . To give an example of a
K-vector space norm, let V be a ﬁnite dimensional K-vector space with a basis
v1,...,v d. Then we deﬁne the so-called maximum norm |·|max onV as follows.
Given an element x∈ V , write it as a linear combination x = ∑ d
i=1αivi with
coeﬃcients αi∈K and set
|x|max = max
i=1...d
|αi|.
One easily checks that |·|max deﬁnes a vector space norm on V . Furthermore, if
K is complete under its absolute value, V is complete under such a maximum
norm.
As usual, any vector space norm on a K-vector space V deﬁnes a topology
on V . Two such norms |·|1 and|·|2 are called equivalent if they induce the
same topology on V . The latter amounts to the fact that there exist constants
c,c ′ > 0 such that |x|1≤ c|x|2≤ c′|x|1 for all x∈ V . It is clear that any two
maximum norms, attached to certain K-bases on a ﬁnite dimensional K-vector
space V , are equivalent. If K is complete, a more general assertion is possible.
Theorem 1. Let V be a ﬁnite dimensional K-vector space, and assume that K
is complete . Then all K-vector space norms on V are equivalent. In particular,
V is complete under such a norm .
Proof. Choose a K-basis v1,...,v d of V and consider the attached maximum
norm|·|max onV . Let|·|be a second K-vector space norm on V . Then there is

Corollary 2. Let L/K be an algebraic ﬁeld extension , and let |·|1,|·|2 be two
absolute values on L which restrict to the given absolute value |·|on K. Assume
that K is complete with respect to |·|. Then|·|1 and|·|2 coincide on L.
Proof. Since L is a union of ﬁnite subextensions of L/K, we may assume that

|·|1 and|·|2, these norms are equivalent by Theorem 1. Thus, there are con stants

We have just seen that for any algebraic ﬁeld extension L/K, there is at
most one way to extend the given absolute value |·|from K to L, provided K
is complete with respect to |·|. We want to show now that such an extension
will always exist.
Theorem 3. Let L/K be an algebraic extension of ﬁelds , and assume that K
is complete with respect to the given absolute value |·|. Then there is a unique
way to extend |·|to an absolute value |·|′ of L. In fact ,

for some elements pj+1∈R⌈⌊X⌉⌋m−1 and qj+1∈R⌈⌊X⌉⌋n−1 satisfying

But then we have

The problem of extending a non-Archimedean absolute value |·|from a
ﬁeld K to an algebraic extension L has been settled in Theorem 3 for the case
where K is complete. If K is not complete with respect to |·|, we can pass
to its completion ˆK which can be constructed as follows. Consider the ring
K N of all inﬁnite sequences in K, addition and multiplication being deﬁned
componentwise. Then the Cauchy sequences deﬁne a subring C(K) of K N and
the zero sequences an ideal Z(K)⊂ C(K). It is easy to see that the quotient
ˆK = C(K)/Z(K) is a ﬁeld and that the canonical map K−→ ˆK sending an
elementα to the residue class of the constant sequence α,α,... is a homomor-
phism of ﬁelds. In particular, we can view K as a subﬁeld of ˆK. We can even
deﬁne an absolute value |·|′ on ˆK extending the one given on K. Namely, for
α∈ ˆK, choose a representing Cauchy sequence ( αi) in K. Then the sequence
(|αi|) is a zero sequence or, due to the non-Archimedean triangle i nequality, it
becomes constant at a certain index i0. Therefore the limit c = lim i− →∞|αi|
exists and is well-deﬁned, and we can set |α|′ = c. One can show that ˆK is
complete with respect to |·|′ and that it contains K as a dense subﬁeld.
Now if L/K is an algebraic ﬁeld extension, we can consider the completi on
ˆK ofK and its algebraic closure ˆK alg. Extending the absolute value of K to ˆK,
as just described, and prolonging it to ˆK alg with the help of Theorem 3, we get
a canonical non-Archimedean absolute value on ˆK alg, which may be denoted by
|·|again. Then we can choose a K-morphismτ : L−→ ˆK alg and pull back the
=====PAGE 193=====
Classical valuation theory 191
absolute value from ˆK alg toL viaτ . Thereby we obtain an absolute value on L
extending the one given on K. However, the latter will not be unique in general,
which corresponds to the fact that the K-morphism τ : L−→ ˆK alg may not be
unique.
Taking the algebraic closure of a complete ﬁeld, we may loose completeness.
In particular, the ﬁeld ˆK alg may not be complete again. However, if we start with
an algebraically closed ﬁeld, its completion will remain al gebraically closed. This
way it is possible to construct extension ﬁelds which are alg ebraically closed and
complete at the same time.
Krasner’s Lemma 6. Let K an algebraically closed ﬁeld with a non-Archime-
dean absolute value |·|. Then its completion ˆK is algebraically closed .
Proof. Consider an algebraic closure L of ˆK, and extend the absolute value
of ˆK to L, using the assertion of Theorem 3. Let f = ∑ n
i=0ciXi be a monic
polynomial in ˆK⌈⌊X⌉⌋. Then f admits a zero α∈ L, and it is enough to show
that α can be approximated by elements in K. To verify this, choose ε > 0
and approximate the coeﬃcients ci by elements di∈K in such a way that the
polynomial g = ∑ n
i=0diXi∈ K⌈⌊X⌉⌋satisﬁes|g(α)|≤ εn. Assuming dn = 1,
write g = ∏ n
i=1(X−βi) with zeros βi∈ K. Then |g(α)| = ∏ n
i=1|α−βi|≤ εn
implies that there is an index i such that|α−βi|≤ ε. Consequently, α can be
approximated by elements in K. □
The argument used in the proof is referred to as the principle of continuity
of roots.
